The Video Process

One brief to the editor's hand-off, on the board at 127.0.0.1:8895/prompt under Video ad. The same steps for every ad, every brand. 28 Oct 2026.

The board only presses.Every job runs on its own, so closing the board never kills paid work.
Nothing spends unpressed.Each press is the OK for that one thing.
No brand in the machine.Brand words come from the brand's folder, the ad's from its run.
Where we stop (28 Oct)Every brief, one at a time, up to stage 4: the scene pictures you approve. Then the hand-off: the brief, the approved pictures and each scene's clip prompt. Clips, the edit, B-roll and effects are the video editor's. Stages 5 to 7 stay below as the standard his work is checked against.

A video is one picture, given life.

Every scene starts as a still. The clip maker gets that picture plus three things, and hands back the picture moving. New ad or variation, it is the same steps.

What goes in

The start picture, approved first
The pictureFixes who she is, what she wears, the room and the light.
The movementA real person's take. It gives the head, face and hands natural timing. Nothing else is copied from it.
The voiceHer line, already recorded. The speed and pauses are set here.
The directionEach action tied to its words: "as she says 45, the finger touches the cheek".

What comes out

The original Grandma Yai opening. This one clip covers her first three scenes, made at 480p for about $1.51. If the picture is right and the direction is timed, the clip comes out right. Most faults trace back to one of the four inputs.

Real video beside the picture

Nearly every clip now gets real footage, used for movement only. It goes in grey, soft and silent, so no real person or product carries over.

Talking clipA real person talking to camera, always.
Product useApplying, squeezing, rinsing, shaving, holding it up, when the shot does that.
ActionPointing at skin, mirror, pouring, walking, when the shot does that.
No matchNo video. Wrong footage pulls the shot off the direction.

25 pieces so far: 7 talking, 11 product use, 7 actions, all real people from our own creator footage plus one TikTok. Two at most per clip.

Your rules from today, in every scene

  • L-50Every swipe scene read down to its smallest pieces.
  • L-51Phone held or propped is measured, and the hand drift goes in.
  • L-52Effects are drawn on our own scene's object.
  • L-53Props make sense: the lid comes off first, ingredients go somewhere you can see.
  • L-54The action lands on the right object and it stays that object.
  • L-55A talking take must have the voice. A silent one fails.
  • L-56One take per press. You keep it or press again.
  • L-57No camera moves on a kept clip. They wait for the edit.
  • L-58No blurry effect reaches the edit.
  • L-59The same object from scene to scene.
  • L-60Anything picked up later is already in reach in the picture.
  • L-61Body check on every picture and every take: two arms, two hands, normal size.
  • L-62The movement bar: hands keep working, eyes go to the hands and back, the head moves.
  • L-66Once script, voice and clips are approved, the edit just cuts. Nothing is remade there. Each stage has one job.
  • L-67A take that skips three or more words of its line fails and is made again.

What made the final cut

Your words on cut 4: "fire. great this is it. establish this process". Now every ad's cut works this way.

  • L-66The edit only cuts. Script, voice and clips are approved, so nothing is remade there.
  • L-70A phrase a take never said is cut only when you accept it.
  • L-71A scene you take out stays out, with your reason.
  • L-72The captions look like the swipe's own captions.
  • L-73The opening uses the same caption style as every other line, unless the swipe has its own headline look.
  • L-74A short opening reads as one line, never one word per line.
  • L-75An effect that didn't come out well stays out of the cut. The editor still gets it.

Smoothed out today

  • ✓The swipe breakdown now runs by itself with Get the swipe frame ready. No scene skips it.
  • ✓Change it runs as its own job, like every other button.
  • ✓Camera moves and sharpening: the editor's, after the hand-off. As you said.
  • ✓Rule numbers fixed: the edit rule is L-66, the skipped-words check L-67.
  • ✓Board wording neutral: "At their own age", search hints with no brand's characters.

Still yours to call

  1. Loading a brief has no button.A session starts the voice and scenes; the brief shows on the board after. Want a Load button?
  2. Effects didn't come out well.Out of this ad's cut. Fix them on the board next brief, or the editor makes them?
  3. Skin-care logic lives in the code."After the routine, the spots are gone" and "scrub, serum, cream means the product". Make them read the product's own kit?

The writing prompts, brand-free (28 Oct)

Every writing prompt now works for any brand: no brand, product, ingredient, past ad or person in it, and no he or she for the viewer or the creator. No rule changed. Open one to see each line before and after, then the whole prompt.

## THE SWIPE'S FORMAT — declared at teardown, never re-guessed (v2)

The teardown already decided what kind of swipe this is. Write inside it:

{declared_layers}

Never re-classify the swipe, never call it a different format, never switch
who carries it. If something below seems to ask you to decide the format,
the answer is the one above.

## THE LANE — paid or organic, and it changes only the ask (2026-10-21)

{ask_lane}

Organic and paid are the same video. The owner, 2026-10-20: *"Our organic and
paid content should be indistinguishable. The only core difference is really
just the ask and the way that we gradualize that sales pitch."* So the lane
above changes exactly two things, and nothing else:

1. **The last ask.** Paid: tap the button below and buy. Organic: comment the
   word (the one word the lane line names), or follow. The last thing said and
   the last thing on screen is that ask, and only that ask.
2. **How gradually the pitch arrives.** Organic lets the story carry longer
   before the product's first claim; paid may bring it sooner. The ORDER of the
   argument never changes.

The job, the product, where the product enters and every claim are the same
on both lanes; only the close differs (below — V23). Claims stay as bold as the brand files allow on both
(the owner, 2026-10-20: "don't worry about over claiming at this point it's
content"). An organic piece is never a piece without the product, and a paid
piece is never a different video with a button added.

**On the organic lane the close is the turn and the ask — no offer stack** (the owner, 2026-10-21: "i'm going for
real binge watch content man, nothing sterile at all"). After the remedy, the product's turn and the lane's ask,
word for word, and nothing else: no bundle or multi-buy, no dosing or usage schedule, no guarantee text, no
label-style statistics ("85 in 100…"). The product, the mechanism and the claims stay — as bold as the brand files
allow. The paid lane keeps the offer.

**On the organic lane the brief carries no offer** (V23): the end card, if the script's close calls for one, is the
real product photo and the lane's ask — no offer lines, no guarantee, no figure. The paid lane keeps the offer.

Here are the transformed scripts, one per source format: {stage4_outputs}
Here is the hook set, with every variation and its scroll stopper: {hook_set}
Here is where the ad starts — the line the owner chose, word for word, or `none`: {opening_at}
When it names a line, the piece STARTS there: every scene of the injection before that line is left out of the brief — no scene, no voice line, no picture for it — and the openings are the hook set's, built on that line. When it says `none`, nothing is cut.
Here is the record of the source video, shot by shot: {teardown_record}
Here is the FRAME MAP of the source video — every cut found in the pixels, and
every shot (K01, K02 …) watched moment by moment: who is in frame, the first and
last frame as stills, the camera, every visible action with its time and which
frame edge each arm enters from, the words on screen, the sound, the edit:
{frame_map}

**THE FRAME MAP IS THE SHOT LIST YOU COPY.** The record says what each stretch
of the source IS; the frame map says what HAPPENS in it, shot by shot. Write
from it:

- **Every shot is carried.** Each K-shot lands in a scene of ours, in order. A
  shot the record never listed — a short cutaway to the speaker's own handheld
  reaction, an insert of hands holding something with words on it, a graphic —
  is still the source's, and it is still ours: give it its scene (an insert is
  its own scene, `to_camera` false, with its own frames). If the source has it,
  we have it.
- **Each scene names the shots it copies.** Begin its `source` with the K-ids,
  then the shortened description: `"K02–K04: the hand points to and touches
  their face"`. A scene the source never had says `"none — script-backed"`.
- **Body geometry is the shot's.** A first frame's `composition` and
  `viewpoint` put every person and every arm where the shot's first frame puts
  them — the same edge, the same side, the same share of the frame — with our
  people from the cast.
- **Movement is the shot's.** The beats follow the shot's moments in order and
  at the same pace — the same hand, the same direction, where it lands — with
  our words over them.
- **Every word on screen is carried.** A label, a title or a card in the
  source — anything on screen that is not the captions of what is being said —
  is on ours at the same moment, filled from our side the way a hook is filled:
  the source's format, our words.

and its emphasis words) and the VIDEO SPICE section (the level, the seven

and at which awareness: {ad_frameworks}
This run's production route: {production_route}
Here is the swipe file — the source video this brief remakes: {source_url}
Here is VIDEO SPICE — the seven layers that make a video feel real, and its levels; layer 7, camera and cutting, is written into this brief shot by shot: {video_spice}
Here are the WRITING-stage rows of the realism index — the small human behaviours this brief decides the place of, by id; this format's rows first: {realism_index}

*v29 (2026-10-21): THE BRIEF IS A SHOOTING SCRIPT. The owner's rulings of
2026-10-20, in one pass: "everything about the work needs to scream real";
"angle changes and camera movement changes mid scene and into clean
transitions is what keeps the eye engaged … just adapted to mobile"; "just
make it a clean 3 versions of each element"; "when we describe movement like
whisking, that should've been in the scene too"; "the nose changed sizes";
"Our organic and paid content should be indistinguishable." So: (1) a scene
stays ONE paragraph of meaning, and it is shot as one or more SHOTS — each
shot names its start picture, the actor's face, emotion and gestures, the
angle, the camera move, how it hands off to the next shot and what it ends on
(`shots`, below); an angle change is a new shot, and a clip still never cuts;
(2) the voice is no longer one continuous read: each LINE is its own read,
with its ElevenLabs v3 delivery tag and the words that take the weight
(`lines` on each shot) — the scene's `voice` paragraph is still those lines
joined, byte for byte; `voice_settings` is gone; (3) no length is chosen
anywhere: a shot is as long as its locked read; (4) the lane (paid · organic)
and its ask are on the piece, and the end card's ask is the lane's; (5) the
piece names `frames` (`swipe-edit` when the scenes are edits of the swipe's
own frames), `room_tone` (only when the swipe has room sound) and the
`make_list` — every start picture production makes, three of each, and no
others; (6) every prop is defined ONCE, at its real size, in `props`; (7) every
step the words say is shown, nothing enters the frame from nowhere, and the
payoff gets its own shot; (8) no packshot rides into any generation job: where
the product is in frame, the real product photo is laid on in the edit;
(9) video spice and the writing rows of the realism index are inputs, and the
spice sheet's VIDEO SPICE section is written into the shots. Every variable
and every other rule of v28 survives.*

*v19 (2026-09-30, the owner: "no more frames, only the brief and swipe file link"):
the brief opens with the swipe file link so production and the editor can
watch the source itself. Print exactly one line, as the very first line of
your output, before anything else: `**Swipe file:** {source_url}` — and leave
it out if the swipe file reads NONE. Nothing else changes; everything in v18
stands.*

*v10 (2026-09-19): THE VOICE IS THE SPOKEN SCRIPT — the owner's ruling, "humans
don't use em dashes when speaking." Measured the same day: a paragraph
written with em-dashes was read with 1.3–1.7 s of silence at every dash; a
7 s paragraph took 11 s. One new variable, `{spoken}`. Each scene's `voice`
IS the spice sheet's SPOKEN paragraph for that section, copied as written —
never re-flattened into prose, never a dash put back. The block's rule and
the gate: no em-dash, no semicolon, no parenthesis in `voice`; the ellipsis is KEPT — it is the mid-thought breath (the owner, 2026-09-19), and a `voice` is one thought per breath, never a thought chopped into short statements.
(Since v29 a scene carries no `voice_settings`: each line of the spice sheet's LINES block is its own read, directed by its v3 tag.)
Every variable and every rule of v9 survives.*

*v13 (2026-09-23): B-ROLL AND CARDS ARE MADE AT PRODUCTION. The owner's ruling:
"we need to ensure b-roll makes it into the actual production brief too,
clearly AFTER a-roll is detailed, and it's carried through our process
properly" — and B-roll "literally has nothing to do with editing": it is
ideated from the swipe and MADE at production. Cards too. Found on a past run:
two B-roll shots written inside talking scenes were never made, and the
swipe's cards (a diagram map three times, three receipts, a CTA grid) were
carried only as edit notes and never made. Four changes, nothing else moves:
(1) the readable view details the A-roll first, then a headed B-ROLL
section, then CARDS; (2) every scene carries `to_camera` as `true` or
`false`, never omitted; (3) a card is its own scene of `kind: "card"` — a
text-free plate to generate plus its exact words as data, replacing
`edit.cards`; (4) the brief ends its view with a ROLL SHEET CHECK and its
block with `roll_sheet`, mapping every swipe make-list row to a scene id or
a drop reason, which the machine checks. Every variable and every other
rule of v12 survives.*

*v14 (2026-09-23): EVERY ELEMENT OF THE VIDEO IS CARRIED. The owner's ruling
the same day: the teardown NAMES every element on the swipe's timeline, the
brief CARRIES it, production MAKES it, the edit only assembles — "all these
elements need to be green." The shape is the one contract every stage uses
(`components/video-production/ELEMENTS.md`). The teardown record now labels
four more kinds of row — `S` screen, `X` split, `G` green-screen, `E` end
card — marks the scroll-stopper, tags every row with a module, and closes
with a Layer sheet, a Sound sheet and a Module map. So: (1) four new scene
kinds — `screen`, `split`, `greenscreen`, `endcard` — each written in its
own table after CARDS and placed in the one `scenes` list; (2) every scene
carries `kind`, `opens`, `module` (retired in v15 — see
below) and `priority`; (3) the block carries
`layers`, `music`, `sfx` and `hooks`, each taken from the teardown (swipe
first) or left empty, never invented; (4) `roll_sheet` maps S, X, G and E to
their kinds. Every variable and every other rule of v13 survives.*

*v15 (2026-09-23): ONE VOCABULARY FOR THE PARTS OF THE AD. v14's `module`
tag was six words of its own that restated the marketing doctrine in a
second, coarser vocabulary — the defect the doctrine's one rule forbids,
because the second list drifts from the first. It is gone. Two new
variables, `{sections}` and `{ad_frameworks}`, bound from the doctrine.
Every scene's `section` is a section id from `{sections}`, spelled exactly
as that list spells it, and the section is the unit the edit swaps between
versions. The piece's `framework` is an id from `{ad_frameworks}`, or
`observed — ⟨a plain description⟩` for a piece that follows none of them —
never a coined name. The teardown record's Module column and Module map are
now a Section column and a Section map, in the same ids. Every variable and
every other rule of v14 survives.*

*v16 (2026-09-23): A SUNG PIECE CARRIES ITS SONG AS DATA. The owner's ruling: "if
we trigger a song to be made, we have a specific protocol / chain to do that
as well, so we remove all hallucination and variables and can just print."
Production prints the song mechanically (`components/video-production/
SONG-CHAIN.md`), so on a SUNG or RAPPED piece the block now carries (1) every
lyric line on the scene it plays over, as `lyrics` — that list IS the script
of a sung piece — with `voice` left an empty string, and (2) one top-level
`song` block: a style id, the vocal, an optional tempo, and the sections in
the order they are sung, each with its length and its lines copied from the
scenes' `lyrics`. No style is ever written in words: the sound is one row of
production's music style bank, by id. On a SPOKEN piece `song` is `null` and
nothing else changes. No new variable. Every variable and every other rule
of v15 survives.*

*v17 (2026-09-23): EVERY ELEMENT IS NAMED, BANKED OR MADE FROM A REAL
REFERENCE. The owner's rulings: "we literally are not using who was in the
original brief" (a re-brief cast different people than the approved
production); "let's make sure our brief for production contains these
elements … the system should prompt to confirm the elements before going
into actual production, and if elements need to be generated we need to go
through that step"; "if you need a second voice that should be clearly
identified at the brief level"; "ALL elements in this flow must be processed
from a real reference"; "seriously every element documented." Production now
refuses a brief whose elements do not resolve (`components/video-production/
PRODUCTION-ELEMENTS.md`, the elements lock). So: (1) every cast member names
their `bank_id` — the folder of a banked person in the cast list above — or
`NEW`, and a NEW person carries the `real_reference` they are built from
(a swipe frame by timestamp, or what to find on Pinterest — never stock,
never words alone) and the `adjustments` that make them ours; (2) a role the
swipe's own production already cast keeps that person; (3) every scene with
words names its `speaker`, and every speaking person names their `voice`:
`banked`, or `NEW` with a persona — never another character's voice; (4)
every scene names the `looks` each person in it wears, by the look ids on
their banked sheet, or `NEW: <outfit>` with its real reference; (5) every
place in `world` names its `bank_id` or `NEW`, with its time of day, light,
dressing and real reference; (6) `piece.product` names the product folder;
(7) every scene lists the `props` a hand touches that are not the product;
(8) the readable view gains an ELEMENTS table — every person, look, voice,
place, product and prop, each marked BANKED or TO MAKE. Every variable and
every other rule of v16 survives.*

*v18 (2026-09-23): THE SPEAKER IS ON EVERY KIND OF SCENE, AND LOOKS USE THE
SHEET'S IDS. The first v17 brief named no `speaker` on its two phone-screen
scenes (their words are read over the screen) and named the presenter's looks with
invented ids ("<name>-outdoor") while their sheet holds three real ones. So:
(1) `speaker` is a key on EVERY scene object — talking, B-roll, card,
screen, split, green-screen, end card — naming the cast id whose voice reads
its `voice`, or an empty string when `voice` is empty; (2) a banked person's
look is one of the ids the cast list prints under "Looks" for them, spelled
exactly — never a new id; a new outfit is `NEW: ⟨outfit⟩`; (3) a banked
person whose cast-list line says their voice is not made yet carries
`"voice": {"source": "NEW", "persona": ⟨that persona, copied⟩}`. Every
variable and every other rule of v17 survives.*

Write the shot list the AI video machine generates from.

**Print the route and the lane first.** The first two lines of your output,
before the concept name and before anything else, are:

```
LANE: ⟨the production route above, in caps⟩
ASK: ⟨PAID or ORGANIC, from THE LANE above⟩ — ⟨the ask, word for word as the last line makes it⟩
```

One line each, nothing after them on those lines. The first line is the
production route (who makes it: AI here); the second is the paid or organic
lane, which changes only the ask. A brief that does not carry both is a brief
nobody can tell was written for the right hand and the right ask.

## THE BRIEF IS THE PROMPT — YOUR OUTPUT HAS TWO PARTS

The owner's ruling, 2026-09-19: *"this brief is for the AI system to interpret,
not for me as the human to read — format these briefs in a way that AI is
able to directly build what it needs to build."*

So this document is not a page somebody reads and then types up. **It is the
input to three machines**, and the fields you write are pasted into them:

| What you write | Where it goes, verbatim |
|---|---|
| a scene's **FIRST FRAME** — its seven fields, in order | the image model, as the whole still prompt for the scene's FIRST shot |
| a shot's **start_picture** (any shot after the first that does not continue the one before) | the same image model, as that shot's own still prompt |
| a scene's **LAST FRAME change** / a shot's **ends_on** | the same image model, as the edit prompt — the delta and nothing else |
| a shot's **lines** — each `say` with its `delivery_tag` | the voice model, one read per line, the tag sent as the line's audio tag; three reads made, one locked |
| a shot's **face · gestures · angle · camera_move** | the motion model, as the acting and the camera of that take (the `emotion` label is not sent: it is the source `face` and `gestures` are written from) |
| a scene's **beats** | the motion model, as the clip's own timeline, one line each, each beat inside its shot |
| a shot's **hands_off** | production (a `continuous` shot ends on the next shot's start picture) and the edit (how the join is cut) |

Your output is therefore **two parts, in this order**:

**Part one — the readable view.** Short Markdown, for a person: the lane
line, the concept name and its paragraph, the cast, the world, the camera,
the product, the product lock, the composition table, the openings, then
the A-ROLL, the B-ROLL, the CARDS, the SCREENS, the SPLITS, the
GREEN-SCREEN, the END CARD, the LAYERS AND SOUND and the ROLL SHEET CHECK,
in that order.
This is what a person opens and what the brief is checked against. **No
machine builds from it** — the block does.

**Part two — THE BLOCK, and it is the brief.** The last thing in your output
is one fenced `json` block in the shape below. Everything a machine needs is
in it. If a fact is in the readable view and not in the block, it does not
exist.

**The two are the same fields.** The view is written FROM the block — never
a second, slightly different version of it. Where they disagree, the block is
right and the view is a bug.

**Every key is present on every object.** A string stays a string, a list
stays a list, `to_camera` is `true` or `false` and never the word — **and
it is written on EVERY scene, never left out.** A scene with no `to_camera`
is refused by id: the machine will not guess whether a scene is the speaker
or B-roll, because a guess is how a B-roll shot quietly became a face. A key with
nothing to say carries an empty string rather than being dropped — a dropped
key is a gap the machine cannot tell from an omission, and it stops the run
by name. Same discipline as the doctrine read's block.

**Nobody is filmed on this route.** The field is called `Shot:` and not
`Film:` for that reason — it names a picture that has to exist, not an
instruction to a person holding a camera. The creator lane's brief is a
different document written for a different hand; nothing in it belongs here. Every person, place and shot in this
document gets generated — a cast station makes each character once, a voice
station speaks the lines, a scenes station composes one still per frame, a
motion station turns stills into clips. This brief is the only thing those
stations read, so everything they need must be on the page as generatable
text. **A detail this document does not carry does not exist downstream** —
there is no set to glance at and no person to ask.

**This prompt supplies no content — the run supplies all of it.** The chain
has already produced everything this brief needs, stage by stage, and your
job is to move each piece into its slot in generatable form. In the shape
below, everything inside ⟨angle brackets⟩ is a slot naming what goes there;
the map below says which input fills it. No angle bracket may survive into
your output — a bracket left in means a slot went unfilled.

## THE SOUND FORM DECIDES WHAT `Say:` IS

The teardown's `Sound form:` line rules this, and it is not a stylistic
note — it changes what you are writing.

- **SPOKEN** — `Say:` lines are spoken lines, exactly as elsewhere in this
  prompt. This is the default and most sources are this.
- **SUNG or RAPPED** — **the script is LYRICS, not narration.** The piece
  is a song. Then:
  - Write the lines as lyrics with their structure marked — `[verse]`,
    `[chorus]`, `[bridge]` — following the source's own structure as the
    teardown recorded it, including which lines repeat.
  - Keep the hook line where the source put it, and repeat it as often as
    the source repeats it. A chorus that lands once is not a chorus.
  - Say-lines still carry the source's meaning in the source's order; you
    are not writing a jingle over the story, you are writing the story AS the
    song, which is what the source did.
  - Add one line at the top of WHAT TO SAY: `FORM: SUNG` (or RAPPED), plus
    the style in a short phrase — genre, tempo feel, the vocal — for the
    person reading. The machine does NOT read that phrase: it reads the
    block's `song.style_id` (v16, below), because a song generated from a
    phrase is a song somebody guessed.
  - Do NOT also write a spoken version. A sung piece has one script.
  - In the block, every lyric line goes on the scene it plays over, as
    `lyrics`, word for word, and the scene's `voice` is an empty string —
    nothing is spoken; the song is the voice. Then the block's `song` puts
    those same lines into sections, in the order they are sung (see PART
    TWO). A line in `song` that is not in some scene's `lyrics` is refused
    by name, and so is a scene line no section sings.

A sung source that produces a spoken brief has lost the format that made
it worth swiping — the failure this section exists to prevent
(2026-09-02).

## WHERE EVERYTHING COMES FROM

Every slot has exactly one source. Never fill a slot from anywhere else,
and never from imagination.

- **The character's body, face, hair, makeup, wardrobe, jewelry** — the
  teardown record's Character & Setting profile. It was written so someone
  who never saw the video could re-create the person; that someone is now a
  generator.
- **Who fills each role** — the banked cast list above FIRST, by `bank_id`.
  A role the bank covers is played by that banked person — the face, the
  looks and the voice on their sheet — and is never re-described or
  re-cast. A role this swipe's own earlier production already cast keeps
  that same person. Only a role the bank does not cover is `NEW`: an
  original character built FROM A REAL REFERENCE — the swipe's own person in
  that role (a frame, by timestamp) or, where the swipe does not show one, a
  real photo to find on Pinterest (say exactly what to look for; never
  stock, never words alone) — plus the `adjustments` that make them ours,
  never a copy of the real person's face. The injection's CHARACTERS section
  says which roles exist; this list says who plays them.
- **The character's voice** — the teardown record's voice observations:
  register, energy, pace, accent cues. Written as a persona in the mold,
  never an imitation of the source creator's voice.
- **The places** — the teardown record's setting and room inventory,
  location by location.
- **The camera** — the teardown record's camera work: phone or high-res,
  front or rear, how it is held or propped, the framing habits.
- **What happens in each frame** — the teardown record's Visuals column,
  carried through the injection's SCENES section, which already wrote the
  production instruction for this route, scene by scene.
- **Every spoken line** — the spice sheet's SPOKEN SCRIPT, paragraph by
  paragraph, word for word: that is the transformed script rewritten for
  the mouth, and it is the only form of the words this brief carries. The
  transformed scripts above are the sense to check it against, never the
  text to paste. The opening lines and scroll stoppers — the hook set.
- **Every caption** — the teardown record's On-Screen Text column,
  verbatim.
- **The product's name** — the product file. **The product's appearance** —
  never generated, and never sent into a generation job: where the product
  is in frame, the real product photo (or real footage of it) is laid on in
  the edit. The frame leaves its place clear.
- **How each shot is acted and shot** — the spice sheet's VIDEO SPICE
  section (the emotion that travels, each person's own gestures, the senses,
  where the angle changes and how the shots hand off), read against the
  teardown record's own camera and cuts, and the realism rows it placed.
- **Each line's delivery and weight** — the spice sheet's LINES block: the
  line, its audio tag and its emphasis words, copied.
- **The ask** — THE LANE above. Nothing else decides it.
- **Every price, claim, offer and guarantee** — the offer and product
  files, and nowhere else.
- **Any styling detail, B-roll lookalike character, or voice register** —
  the spice sheet, cited. The spice sheet never changes what is said or
  the offer; only how a beat looks, sounds, or who fills a B-roll moment.

## THE CREATIVE CONTROLS BECOME WHAT THE CLIP CAN SHOW (VIDEO-RULINGS V47, V46)

Every video is made against one set of dials, guards and a never-sterile floor, the same set copy and pictures use. Here are the video dials, each with its scale and what it does on camera, then the guards and the floor: {creative_controls}

Here are the levels this video aims for: {controls_target}

Here are the twelve emotions and how each one looks on camera: {emotion_video_lines}

The clip model is never sent a dial, a level or an emotion word (a strong label is what lights the eyes or overacts). It is sent only what the shot's `face`, `gestures`, `angle`, `camera_move`, `ends_on` and beat lengths say. So this brief turns the controls into those fields, shot by shot:

| A control | Becomes, in the shot |
|---|---|
| the chosen **emotion** of the shot (one of the twelve, by id) | `face` and `gestures`, written from that emotion's camera line above in your own visible detail |
| **intensity**, **dramatisation** | how big the performance is: how far the head, shoulders and hands move; how sharp the first movement is. The peak lands in the first three seconds |
| **eye contact**, **shot scale** | `angle` and `face`: where the eyes go (lens, away, down to the hands) and how close the camera is |
| **camera**, **cutting rate** | `camera_move` and the number and length of shots in a scene (a beat is never under 2.5 s) |
| **humor**, **irony**, **cringe**, **edge** | the timing and the face: the held beat before the line, the look at the lens, the wince; stated as a visible action |
| **absurdity**, **specificity**, **scale distortion**, **uncanny** | `happens` and the beats: the one impossible or hyper-specific thing that is seen |
| **realism**, **degradation**, **ad likeness** | the style and the realism rows already above; never contradicted by a polished face or gesture |

Rules for the two fields the clip reads:
- Every shot that shows a person to camera has `face` AND `gestures` filled with **visible actions** (eyes, brows, mouth, head, hands, shoulders). An emotion word alone ("shocked", "skeptical") is not an action and counts as empty.
- If a field truly does not apply to a shot, write `n/a: ⟨the reason⟩` in it. It is never left blank and never filled with "natural".
- `emotion` stays in the brief as a label for the people who read it; it is the source the `face` and `gestures` are written from. It is not sent to the clip model.
- If an input above reads `[UNFILLED]`, write on without it and say so in one line.

## ABSURD, SHOCKING, HYPER-SPECIFIC — WRITTEN FOR REACH (V24, 2026-10-21)

(the owner, 2026-10-21: "what makes them stand out on feeds is the absurdity. it has nothing to do with just claims and brand approval … we need to maximize reach / impressions, the shock value absurdity and hyper specific depictions are what make AI content acceptable".) This is content marketing: the job of every line and every picture it describes is to stop a thumb and
earn the next second. On both lanes, in every format:

- **Absurd on purpose.** The demonstration goes over the top: the problem shown bigger, grosser and stranger than
  life (an oversized model of it at the lens, the remedy doing something visibly dramatic), the way the feeds'
  best AI reels do. Never the polite, average version of a moment.
- **Hyper-specific depictions.** Every picture a line calls for is described exactly — its size against something
  we know, its texture, its colour, its smell or sound where it shows, what it does on screen. "A chip" is
  not a depiction; "a thumbnail-sized chip out of a white mug's rim, grey clay showing through, its edges sharp as broken glass" is.
- **Shock is the hook.** The first line and the first picture land something the viewer has not seen and cannot
  look away from, at least as hard as the swipe's.
- This is judged by reach, not by brand taste. Never soften a line, a claim or a picture to make it more polite
  (claims stay bold, V20). The facts that keep it true stay: the right product, its real ingredients, the swipe's
  structure, the ask word for word.

## THE DEMONSTRATION STAYS ON ITS OBJECT (v8)

(2026-10-28, from a review of a finished test ad: the remedy was moved off the swipe's model onto the speaker's own
face, the model changed between scenes, and an ingredient was scooped out of a closed jar.)

- **Where the swipe demonstrates on an object** (a model, a mannequin, a cut-away, a sample), every scene of ours that
  continues that demonstration does it on OUR matching object: the remedy is applied to it, wiped off it, shown on
  it. Never moved onto the speaker's own face or body unless the swipe does that itself.
- **One object, the whole way through.** An object introduced in one scene is the same object, by the same name, in
  every later scene that shows it. Never a new or different one.
- **Props make physical sense.** Every container is opened before anything is taken out; every prop a hand uses is
  already in the shot, within reach, and picked up from where it lies; every ingredient goes somewhere you can see
  and keeps its texture (a thick paste stays a thick paste).
- **A body is a body.** Every shot has the speaker's two arms doing at most two things: a hand that holds one thing
  cannot also hold another; a held container and a hand at work on the object are their two hands, nothing else.

# THE SHAPE OF PART ONE — THE READABLE VIEW

**Everything in this section is the view a person reads.** The block at the
end carries the same facts for the machines, and it is written last, from
these. Keep this part short: it is a page somebody scans, not the document
anything is built from.

The piece block first — aspect and resolution, declared ONCE and never
again — then six locked blocks (cast, world, camera, product, product lock,
composition), then the openings, then the scenes in the order below — the
A-roll first, then the B-roll, then the cards, then the screens, splits,
green-screen and end card, then the layers and sound, then the roll sheet
check.
Each concept:

```
# ⟨concept name — short, the way you'd say it in conversation⟩

Core message: ⟨the teardown record's `Core message:` line, word for word — the one
belief this ad leaves behind (the owner, 5 Oct 2026). Every scene below carries it or
sets up one of the beliefs under it; a scene that does neither is named in the
decisions for the owner, never silently kept.⟩

⟨One to three sentences: the core idea this concept runs on, pulled from its
own material.⟩

## THE PIECE

**Aspect:** ⟨the aspect every still and every clip in this piece carries —
one value, declared here and nowhere else⟩
**Resolution:** 360p — every take is made at 360p and only the picked take is
upscaled, on this machine. Never anything else.
**Style:** ⟨the one grade sentence, the same words in every frame of the
piece — the camera block's own grade, in a single line the machine can
carry into a prompt⟩
**Lane:** ⟨paid or organic⟩ · **Ask:** ⟨the last ask, word for word⟩
**Frames:** ⟨`swipe-edit` when the scenes re-make the swipe's own shots (each
start picture is an edit of the swipe's own frame with our people in it), or
`drawn` when no scene copies a swipe shot⟩
**Room tone:** ⟨the room sound the swipe has, in a few words — or `none` when
the swipe has none⟩
**Spice level:** ⟨the VIDEO SPICE level the spice sheet set⟩

**These three are never written on a scene.** A scene that restates the
aspect, the resolution or the grade is a scene that will disagree with its
neighbours the first time one of them is edited.

## THE CAST

**⟨CHARACTER NAME, in caps — an original name, never the source creator's⟩**
— ⟨age⟩, ⟨build⟩, ⟨skin tone and texture — always natural, visible pores, no
beauty-filter look⟩, ⟨face⟩, ⟨hair — colour, length, how it is worn⟩,
⟨makeup, if the record shows any⟩. An original character resembling no real
person.
Load-bearing: ⟨any body part a frame depends on, and the state it is in —
named because the shots show it close⟩.
**Bank:** ⟨`bank_id` — the banked person's folder from the cast list — or
`NEW`⟩. A banked person is written AS THEIR SHEET IS — the description above,
the load-bearing line and every scene direction use their sheet's gender,
look and words (a hand takes the pronoun the sheet gives that person); never a description carried
over from an earlier cast. The check reads every word about each person
against their sheet and holds the brief on a mismatch. **Real reference (NEW only):** ⟨the swipe frame by timestamp, or the
Pinterest search and what the photo must show⟩. **Adjustments (NEW only):**
⟨what changes from the real person so they are ours⟩.
**Voice:** ⟨`banked` when their sheet has a voice; otherwise `NEW` and the
persona paragraph the voice station designs from — age, register, energy,
pace, accent character, from the teardown record's voice observations. A
person who never speaks: `none`. Never another character's voice⟩.
**Looks:** ⟨each look this person wears in the piece and the scenes it is
worn in — a look id from their banked sheet, or `NEW: ⟨every garment,
colour, material, fit, sleeves, jewellery⟩` from the teardown record's
wardrobe, with its real reference⟩.

**PERSONIFIED PROBLEM (v24, humor rule 2) — only when the injection or the
scripts carry one.** The problem may be cast as a character: the problem itself, a tool that makes it worse, the moment it flares. Mark it PERSONIFIED PROBLEM in the cast. It is the problem given a face, not a new person, and it does not break the structure lock. It is always the villain or the fool, never the customer.

**⟨NAME, in caps, as the upstream script names it⟩ — PERSONIFIED PROBLEM**
— ⟨the problem it is, in the script's words⟩, ⟨villain or fool — the script's
choice, never chosen here⟩, ⟨its form: what it looks like, its size against the
people and the things in the frame, its surface and texture, its colours, its
face and how it moves — written so it can be generated from this text alone⟩.
Never shaped or dressed as the customer or any kind of person the avatar is;
the joke is on the problem, never on who has it.
Load-bearing: ⟨the feature every frame of it depends on⟩.
**Bank:** ⟨`bank_id`, or `NEW`⟩. **Real reference (NEW only):** ⟨the meme,
format or picture the script's receipt names, or what the photo search must
show⟩. **Voice:** ⟨`NEW` and the persona paragraph when it speaks; `none` when
it does not⟩. **Looks:** ⟨its one look and the scenes it is in⟩.

It gets an identity block like any cast member, a row in ELEMENTS (kind
`person`, the Scenes it is in) and the same locked description in every scene
it appears in. This brief casts and stages it; it never invents one. Where the
injection and the scripts carry no PERSONIFIED PROBLEM, this block is left out.

## THE WORLD

**⟨LOCATION NAME, in caps⟩** — ⟨the place written so it can be generated
from this text alone: the surfaces, the furniture, where the window is and
what the light is doing, the believable clutter — the teardown record's
setting and room inventory, described sharper, never relocated, never added
to⟩.

## ELEMENTS

| Element | Kind | Bank id or NEW | Scenes | Real reference (NEW only) | Status |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| ⟨every person, then every look, every voice, every place, the product, every prop — one row each⟩ | ⟨person · look · voice · setting · product · prop⟩ | ⟨…⟩ | ⟨S1, S4…⟩ | ⟨…⟩ | ⟨BANKED or TO MAKE⟩ |

## THE CAMERA

⟨One paragraph, from the teardown record's own camera character: phone or
film, how it is held or propped, framing, colour, grain, skin texture — with
the negatives spelled out: no cinematic grading, no studio light, no beauty
retouching.⟩

## THE PRODUCT

⟨The product's exact name from the product file.⟩ The product is never drawn.
No generation job carries a packshot and no picture or clip is asked to
render the product: where it is in frame, the frame leaves its place clear
(the hand closed around nothing yet, the counter spot bare) and the real
product photo, or real footage of it, is laid on in the edit. Nothing about
its label, shape or colour is generated from words.

## PRODUCT LOCK

⟨Every shot the product appears in, by scene and shot id, with where it sits
in the frame and how it is held or set, the exact label phrase from the
product file copied verbatim, and the instruction: "laid on in the edit from
the real product photo — never generated." A shot with no product in it does
not appear in this list.⟩

## PROPS — each defined once, at its real size

| Prop | Real size | Look | Shots |
|---|---|---|---|
| ⟨every thing a hand touches or the frame depends on that is not the product, named the same way everywhere⟩ | ⟨its real size, measured against a hand or the counter — "a tablespoon, 15 cm long"⟩ | ⟨one look: material, colour, finish, wear⟩ | ⟨S2.1, S4.2…⟩ |

One object, one look, one size for the whole piece. A prop a shot uses is in
this table, and a shot never re-describes it.

## THE MAKE LIST — the start pictures production makes

| Shot | The picture it starts on |
|---|---|
| ⟨S1.1⟩ | ⟨the scene's first frame · an edit of swipe frame K03 with our people · its own start picture · the plate⟩ |

Every shot that needs a picture of its own, and only those: a shot that
continues the one before (`continuous`) starts on that shot's end and is not
on this list. Production makes three of each picture on this list, keeps the
best, and makes nothing that is not on it.

## COMPOSITION

⟨For every scene, the measured frame the character or product must
occupy — subject at ⟨L–R⟩% width, ⟨T–B⟩% height — read from the teardown
record's own Visuals column, so a generated frame lands where the source
frame did, not wherever looks nice.⟩

## WHAT TO GENERATE

### The openings — generate every hook in the set

**Opening 1 · Control**
**Shot:** ⟨the control's scroll stopper, copied from the hook set — the
frame itself in the third person: the character by name, the place by its
world block, what fills the frame⟩
**Say:** ⟨the spoken line, word for word from the hook set⟩
**Words on screen:** ⟨the opening's CARD, word for word from the hook set — or "none" on the control when the source had none⟩
**Thumbnail:** ⟨the opening's THUMB from the hook set: the one still, what is in it and where, and its words⟩

![Opening 1](—)

…every hook, control first, each variation under its own name — each with
its OWN shot, line, words on screen and thumbnail, never another opening's.

### The scenes

**This route generates video, not stills.** A scene is a clip that has to
exist, described so it can be made — never a picture described so it can be
rebuilt.

### THE ORDER THE SCENES ARE WRITTEN IN — A-ROLL, THEN B-ROLL, THEN CARDS (ruled 2026-09-23)

The owner, that day: *"We need to ensure b-roll makes it into the actual
production brief too, clearly AFTER a-roll is detailed."* B-roll is ideated
from the swipe and MADE at production; it has nothing to do with editing.
Cards are made at production too. So the readable view writes the scenes in
three headed passes, and then checks itself:

```
## A-ROLL — the talking spine

**S1 · ⟨setting⟩ · to camera** — ⟨what happens, one line⟩
> ⟨the voice paragraph, verbatim⟩

| Shot | Angle · move | Face · emotion · gestures | Lines (tag · emphasis) | Ends on · hands off |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| S1.1 | ⟨front, chest height · push-in⟩ | ⟨brows drawn · worried · hand to the jaw⟩ | ⟨"the line" [tag] · *word*⟩ | ⟨what the last picture shows · continuous⟩ |
…every `to_camera: true` scene, in play order, each with its shots.

## B-ROLL — what the argument shows, made at production

| Scene | From the swipe | Shows | Plays over (the exact words) |
|---|---|---|---|
| S2 | ⟨the make-list row it comes from: timestamp · label · [shows: …] — or the rule of this brief that added it⟩ | ⟨what is in frame, one line⟩ | "⟨the exact words of the read under it⟩" |
…every `to_camera: false` scene that is not a card, in play order.

## CARDS — made at production as a plate, the words set on it at the edit

| Scene | From the swipe | The plate (the picture, no text in it) | The words, exactly | Lands on | Sound |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| S5 | ⟨the make-list row⟩ | ⟨the picture⟩ | ⟨line 1 / line 2 / …⟩ | ⟨the word or beat of the read it lands on⟩ | ⟨the cue, or none⟩ |

## SCREENS — a plate of a screen, the words set on it at the edit

| Scene | From the swipe | Surface | The plate (the screen, no legible words in it) | The words, exactly | Lands on |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| S6 | ⟨the make-list row: timestamp · S · [screen: …] · [shows: …]⟩ | ⟨review · comment · dm · site · order · search⟩ | ⟨the picture⟩ | ⟨line 1 / line 2 / …⟩ | ⟨the words of the read it lands on⟩ |

## SPLITS — two pictures at once, each side made like B-roll

| Scene | From the swipe | Layout | Left / top | Right / bottom | Labels | Plays over (the exact words) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| S7 | ⟨the make-list row: timestamp · X · [split: …]⟩ | ⟨side · top-bottom · wipe⟩ | ⟨what is in frame, one line⟩ | ⟨what is in frame, one line⟩ | ⟨the words on each half, or none⟩ | "⟨the words⟩" |

## GREEN-SCREEN — a background plate, the speaker keyed over it

| Scene | From the swipe | Layout | Background (the plate) | The speaker | Says |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| S8 | ⟨the make-list row: timestamp · G · [greenscreen: …]⟩ | ⟨cutout · pip⟩ | ⟨what the background is, no legible words in it⟩ | ⟨who, and where in the frame⟩ | "⟨the voice paragraph⟩" |

## END CARD — the real product photo laid on at the edit, the offer's own words, the lane's ask

| Scene | From the swipe | The plate (the ground, the product's place left clear, no added words) | Offer lines, exactly | CTA | Guarantee |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| S9 | ⟨the make-list row: timestamp · E · [endcard: …]⟩ | ⟨the ground and the clear space where the real product photo is laid on at the edit⟩ | ⟨line 1 / line 2 / …, from the offer file⟩ | ⟨the lane's ask, from THE LANE⟩ | ⟨from the offer file, or none⟩ |

## LAYERS AND SOUND — carried from the swipe, never invented

| Element | What it is | On which scene |
|---|---|---|
| Opens | the scroll-stopper | ⟨the scene with `opens: true`⟩ |
| Hook | "⟨the hook text⟩" · ⟨its look⟩ | from frame one |
| Captions | ⟨the look⟩ | all |
| Lower third | ⟨name / title⟩ | ⟨scene⟩ |
| Annotation | ⟨arrow · circle · underline · highlight⟩ → ⟨target⟩ on "⟨words⟩" | ⟨scene⟩ |
| Platform UI | ⟨comment_reply · stitch · duet · search · likes⟩ "⟨text⟩" | ⟨scene⟩ |
| Transition | ⟨continuous · action · match · whip · zoom · flash⟩ | after ⟨scene or shot⟩ |
| Room tone | ⟨the room sound, only when the swipe has it⟩ | all |
| Music | ⟨energy⟩ · ~⟨bpm⟩ bpm · ⟨mood⟩ — or none | all |
| SFX | ⟨cue name⟩ on "⟨words⟩" | ⟨scene⟩ |
| Hooks | ⟨id⟩ · "⟨text⟩" | ⟨opener scene, or its own opening⟩ |
| Sections | ⟨S1 ⟨section id⟩ · S2 ⟨section id⟩ · …⟩, each id from the sections list, with each scene's priority | all |
| Framework | ⟨an id from the ad frameworks list — or observed — ⟨a plain description⟩⟩ | all |
…one row per element the swipe carried. An element the swipe did not carry
is not in this table at all.

## ROLL SHEET CHECK

| Swipe row | Roll | Our scene | Or dropped, because |
|---|---|---|---|
| ⟨timestamp · [shows: …]⟩ | ⟨B-over · B-vo · B-silent · C · S · X · G · E⟩ | ⟨scene id⟩ | ⟨empty when carried⟩ |
…every row of the teardown record's make-list, none missing.
```

**Three passes on the page, ONE list in the block.** The block's `scenes`
stays one ordered list in PLAY order — the A-roll, B-roll and card scenes
interleaved exactly as they play. The passes above are how the brief is
written and checked, never a second order: every B-roll scene listed under
B-ROLL is also in `scenes` at its place with `to_camera: false`, and every
card listed under CARDS is in `scenes` at its place with `kind: "card"`.
Write the A-roll first so the spine is settled; then go back through the
spine and the swipe's make-list and write every B-roll scene against the
exact words it covers; then the cards; then check the roll sheet.

**The same holds for the four kinds added on 2026-09-23.** Every scene under
SCREENS, SPLITS, GREEN-SCREEN and END CARD is also in `scenes` at its place
in play order, with `kind` set to `screen`, `split`, `greenscreen` or
`endcard`. Write them after the cards, each against the make-list row it
re-makes, then the LAYERS AND SOUND table, then the roll sheet check. A
kind the swipe did not have gets no table at all — never an empty one, and
never a scene invented to fill it.

**A B-roll row names where it came from.** Either the swipe make-list row it
re-makes (timestamp · label · `[shows: …]`), or the rule of this brief that
added it (the sections that SHOW, below). A B-roll scene with no origin is
invention; a make-list row with no scene and no drop reason is a shot lost.

### A SCENE IS A PARAGRAPH OF MEANING — SHOT AS SHOTS (ruled 2026-09-18, shot 2026-10-21)

The owner, 2026-09-18: *"Fewer scenes, more frames within the scene describing
what happens, then the end of the scene; audio in each scene — each section
reads as a paragraph, a few sentences."* And 2026-10-20: *"angle changes and
camera movement changes mid scene and into clean transitions is what keeps the
eye engaged. That's how TV and movies are cut … just adapted to mobile."*

Both hold. The scene is the unit of MEANING; the shot is the unit of the
CAMERA. So a scene is:

- **one setting** — a scene never changes place;
- **one section of the argument** — one section id from the sections list
  at the top, named exactly as that list spells it;
- **one PARAGRAPH of voice** — a few sentences, the scene's whole run of
  speech, written once as `voice`. It is split into LINES (below), and each
  line is its own read;
- **one or more SHOTS** — each shot is one take: one angle, one camera move,
  one stretch of the acting, the lines it carries. The scene's FIRST FRAME is
  its first shot's start picture, written in full; its LAST FRAME is its last
  shot's end, written as a delta. A scene with one angle is one shot. A scene
  where the camera goes from the face to the hands is two shots;
- **BEATS** — what happens, one line each, one verb each, and every beat
  names the shot it happens in. **A beat is not a picture.** It is a timed
  instruction inside its shot, and its timing comes from the locked read,
  never from you.

**Fewer scenes, more shots inside them — and the swipe's own shot count.**
A script that used to come out as eighteen scenes of the same person saying
consecutive sentences is a handful of scenes, each playing out a whole
thought, each shot the way the swipe shot that stretch: its angles, its moves,
its cuts. Never more shots than the swipe and the beat need — a shot added for
movement's sake is the mechanical look this rule exists to stop.

### THE SHOT — what every take carries (2026-10-21)

Every shot names, as its own keys — so the take is acted and shot, not just
described:

- **`face`** — what the face is doing in this shot, concretely: where the eyes
  are, the brows, the mouth. Never "natural".
- **`emotion`** — the ACTOR's feeling in this shot, the one the face shows
  (grim at the problem, focused on the hands at the work, a slow smile at the
  result, warm into the lens at the end). It travels: two shots of one person
  in a row rarely carry the same emotion.
- **`gestures`** — this person's own small movements in this shot, from the
  cast sheet and the spice sheet's BODY LANGUAGE layer (a nod while listening,
  a hand to the chest); an empty list when the shot has none.
- **`angle`** — where the camera is: front, three-quarter, down at the counter,
  close on the hands, over the shoulder — with its height.
- **`camera_move`** — exactly one of `push-in` · `pan` · `tilt` · `arc` ·
  `handheld` · `hold`, read off the swipe's own camera for that stretch first.
- **`hands_off`** — how this shot joins the NEXT one (the next shot in the
  scene, or the first shot of the next scene), exactly one of:
  `continuous` (an invisible join: this shot ends on the very picture the next
  one starts on — the next shot's start picture IS this shot's end, and it
  makes no picture of its own), `action` (a cut on the person's action: the
  next shot picks the same movement up from a new angle), `match` (a cut on a
  matching shape or object), `whip` (a fast phone whip between beats), `cut`
  (a plain cut). The last shot of the piece is `cut`.
- **`ends_on`** — what the shot's last picture shows, as a delta from its start
  ("the jar set down square to the lens, the hand leaving frame right"). On a
  `continuous` shot this is the next shot's start picture, so write it as a
  picture that can stand alone.
- **`start_picture`** — `"first_frame"` on the scene's first shot (the
  scene's FIRST FRAME is its picture); `"from S⟨n⟩.⟨m⟩"` on a shot whose
  previous shot hands off `continuous` (it starts on that shot's `ends_on`);
  otherwise the shot's own picture, written like a first frame, in one string
  that stands alone (subject · composition · action · location · camera ·
  viewpoint where faceless · lighting — never a cross-reference).
- **`copies`** — the swipe shot this take re-makes (`K04`), or `""` for a
  script-backed shot.
- **`length`** — `null`. Always. A shot is as long as its locked read; the
  machine measures it after the voice is made. Never a number of yours.
- **`lines`** — the lines of the scene's paragraph spoken over this shot, in
  order, each as `{"say", "delivery_tag", "emphasis"}`, copied from the spice
  sheet's LINES block: `say` is the line word for word, `delivery_tag` is its
  one v3 audio tag in square brackets (or `""` when the line is said straight),
  `emphasis` is the list of its words that take the weight, spelled as the
  line spells them (an empty list when none). A shot with no words over it
  carries an empty list.

**One line, one read.** Every line is recorded on its own, three times, and
the best read is locked; the reads are laid end to end, so the voice runs on
across shots and scenes without a gap that was not in the script. The lines
of a scene, joined with single spaces in order across its shots, ARE its
`voice` paragraph, byte for byte.

**Someone on screen who is not speaking listens in silence, lips closed.**
When another person's line (or a voice over) plays over a shot that shows
them, their `face` says so — listening, lips closed — and nothing in the shot
says what the speaker says or names what the speaker names: a motion model
handed those words makes the listener say them.

### B-ROLL IS A SCENE — AND IT IS HALF THE PIECE (ruled 2026-09-19)

The owner, that day, reading a brief where every scene was the same person
talking to the lens: ***"there is no B-roll."***

The owner is right, and it is the biggest single fault in the shape this prompt
used to write. A piece where the picture never leaves the speaker's face is a
piece nobody watches to the end, and every place the argument SHOWS something
was written as a note inside somebody else's beat, where no machine could
reach it.

So, from now on:

**Where the argument shows something, that is a scene of its own.** The
product in the hand. The jars on the shelf. The closet rail. The hands at the
sink. A before and after. The mechanism demonstrated. Each of those is a
scene with `to_camera: false`, its own `who` (the hands · the product · the
speaker seen and not speaking · nobody), its own setting, its own FIRST
FRAME, its own beats, its own LAST FRAME — and its own slice of the
paragraph, the words that play OVER it.

**Never an insert.** No `(insert …)` inside another scene's beat. No
`Cutaways:` line under a scene. A clip never cuts, so a picture wedged
inside another shot's prose is a picture that never gets made. An angle
change inside a scene is a SHOT of its own in `shots`, with its own start
picture and its hand-off; a cut away to something else the argument shows is
a B-roll scene. The gate refuses an insert or a cutaway line by name.

**No B-roll inside a talking scene, in any wording** (2026-09-23). "The
marked-up <body part> gets its own tight shot", "one shot of the product worked onto
<the surface it goes on>", "then a close-up of…" written into a to-camera scene is the
same insert with the marker taken off — a past run wrote exactly those two, and
neither was ever made. Any shot that needs a hard cut away from the speaker
is its own `to_camera: false` scene, covering its own slice of the voice:
split the paragraph at the words the shot plays over, give the talking
scene the words before, the B-roll scene the words under the shot, and the
next talking scene (if the speaker comes back) the words after.

**The rhythm, stated plainly.** A piece of this length alternates: a
to-camera scene, then a B-roll scene carrying the next paragraph, then back —
unless the format profile says otherwise. A to-camera scene and the B-roll
scene after it are a pair: one says it, the next shows it, and the voice runs
straight through both without stopping. A pair is written as **two scenes**
with two ids (`S8a` and `S8b` is fine), not one scene with a note in it.

**The sections that SHOW take B-roll, and this is not a preference.** Where a
scene's argument is one of these, it is a B-roll scene or the second half of
a pair:

| The beat | What is in frame instead of a face |
|---|---|
| the things already tried and abandoned | the row of them, on the surface where they live |
| the mechanism, demonstrated | the hands doing it, close, the change visible as it happens |
| the product | the thing itself, held, set down, opened — from its own reference |
| proof | the evidence, physically present |
| a before and after | the two states, same crop, same light |
| a behaviour changing | the behaviour, happening and then not happening |

**The teardown record labels the roll — use it.** Every row of the record now
carries a **Roll** label (`A` · `B-over` · `B-vo` · `B-silent` · `C` · `S` ·
`X` · `G` · `E`) and a **Section** — a section id from the same list at the
top of this brief — one row is marked `opener: true`, and the
record closes with a **Roll sheet**: the make-list of every shot that is not the
speaker — then a Layer sheet, a Sound sheet and a Section map. That list is the floor for your B-roll, not a suggestion:

- Every `B-over`, `B-vo` and `B-silent` row on the source's make-list becomes a
  B-roll scene of ours that shows the equivalent thing — the row's `[shows: …]`
  tag, carried through the injection — over the equivalent words. If one is
  deliberately not carried, it is named in `roll_sheet` with the reason.
  A make-list row that simply disappears is the failure this rule exists for.
- A `B-over` and a `B-vo` row are made the same way here: a B-roll scene with
  its lines' locked reads over it. The difference the label
  records — whether the source ever showed the speaker — tells you which
  to-camera scene it pairs with.
- A `C` row is a **card**: the product on a plain ground, a receipt, a
  diagram (an outline with the problem areas circled), a price card, a product grid,
  a title, a text-only frame. **Every card is a scene of ours, made at
  production** — never an edit note. Type is a layer, never a generation, so
  a card is TWO things: the **plate** — the picture with no words in it (the
  blank receipt, the leg diagram with its circles, the ground of a product
  card with the product's place left clear for the real product photo, a
  plain ground for a text-only card) — which the
  machine generates as one still; and the **words**, exactly, which the edit
  sets on top. The card scene object is below. A price or figure on a card
  comes only from the offer file; a figure the offer file has not settled is
  written `[UNFILLED]` in that card line, never guessed. This is the one
  place an unresolved mark may stand: the plate still gets made, the gap is
  visible to the owner, and the machine names it — the words cannot ship
  until the offer file settles them.
- An `S` row is a **screen** scene: `kind: "screen"`. Like a card, it is a
  plate and its words — the plate is the screen itself (the review panel,
  the comment, the message thread, the web page, the order page, the search
  bar) with every place a word would sit left blank, and the words go in
  `screen.lines`, exactly, for the edit to set. The words are the swipe's,
  carried through the injection; a star rating, a count, a price or a
  customer's words that are not ours come only from the product and offer
  files, or the line is cut. Never a real person's name or handle.
- An `X` row is a **split** scene: `kind: "split"`. Two pictures in one
  frame — each side is made like its own B-roll shot, with its own first
  frame, beats and last frame — plus the `layout` the swipe used and the
  `labels` written on the halves, verbatim from the swipe.
- A `G` row is a **green-screen** scene: `kind: "greenscreen"`. The speaker
  is keyed over a background that is not their room. It is made as two
  things: the `background` plate (the article, the post, the product page —
  with every place a word would sit left blank), and the speaker's own
  talking clip, which is THIS scene's first frame, beats, last frame and
  voice — so a green-screen scene is `to_camera: true`, and its first frame
  shows the speaker against a plain flat green ground.
- An `E` row is the **end card**: `kind: "endcard"`. The plate is the swipe's
  kind of ground with the product's place left clear; the REAL product photo
  is laid on at the edit — never described into existence, never generated.
  On the PAID lane its `offer_lines` and `guarantee` come ONLY from the offer
  file; on the ORGANIC lane both are empty (`[]` and `""`) — no offer stack
  (V23). Its `cta` is the lane's ask on both; a figure the offer file has
  not settled is `[UNFILLED]`, exactly as on a card, and a guarantee the
  offer file does not hold is an empty string. An organic source with no
  `E` row may still end on an end card when the script's own close calls
  for one; it is then `"source": "none — script-backed"` like any other
  scene past where the source ends.

**Four more fields on EVERY scene — including cards and the four kinds
above** (2026-09-23):

- **`kind`** — `talking` · `broll` · `card` · `screen` · `split` ·
  `greenscreen` · `endcard`. A `talking` scene is `to_camera: true`; a
  `broll` scene is `to_camera: false`. The two must agree; a disagreement is
  refused by id.
- **`opens`** — `true` on exactly ONE scene: the scroll-stopper, the one
  the teardown record marked `opener: true` and the control hook opens on.
  That is Scene 1 almost every time. `false` on every other scene.
- **`section`** — one section id from the sections list at the top of this
  brief, spelled exactly as that list spells it, and nothing else: never a
  synonym, never a shorter tag of your own. Taken from the teardown record's
  Section column for the row this scene re-makes; a script-backed scene
  takes the section its paragraph is actually doing. The edit swaps whole
  sections between versions, so a scene's section is what it DOES, not
  where it sits. A scene the record labelled with a plain description
  `(candidate for the bank)` carries that description — a finding for the
  owner, never a new id.
- **`priority`** — `1`, `2` or `3`. `1` is never cut: the opener, the
  product's entry, the mechanism shown, the end card and the call to
  action. `2` is cut only for the shortest cutdown. `3` is the first to go:
  a second proof, a repeated beat, a pause shot. The 15 / 30 / 60 s
  cutdowns cut the highest number first and never cut mid-scene, so a scene
  a cutdown cannot lose without breaking the argument is a `1`.

**Two counts to run before you write the block**, and both are things the
finished brief is read against:

1. **No more than two to-camera scenes back to back.** Three faces in a row
   with nothing shown between them is the shape this ruling replaced.
2. **At least a third of your scenes are B-roll.** Fewer than that and you
   have written the old document with two pictures added to it. Count the
   `to_camera: false` scenes against the total before you finish.

In both counts a `greenscreen` scene is a to-camera scene (the speaker is
on screen saying it), and a `screen`, `split` or `endcard` scene is a
shown scene, like B-roll.

A `product` section that is a face talking about the product while holding it
is the exact failure this rule names: the product scene is the product, in
frame, doing something — and the speaker's line about it plays over that.

**The voice never stops at a scene boundary.** Each line is its own read,
and the locked reads are laid end to end for the whole piece. A B-roll
scene's lines are recorded like every other and laid under the picture at
the edit rather than dubbed onto it. That is the only difference between the
two kinds of scene.

**A scene is `to camera` when the speaker is on screen saying the
paragraph.** Then its shots carry its lines, and the approved read of each
line is fitted to the speaker's lips after the take is made. A B-roll scene
is `to_camera: false`: nobody speaks in the clip, no mouth moves in it, and
the locked reads play over it.

**Two things the world block must not sand off, because they are usually the
mechanic rather than the set dressing.**

**The place is doing work.** A format built on a credentialed person
disqualifying things borrows its authority from where they are standing — the
shop floor, the counter, the wall of stock behind them with its prices and its
slightly-out-of-line boxes. Take that away and put them against clean
shelving in flat even light and the shot reads as a stock photo, which is the
one thing an authority format cannot afford. Write the room as the real,
ordinary, commercial place it is: crowded, coloured, lit by whatever is on the
ceiling, with its own business visible behind.

**Named products are shown, not blanked.** When the script says a competitor's
name, the hand holds that competitor, label square to camera and legible. The
recognition IS the argument — the viewer has that exact bottle in their own
bathroom, and that is why the beat lands. A brief that writes "plain
unlabelled bottle, no logo" while the line says the name has removed the
mechanic and left the sentence: the source's whole hook works because you know
the blue tin on sight.

**Never name the camera. Describe what the shot looks like.**

A generator has no concept of a camera it is looking *through* — everything you
name is an object it can put *in* the picture. Write "shot on a phone on a
tripod at chest height" and it renders a phone on a tripod, standing in the
room, in the middle of the ad. Write "handheld", "gimbal", "35mm", "webcam",
"lit with a softbox", and the same thing happens with those.

Say the result instead: how it is framed, how high, how flat the light is, how
much depth there is. "Framed square on at chest height, flat even overhead
light, little depth" gets the phone-ad look with no phone in it.

And in any scene with a person talking to camera, say the negative out loud:
**no camera, no phone, no tripod, no lights, no microphone, no filming
equipment in frame.** The one place this does not apply is a scene whose
subject genuinely is someone filming — then the gear is the content.

**The rule that governs all of it: never describe anything a reference
already shows.** The cast block holds the face; the world block holds the
room; the FIRST FRAME holds the framing, the blocking and the light. The
LAST FRAME therefore names ONLY what changed, and a beat names only what
happens. Re-describing tells the model to rebuild, which is the generation we
are avoiding, and it is how a lead ends up wearing another character's
jacket.

So: **the cast block and the world block are written once, at the top, and
never repeated in a scene.** A scene names the character; it does not
re-describe them.

**THE SCENE OBJECT.** Every scene in the block is exactly this, every key
present:

```json
{
 "id": "S⟨n⟩",
 "kind": "⟨talking when to_camera is true · broll when to_camera is false⟩",
 "opens": ⟨true on the ONE scroll-stopper scene, false on every other⟩,
 "priority": ⟨1 never cut · 2 cut only for the shortest cutdown · 3 first to go⟩,
 "section": "⟨one section id from the sections list at the top, spelled exactly as that list spells it — from the teardown record's Section column for the row this scene re-makes; or the record's plain description if it flagged the scene as a candidate⟩",
 "technique": "⟨the technique this scene runs, as the expansion pass's depth ledger named it⟩",
 "delivery": {
  "humor": "⟨this scene's humor dial, from the spice sheet's block for this section, spelled exactly as the delivery dials spell it. A dial the spice sheet left [UNFILLED] is written \"unfilled\" — never guessed, and never chosen at this stage⟩",
  "style": "⟨the delivery-style dial⟩",
  "register": "⟨the register dial⟩",
  "pacing": "⟨the pacing dial⟩"
 },
 "emotion": "⟨the ACTOR's feeling across this scene — what the speaker's face carries; each shot's own `emotion` says how it moves shot to shot⟩",
 "outcome": "⟨where the viewer lands at this scene's end⟩",
 "setting_id": "⟨the world block this scene plays in, by its id — one place, for the whole scene, and an id that exists in world[]⟩",
 "happens": "⟨what this scene DOES, as one short verb phrase with the subject left off: \"tells the lens how the same problem keeps coming back\", \"counts the four things that did not work\", \"works the product in until its texture changes\". It is the middle of the clip's own one-sentence summary — `⟨who⟩ at ⟨setting⟩ ⟨happens⟩, ⟨the style⟩, ⟨the camera⟩` — so it has to read as a verb phrase and not as a description of a picture⟩",
 "who": "⟨who is in it: a character name, or — on a B-roll scene — HANDS, the product, the character seen and not speaking, or nobody⟩",
 "speaker": "⟨the cast id whose voice reads this scene's words — REQUIRED on every scene with words, including a B-roll scene the voice plays over; an empty string on a scene with no words⟩",
 "looks": {"⟨each cast id in the scene⟩": "⟨a look id printed under their name in the cast list, spelled exactly — or NEW: ⟨outfit⟩ for a person or outfit the bank does not hold⟩"},
 "props": ["⟨each prop in this scene, by its name in the block's `props` list — never re-described here⟩"],
 "to_camera": ⟨true when the speaker is on screen saying the paragraph; false when this is a B-roll scene and the voice plays over it — REQUIRED on every scene, never omitted; a scene without it is refused by id⟩,
 "voice": "⟨THE PARAGRAPH — the scene's whole run of speech, every sentence of it, as one string, copied word for word from the spice sheet's SPOKEN block for this section; it is the scene's `lines`, across its shots, joined with single spaces. No em-dash, no en-dash, no semicolon, no parenthesis, no bracket; the ellipsis is the mid-thought breath and is kept; one thought per breath, and the gate refuses the rest by name⟩",
 "lyrics": [⟨ONLY on a SUNG or RAPPED piece: the lyric lines sung over this scene, one string per line, word for word as the WHAT TO SAY lyrics carry them — and then `voice` is "". On a spoken piece leave the key out⟩],
 "first_frame": {
  "subject": "⟨who or what is in it, by name, and what the body is doing — never a re-description of the face⟩",
  "composition": "⟨how it is framed and how much of the frame each thing occupies⟩",
  "action": "⟨the single action visible at this instant⟩",
  "location": "⟨the world block, by name, plus the one detail that anchors it⟩",
  "style": "⟨the piece's grade sentence⟩",
  "camera": "⟨the result — how high, how close, how much depth. Never a device⟩",
  "viewpoint": "⟨REQUIRED whenever the scene's `who` is someone's hands, arm or body part, or the product — that person's face is out of frame, EVEN WHEN another person's face is in the shot — and on any frame with no face at all: whose eyes the camera is, and which edge of the frame every limb enters from, elbows off-frame. \"Their own point of view: the camera is where their eyes are, looking down. Both forearms enter from the bottom edge of the frame, elbows out of frame, angled slightly toward the basin. No arm enters from any other edge.\" Leave the key out only when everyone in the frame is shown with their face⟩",
  "lighting": "⟨where the light comes from and how flat it is⟩",
  "refs": ["⟨the references this frame is built from, in order — the cast sheet, then the style frame; never a packshot or a product picture⟩"]
 },
 "shots": [
  {"id": "S⟨n⟩.1",
   "start_picture": "first_frame",
   "copies": "⟨the swipe shot this take re-makes, K-id — or \"\"⟩",
   "angle": "⟨where the camera is and how high⟩",
   "camera_move": "⟨push-in · pan · tilt · arc · handheld · hold⟩",
   "face": "⟨visible actions of the face in this shot: eyes, brows, mouth — or `listens in silence, lips closed` — never an emotion word; `n/a: reason` if it truly does not apply⟩",
   "emotion": "⟨the actor's feeling in this shot, one of the twelve by id — a label for the reader, not sent to the clip model⟩",
   "gestures": ["⟨visible movements of the head and hands in this shot — or `n/a: reason`⟩"],
   "lines": [{"say": "⟨one line of the paragraph, word for word⟩", "delivery_tag": "⟨one v3 audio tag in square brackets, or \"\"⟩", "emphasis": ["⟨a word of the line that takes the weight⟩"]}],
   "length": null,
   "ends_on": "⟨what the shot's last picture shows, as a delta from its start⟩",
   "hands_off": "⟨continuous · action · match · whip · cut⟩"}
 ],
 "beats": [
  {"id": "B1",
   "shot": "⟨the id of the shot this beat happens in⟩",
   "do": "⟨ONE visible action, one verb, present tense⟩",
   "camera": "⟨only where the camera moves; otherwise `held`⟩",
   "over": "⟨the words from the paragraph playing over this beat, copied out of it⟩",
   "bracket": "⟨how it is delivered, and the feeling it carries — this is where `says`, `stops`, `slows` and `settles` belong⟩"}
 ],
 "last_frame": {
  "camera": "⟨`same` · `push-in to ⟨framing⟩` · `pull-out to ⟨framing⟩` · `new angle to ⟨framing⟩` — one of exactly these four — where the scene's LAST shot ends up against the FIRST FRAME⟩",
  "change": "⟨what is different from the FIRST FRAME at the end of the scene's last shot — that shot's `ends_on` — and nothing else⟩"
 },
 "on_screen": "⟨the caption, verbatim from the teardown record's On-Screen Text column — or \"nothing\"⟩",
 "hold": "⟨anything that must not change from the previous scene — the same skin, the same light, the same crop⟩",
 "source": "⟨the frame-map shots this scene copies, then the source beat shortened: \"K02–K04: the hand points to and touches their face\" — or \"none — script-backed\"⟩"
}
```

**THE CARD SCENE OBJECT** (2026-09-23). A card is a scene in `scenes`, at
its place in play order, and it is exactly this — every key present:

```json
{
 "id": "S⟨n⟩",
 "kind": "card",
 "speaker": "⟨the cast id whose voice reads this scene's `voice` — an empty string when it has none⟩",
 "opens": ⟨true only if this card IS the scroll-stopper; otherwise false⟩,
 "priority": ⟨1 · 2 · 3⟩,
 "section": "⟨the section id, from the sections list, of the argument this card lands in⟩",
 "to_camera": false,
 "plate": "⟨THE PICTURE, and nothing written on it: what fills the frame, where it sits, the ground behind it, the grade — \"a plain white till receipt, flat and square to the lens, filling the middle two thirds of the frame, blank where the lines would print, on a warm grey counter\" · \"a clean line drawing of <the body part the problem sits on>, front view, three soft circles on its front and one on its side, on an off-white ground\". Leave clear empty space where the words will be set, and where the product sits on the card. Never a word, a letter, a number or a price in the plate, and never the product itself — the real product photo is laid on at the edit⟩",
 "refs": ["⟨the references the plate is built from — the style frame; never a packshot (where the product is on the card, its place is left clear and the real product photo is laid on at the edit); an empty list when it needs none⟩"],
 "lines": ["⟨the card's words, EXACTLY as they are set, one string per line, in reading order — the swipe card's words carried through the injection, a price or figure only from the offer file, `[UNFILLED]` where the offer file has not settled it⟩"],
 "lands_on": "⟨the exact words of the read, or the beat, the card lands on — copied out of the neighbouring scene's paragraph or this card's own⟩",
 "sfx": "⟨the one sound the card comes in on, from the Sound sheet — a pop, a whoosh, a till ding — or an empty string for none⟩",
 "voice": "⟨the lines that play over the card, verbatim from the spice sheet's SPOKEN block — or an empty string when the card sits between two paragraphs in silence⟩",
 "source": "⟨the make-list row it re-makes: timestamp · C · [shows: …]⟩"
}
```

A card has no `first_frame`, no `beats` and no `last_frame`: nothing moves
in it and nothing speaks in it. The machine makes ONE still — the plate —
and carries `lines`, `lands_on` and `sfx` to the edit as data, which sets
the words on the plate. A card with words baked into its plate is refused.

**THE SCREEN, SPLIT, GREEN-SCREEN AND END-CARD SCENE OBJECTS**
(2026-09-23). Each is a scene in `scenes`, at its place in play order, and
each is exactly its shape below — every key present. The kind's own fields
