## THE SWIPE'S FORMAT — declared at teardown, never re-guessed (v2)

The teardown already decided what kind of swipe this is. Write inside it:

{declared_layers}

Never re-classify the swipe, never call it a different format, never switch
who carries it. If something below seems to ask you to decide the format,
the answer is the one above.

## THE CREATIVE CONTROLS FOR VIDEO (VIDEO-RULINGS V47, V46)

Every video is made against one set of dials, guards and a never-sterile floor, the same set copy and pictures use. Here are the video dials, each with its scale and what it does on camera, then the guards and the floor: {creative_controls}

Here are the levels this video aims for: {controls_target}

Here are the twelve emotions and how each one looks on camera: {emotion_video_lines}

How every pass uses them:
- The floor holds on every awareness level, offers included: nothing below it, and a real mark or face is never kept or blanked.
- A dial is a level of something the viewer can see or hear. Write each one into the words, the faces and the timing, never as the label.
- If any input above reads `[UNFILLED]`, write on without it and say so in one line.
- THIS PASS: set the script's humor, absurdity, valence arc (open low, close high), dramatisation and borrowedness at the levels above, in the words the lines use. The dial must be audible in the line itself.

## THE LANE — paid or organic, and it changes only the ask (2026-10-21)

{ask_lane}

Organic and paid are the same video. The owner, 2026-10-20: *"Our organic and
paid content should be indistinguishable. The only core difference is really
just the ask and the way that we gradualize that sales pitch."* So the lane
above changes exactly two things, and nothing else:

1. **The last ask.** Paid: tap the button below and buy. Organic: comment the
   word (the one word the lane line names), or follow. The last thing said and
   the last thing on screen is that ask, and only that ask.
2. **How gradually the pitch arrives.** Organic lets the story carry longer
   before the product's first claim; paid may bring it sooner. The ORDER of the
   argument never changes.

The job, the product, where the product enters, every claim and the finish are
the same on both lanes. Claims stay as bold as the brand files allow on both
(the owner, 2026-10-20: "don't worry about over claiming at this point it's
content"). An organic piece is never a piece without the product, and a paid
piece is never a different video with a button added.

**On the organic lane the close is the turn and the ask — no offer stack** (the owner, 2026-10-21: "i'm going for
real binge watch content man, nothing sterile at all"). After the remedy, the product's turn and the lane's ask,
word for word, and nothing else: no bundle or multi-buy, no dosing or usage schedule, no guarantee text, no
label-style statistics ("85 in 100…"). The product, the mechanism and the claims stay — as bold as the brand files
allow. The paid lane keeps the offer.

## THE SWIPE'S BITE — KEEP ITS SHOCK LEVEL (2026-10-21)

The owner, 2026-10-21: *"i'm going for real binge watch content man, nothing sterile at all"* · *"this script seems
much more tame."* Found on a run the same day: the swipe's "parasites hiding in your gut lining" came out as "<our problem's
mildest label> hiding under the surface" — the same sentence with the disgust taken out, and the reason anyone kept watching
gone with it.

So, for every line of the swipe that carries its bite:

1. **A scary, concrete thing maps to an equally concrete thing for our problem — never a mild, abstract word.**
   Name what our problem physically IS, at its most vivid and slightly gross true version: what it is made of,
   where it sits, what it does, what it looks like up close. "<the problem's name>" is the label; the bite is what the
   brand files and the avatar's own words say they are and do. A word a clinical leaflet would use is the
   sterile version, and it is a defect.
2. **Name an ENEMY, in the swipe's own shape.** Where the swipe had a culprit (the parasites, the toxin, the
   thing hiding), ours has one too, said as plainly, sitting in the same place in the sentence.
3. **Name a VISIBLE RESULT, in the swipe's own shape.** Where the swipe promised something you can see or feel
   change, ours promises the thing a viewer would see change on themselves.
4. **Same shock level, never higher than the files can carry and never lower than the swipe.** Every fact still
   comes from the brand's files and the avatar's words; the bite is in which true thing you name and how
   concretely you name it. Claims stay bold (the owner, 2026-10-20: "don't worry about over claiming at this point
   it's content").

In the ELEMENT LEDGER, every line that carried the swipe's bite is a row: the swipe's words, ours, and `KEPT` (as
concrete and as charged) — a line marked softer is an Adapted change and goes to the owner.

Here is the doctrine read of the source as swiped — its humor, delivery, register, reference-world and mood lines included: {doctrine_read}
Today is: {today}
Here is real language selected for this stage: {language}
Here is a complete teardown of a video asset: {teardown_record}
Here is the brand's banked cast — every person already made, with their name, age, look and voice. A name, an age, a credential or a tenure written here is a SUPPLIED fact: use it rather than leaving a slot, for the role that person plays: {casting}
Here are the body techniques and what each one is for: {techniques}
Here is our brand's declared position — its LINE, SPINE, MECHANISM, the MARKET STAGE it sells
into and what that stage LEADS WITH, as labelled slots: {position}
Here is our brand's story — who tells our version and what happens to them: its SPINE, TELLERS, STORIES with their beats and who each fits, and, once stage 1b has run, the story and teller this run picked (THIS RUN'S STORY), as labelled slots; `[UNFILLED]` when the brand has no story yet: {story}

## THE SWIPE'S STRUCTURE IS LOCKED — read this before anything else

You adapt a swipe; you never rebuild it. Keep, exactly as the swipe has them:

- **Every place.** Each location stays the same kind of location (for example, an office stays an office and a street outside a business stays a street outside a business). Only what identifies the source changes — a real business name becomes an unbranded or fictional one.
- **Every role.** The people on screen stay the same kinds of people in the same number: for example, three experts stay three experts of the same kind, and one creator talking to camera stays one. Never replace a role with a different kind of person, never merge two, never add one.
- **The problem may be cast as a character** (humor rule 2, v21). The problem may be cast as a character: the visible sign, the cause, the tool that failed, the flare-up. Mark it PERSONIFIED PROBLEM in the cast. It is the problem given a face, not a new person, and it does not break the structure lock. It is always the villain or the fool, never the customer.
- **Every shot's camera.** A selfie shot stays a selfie shot (the speaker holding the phone, arm's length, handheld), a handheld walk stays a handheld walk, a two-shot stays a two-shot, a close-up stays a close-up. Who holds the camera, how close it is and how it moves are the swipe's, shot for shot. Never turn a selfie into a composed tripod shot or the reverse.
- **Who says each line, the number and order of scenes and beats, and the story's progression** (whatever steps the swipe moves through, in that order).

**The lock covers the swipe's OWN shots; it never stops what the swipe lacks from being ADDED.** An organic swipe has no commercial structure — no product, mechanism, proof, offer or close; and the expansion and close stages exist to add it (the owner, 2026-09-24: "expansion should've been triggered here because it was organic"). Added beats are built INSIDE the swipe's world: its places, its people and its camera styles (for example, a selfie, a close-up on hands, a propped shot) — never a new kind of place or person (a PERSONIFIED PROBLEM is not a new person: it is the problem given a face, the villain or the fool, never the customer) — and every added beat is tagged [ADDED]. Leave room for them; do not squeeze the commercial structure into a voice-over on the last card.

What you change is only: our brand, our product, our claims and offer, the words (by the substitution rules below), and names.

**The brand's position and story files shape the WORDS and the angle of each line. They never move a place, remove or replace a role, or change the story.** A position line such as "our setting replaces theirs" tells you how our people TALK; it does not license taking the swipe's setting out of the swipe.

If you believe the position genuinely conflicts with the swipe's structure, do NOT resolve it yourself. Keep the swipe's structure in everything you write, and add at the very end a block:

```
## STRUCTURE QUESTIONS FOR the owner
| # | The swipe has | The position suggests | Why it might matter |
```

The owner decides. The runner stops the chain on any such question, and on any ELEMENT LEDGER row marked Adapted or Dropped, until they approve it by number. The only structural changes you may apply are the ones listed here as approved for this run: {structure_approvals}

---

**The language above is QUERIED, not dumped.** These rows were chosen
because of what they ARE — the kind of thing this stage needs — and ranked by
topic fit, how loud the row was, and how well sourced. Each is a real sentence
a real person said, carrying who said it and where it came from.

Use their words. Weigh the provenance: a row flagged `ATTRIBUTION ASSUMED` is
weaker evidence, and a paid panel is not a customer speaking — never present
either as something a customer said. If nothing in the rows fits, say so; an
empty result is a fact about the bank, not a licence to write the sentence
yourself.

**The bank is evidence, not a script. Interpret it; do not transplant it.**
Everything in it was true of a particular person, at a particular moment, in a
particular conversation — none of it was written for the asset you are making.
Re-judge every line before using it: does it fit what this is about, who is
speaking, and when this runs. **Keep the truth, drop the frame.**

**A month must be the real one.** Today's date is at the top and it is data,
exactly like the avatar and the banks. The banks record months because real
people name them — but they recorded when the speaker spoke, not when this
runs. Do not inherit theirs; substitute ours. A genuine historical reference — a decade,
an age, "when the kids were small" — is a fixed point and stays.


And here is its replication spec: {replication_spec}

Our brand: {brand_name}. Our avatar: {avatar}. Our product details:
{product_file}. Our offer: {offer_file}. Our language bank:
{language_bank}. Our identity anchors: {identity_anchors}. We are
producing this via {production_route}.

Rebuild this exact video for our brand and turn it into a production
brief.

**THIS IS A SUBSTITUTION, NOT A REWRITE.**

Work through the teardown record's VERBATIM TRANSCRIPT (the numbered
table at the end of the teardown record — T1, T2, …) one line at a time, in
order. For each numbered source line, output one line for us — including
every short reply and interjection ("Mm-hmm." "No." "That's scary." "Why?"
"Huge."): the exchange between the people IS the video. The shot table's
speech column is a fragment for orientation only; never work from it. The source script is
the template — you are swapping the brand-specific words out of it and
our brand-specific words in, and changing nothing else.

Hold all of this exactly as the source has it:

- **Sentence structure and word order.** If the source says "And not
  because it's loaded with X to do Y to the thing it acts on," ours
  says "And not because it's loaded with [our X] to [our Y] to the thing that
  looks like this."
- **The connectives and their sequence.** "And not because…" / "Or
  because…" / "And definitely not because…" is three lines with those
  three openers, in that order. Never collapse them into one.
- **Line count.** One output line per source line. Never merge two source
  lines, never split one, never drop one, never reorder them, never add
  one.
- **Length and rhythm.** Each line stays about as long as the source line.
  If the source rambles, ours rambles. If it repeats a phrase four times,
  ours repeats it four times.
- **Register and filler.** Keep the slang, the contractions, the asides,
  the "yeah," the "I don't know if," the profanity if it's there. Do not
  make it more polished than the source. **This is camouflage, in the
  technique list supplied above** — believability borrowed from the medium
  the asset lives in, by holding the host format's own register and
  phrasing so there is no gear-shift into ad voice. Polishing the register
  is not a small stylistic edit; it removes the technique the source was
  running, and the viewer's skepticism arrives with it.
- **Lines with no brand content stay identical.** Copy them across
  verbatim and mark them unchanged.
- **A cut-off sound is not a word.** The transcript writes down what was
  heard, stumbles included ("that really br- br- that little bit"). A
  fragment (a word cut off before it was finished, marked with a hyphen or
  never completed) cannot be said from a script, and a voice reads it as
  nonsense. In our line, keep the stumble's rhythm with real words, or drop
  it: "that really br- br- that little bit" → "that really… that little
  bit". Every word in our column is a word a person would write down. This
  is the only change an otherwise-identical line takes, and it is not
  marked unchanged.

Swap only what is genuinely brand-specific: the brand name, product name,
ingredients and actives, the mechanism, the problem and the body part it
affects, the competitor or procedure being displaced, the origin story,
the offer, and any numbers.

**THE LINE IS THE SPINE OF EVERY SWAP.** `{position}` is the brand's declared
reason to exist for this reader, and it decides what fills the slots the source
opened — it never adds a line the source did not have.

- Where the source carried **its** mechanism — the how, the why-it-works, the
  why-everything-else-failed — ours carries `MECHANISM`, named in
  `MECHANISM NAME`'s own words. Never a generic "formula", never an
  ingredient list standing in for the how.
- Where the source named **its** problem, ours uses `PROBLEM WORD` — the
  reader's dominant word, not the category's synonym.
- Where the source displaced **its** competitor or procedure, ours displaces
  from `DISPLACES` — a thing this reader has actually tried, per the bank.
- Where the source's line was **its** brand's one sentence — the claim it
  hangs on — ours is `LINE` or `SPINE`, cut to the source's length and rhythm.
- Anything in `NEVER` is never written, even when the source's structure
  invites it; write the swapped line the structure demands with the banned
  word replaced by the bank's, and record the substitution in CONFLICTS.

**THE STORY CARRIES THE PERSON.** `{story}` says who tells our version and what
happens to them. The swipe supplies the structure; the story decides whose it is.

- Where the source had **its** person, ours is the `TELLER` in THIS RUN'S STORY,
  cast through {identity_anchors}. Never a person the anchors do not allow.
- Where the source told **its** before, turn and after, ours tells the picked
  story's beats in that order: `BEFORE` where the source set up, `TURN` where the
  source turned, `AFTER` where it paid off. The source's timing stays; the story
  fills it.
- Where the source gave **its** reason to switch, ours is `REASON TO SWITCH`, which
  points at `DISPLACES` in `{position}`.
- The product enters where `PRODUCT ENTERS` says, inside the slot the source gave
  its product. If the source shows it earlier, keep the source's timing and record
  the clash in CONFLICTS.
- A story's receipt is a real person's words: quote it only as the story file quotes
  it, tagged `VERBATIM`. A beat the story describes but no receipt or bank row
  supports is written from the bank and tagged as the bank tags it.
- Anything in the story's `NEVER` is never written.

If `{story}` is `[UNFILLED]`, or 1b picked `none fit`, keep the source's own arc,
swap facts only, and print `STORY: none` in check 4.

**What the stage changes.** `MARKET STAGE` and `LEADS WITH` say what this
market still believes. A source that leads with a plain claim, swapped into a
market at *claims exhausted*, is a script whose opening the reader has already
discounted — you still swap it faithfully (the structure is the source's), and
you flag it in CONFLICTS as `STAGE: the source leads with <x>; our market leads
with <LEADS WITH>`. The hook and placement passes read that flag.

**Do not improve the source.** Do not soften a claim, fix a tone, correct
an exaggeration, replace a device you find distasteful, or restructure a
beat because a better version occurred to you. Your judgment about whether
the source is a good ad is not wanted here — it already performed, which
is why it was swiped.

**Do not invent.** If a line needs a fact we have not supplied — a price,
an offer, a user count, a study, a person — put `[SLOT: what's needed]`
inline exactly where the fact goes, and list it at the end. Never guess a
number.

The test for "supplied" is exact: **a fact is ours only if it is written
in one of the files above.** Not something you know about the category,
not something that is probably true of a brand like ours, and above all
not something you read in the source video. Three cases account for
nearly every leak, and each has one correct move:

- **The source's offer is the source's.** A sale, a discount, a price
  drop, a "cheapest it has ever been," a limited window — those are facts
  about *their* brand. Ours has exactly the offers written in
  {offer_file} and no others. Substitute a claimed promotion only if the
  offer file carries it; otherwise the swapped line keeps the source's
  shape with `[SLOT: current promotion — needs owner approval]` in place
  of the claim. An empty promo calendar means evergreen only; it does not
  mean you may describe a sale.
- **Only the supplied product is ours to describe.** If the script needs
  a second product — a bundle partner, a companion item, a follow-up
  step — you may name it if the offer file names it, but you may never
  state its ingredients, texture, mechanism or claims from memory. Write
  `[SLOT: <product name> — ingredients and claims]` and move on. Pulling
  an ingredient from the product file you *were* given and attaching it
  to a product you were *not* given is the most common version of this
  error and the hardest to spot on the page.
- **The source's actives are the source's.** Never carry a source
  ingredient into our line, and never invent one to fill the slot the
  source ingredient occupied. Ours come from {product_file} only.

A `[SLOT: …]` costs a reviewer thirty seconds. An invented sale or an
invented ingredient goes on camera.

**Conflicts get flagged, never fixed.** If a source line, once swapped,
would break a rule in {language_bank} or {product_file}, still write
the swapped line as the structure demands. Then record it in the CONFLICTS
section: the line, the rule it breaks, and what it would cost to change
it. The owner decides, not you.

Return exactly these seven sections and nothing else.

**1. THE INJECTED SCRIPT**

A table: `# | Timestamp | Speaker | Source line | Our line`

One row per numbered line of the VERBATIM TRANSCRIPT, in its order, with its
number (T1, T2, …) in the first column — every number, none skipped, none
merged. Speaker: who says it on screen (the transcript's voice label read
against the shot table — e.g. the creator, the first expert). Source line
quoted exactly from the transcript. Our line is the substitution, said by the
same person. Mark any identical line "unchanged" in our column. A line you
believe cannot be carried stays in the table with our column reading
`DROPPED — <why>` and a Dropped row in the ELEMENT LEDGER: the chain stops
for the owner on it. Added beats (expansion) are not in this table — they come
later, in their own stages.

**2. DOMINANT TRAIT**

One line. The product has one trait that summarises it — the thing the whole
argument hangs from, taken from the product file and named in the product
file's own words. Not a list, not the three best benefits: one. Cite the line
in the product file it comes from. Every other trait blends in beneath it,
and a script that leads with a second trait is a script written for a
different product.

If the product file does not settle it, say `[SLOT: dominant trait — the
product file does not name one]` and carry on. Choosing one yourself is
inventing a positioning.

**The position settles it first.** If `{position}` names a `MECHANISM`, the
dominant trait is that mechanism, in `MECHANISM NAME`'s words, and the product
file is its receipt — cite both. The slot above is for a brand whose position
file and product file are both silent. A dominant trait that contradicts the
LINE is a defect: print the LINE beside it and flag it.

**3. FUNCTIONAL LEAD**

One line. **The functional product is what it DOES; the physical product is
what it is MADE OF, and only the functional performance sells.** Name the one
functional performance this injection sells, in the words the avatar would
use for it.

Then list the physical facts you carried across and, beside each, which of
these jobs it is doing — it may do only these: justify the price · document
the quality · promise durability · sharpen the mental picture · supply a
fresh believability mechanism. **A physical fact with no job on that list is
subordinate to nothing and does not belong in the script** — it is a
specification the viewer did not ask for. Cut it or move it under the
performance it proves.

A line that leads with an ingredient, a material or a component, where the
source's line led with an outcome, has inverted this and has to be rewritten.

**4. THE CHARACTERS**

Who is on screen, taken from {identity_anchors} — name, how they look, and
which role from the spec they fill. If a role has no real person assigned,
write `[SLOT: identity_anchors — role]` and do not describe an invented
person.

**5. THE SCENES**

One row per scene, following the teardown record's own scene breaks and
the spec's structural skeleton. Each scene gets: the script lines it
covers, its duration, exactly what happens in frame, and the production
instruction written for {production_route} — ready to execute, no
judgment calls left open. Match what the reference did in frame at each
beat.

**THE DEMO SHOWS WHAT OUR PRODUCT DOES.** The swipe's demonstration keeps its
place, its hands and its beat — but it shows our product's action, never a
result our product does not deliver in that moment. Where the source shows an
outcome on camera (a lift, a change, a before-and-after inside one shot), ours
shows an outcome only if the product file states that outcome AND that it
happens that fast; otherwise the scene shows the how — the product, the
gesture, the steps — and nothing on screen changes. A picture makes a promise
the words cannot take back.

**6. SLOTS**

Every `[SLOT: …]` from above, listed with what fact would fill it and who
would have it. If there are none, say so.

**7. CONFLICTS**

Every swapped line that breaks a brand rule: the line, the rule, and the
cost of changing it. If there are none, say so.

---

## WHAT IS NEVER SUBSTITUTED

Added at v5, after a run destroyed a video's punchline while reporting success.

Substitution replaces **the thing being sold**. It does not replace **the thing
being worn, revealed, or laughed at.**

### The failure this exists to stop

The source: a 70-year-old talks for 35 seconds about a red string swimsuit a
relative left behind, then walks through a doorway **wearing it**, holds both
arms out, pulls a horrified face, and retreats. The swimsuit is the setup, the
wardrobe, and the punchline.

The injection swapped the swimsuit for a tube of the brand's own product — the
object held up, the thing that "didn't work for me" — and then wrote:

> *"The speaker walks through the doorway in a sleeveless top or short sleeves,
> arms bare, having previously been covered. Structurally identical to the
> source's swimsuit walk-through."*

It is not structurally identical. The laugh is a person of that age walking out
in a string swimsuit. Changing into a short-sleeved top is getting dressed. The
video kept its shape and lost the only reason anyone watched to the end, and
every pass downstream inherited a version with no climax and reported fine.

### The rule

Before substituting any object, ask: **is this object worn, revealed, or
reacted to on camera?**

- **If it is only referred to or held up** — a bottle, a jar, a gadget on
  a counter — substitute freely. That is the job.
- **If it is worn, or the camera's reaction to it is the payoff** — it stays.
  Every time. The swimsuit stays a swimsuit. The dress stays a dress. The
  wardrobe change stays a wardrobe change.

**Some products cannot be worn at all.** When the source's object cannot
physically be our product, that is not a licence to change the object — it is
the signal that the object was never the swap.

### Where the product goes instead

It enters **after** the beat, as what the speaker does about what the reveal
exposed. They still walk out in the swimsuit. The camera still finds what the
reveal exposed on their arms and legs. That is our problem statement, delivered
by the source's own punchline — stronger than anything we would write, because
it is already funny and the speaker is already in on it.

Follow the source until the product has a reason to exist. On organic sources
that reason almost always arrives *after* the payoff, never in place of it.

**The product may be the punchline of a beat we add** (humor rule 4, v21). The
source's own payoff is never replaced. But where the product enters, the joke
may land on it working. Keep: no joke on the proof or the offer. Allow: the twist beat may be the product doing its job, shown, not claimed. A plain beat with the proof always follows it. A claim stuffed inside the gag does not land; the
plain beat after it does.

### The check before this pass ends

Find the beat with the biggest visual reaction in it. Read our version of that
beat. **If a stranger would not laugh, wince or lean in at the same moment the
source made them, the substitution has eaten the asset.** Put the object back.

---

## EXAMPLES ARE EXAMPLES

Every specific in this prompt — an age, a gender, a garment, a body part, a
product category, a named person — is **illustrative only**. None of it
describes the asset in front of you. Read an example for the shape of the rule,
then apply the rule to what you actually have.

The subject may be any age, any gender, anywhere, selling anything. This chain
runs the same on one person on a beach, someone talking to camera in a parked
car, two people in a kitchen, or a pair of hands on a countertop. Nothing in
this prompt assumes which.

**Who is on camera comes from the teardown record, never from this prompt.** If
the teardown says a person in their fifties in a parked car, that is the
subject — every rule below applies to them unchanged, and any pronoun in an
example above is about that example, not about the person in front of you.

**And the variables are agnostic too.** An example may be reworked freely — it
is illustrative and always was. But nothing that gets filled in at run time
may name a brand, a product or a category: brand detail arrives only through
the variables above, resolved from that brand's own folder. A rule that
cannot be written without naming the brand belongs in the brand folder, not
in this prompt. This machine is meant to run on every brand we own; anything
that only makes sense for one of them does not scale.

---

## THE ROUTE CHANGES WHAT A PRODUCTION INSTRUCTION IS

`{production_route}` is not a label to repeat back. It decides what a
production instruction *means*, and the two are different documents for
different hands. Write for the route you were given and for no other.

### AI — nobody is on set

The instruction is addressed to a generator and to an editor with a footage
library. Every beat resolves to one of three things and says which:

- **CUT** — the brand already owns footage that carries this beat. Name the
  creator and what to look for. Owned footage is always the first thing to try.
- **GENERATE** — no owned footage carries it. Say what exists in the shot;
  cast people by their banked character names so they resolve to trained
  identities, never by fresh description.
- **COMPOSITE** — a label, a price, a guarantee, a wordmark. Never generated,
  always laid over from the real asset.

Two things only this route has to obey, both learned the hard way:

- **Never name camera equipment.** A generator has no camera it looks
  *through*; anything named is an object it can stand in the room. Say the
  framing and the light, and on any shot with a person, say the negative:
  no camera, no phone, no tripod, no lights in frame.
- **A named product is shown with its label.** If the script says a
  competitor's name, the hand holds that competitor, readable. Blanking it
  removes the mechanic and leaves the sentence.

### CREATOR — a real person films this

The instruction is addressed to someone holding a phone, in their own home,
with what they already own. It is a shot they can actually get:

- **Say what to film, not what to render.** Where they stand, what is behind
  them, what is in their hand, what they do with it.
- **Props are things they can buy or already have.** Name the product on the
  shelf at the drugstore; add the fallback line for when they cannot get it.
- **Wardrobe is theirs.** Describe the register, not a costume they have to
  source.
- **Their camera and their tripod are fine.** They are filming; the gear is
  how the shot exists, and none of the AI route's equipment rules apply.
- **Never direct a shot that needs a second person** unless the brief says
  who, and give a solo alternative when it does.

### FOUNDER — the founder films it

As CREATOR, but the person is known and their world is known. Skip the
fallbacks written for someone whose room you have not seen.

---

## THE ELEMENT LEDGER — the last thing you write, and it is not optional

The spec handed you numbered **ELEMENTs**, each with a **MANDATE**. Those are
the format's load-bearing parts — the reasons it works. Account for every one
of them, by number, in a table at the end of your output:

```
## ELEMENT LEDGER
| # | Element | Carried / Adapted / Dropped | How, or why not |
|---|---|---|---|
```

- **Carried** — it survives intact. Say in what beat.
- **Adapted** — it survives in a different form. Say what changed and why the
  mandate still holds. **An Adapted or Dropped row stops the chain until the owner
  approves it** — so adapt only what must change for our brand, and never a
  place, a role, or the story (v12).
- **Dropped** — say so out loud, and say what it costs. A dropped element is
  sometimes right. A dropped element nobody noticed never is.

**Swipe had it, we swap it (the owner, 2026-10-27).** Every ELEMENT the swipe has stays in our version, swapped for
ours. Nothing is dropped, and no ruling or note removes an element: "we cannot overcomplicate this process … Swipe had
this element. We make swap. It's as simple as that." (One run dropped the swipe's two physical models and
moved the split onto the cast member; the swap was two physical models of our own.)

**This exists because of a real failure (2026-09-11).**
Two mandated elements went missing between the spec and the brief and nothing
caught them:

- The spec mandated *"a real, ordinary, public, commercial space with its own
  visible business in the background — never a studio."* The injection wrote
  "a treatment room", because that is where our delivered footage happens to
  be shot. The generated scenes came back as a white void and read as stock
  photography — which is the one thing an authority format cannot be.
- The spec mandated the presenter *"physically hold and point to high-volume,
  **recognizable** category competitors."* The injection wrote "no label". The
  script still said the competitors' names, so the hand held a blank bottle
  while the line named a product — the mechanic removed, the sentence left.

Both came from the same instinct, and it is a good instinct pointed the wrong
way: **what we already own quietly overrode what the format requires.** Using
owned footage is right. Letting its limits rewrite a mandate without saying so
is not. If the footage cannot carry an element, that is an **Adapted** row with
its reasoning, or a **Dropped** row with its cost — and then somebody can
decide whether to shoot it, generate it, or accept the loss.

## Before you hand this over

Run these and **print the result of each**. A check whose result is not
written down did not happen, and a principle in a bullet list does not bind —
this is the same rule stated as work.

**Fix first, in the copy itself, then report.** You cannot go back and edit
what you already wrote, so what you print above must already be corrected. A
fix described under an uncorrected line leaves the wrong words where people
read from.

**1 · TIME.** Name every month, season and holiday you wrote. Beside each,
confirm it agrees with the date at the top. A month that is not the current
one is a defect unless it is plainly historical — substitute the real one and
report it fixed. "The bank said July" is not a defence.

**2 · SOURCE.** For every concrete specific — an age, a number, a duration, a
named behaviour, a quoted line — name the file it came from **and tag what
kind of language it is**: `VERBATIM` (a real person's recorded words) ·
`AVATAR` (a documented behaviour in the profile's phrasing) · `ANGLE` (a line
a marketer drafted) · `FILE` (a product or offer fact) · `POSITION` (the brand's
declared line, mechanism or stage, from `{position}`). These files record the
difference deliberately; flattening it presents a copywriter's invention as
something a customer said. Anything untraceable is removed or rewritten.

Then one line: how many specifics are `VERBATIM`, and which lines carry
`ANGLE` language. A piece resting mostly on `ANGLE` is not disqualified — it
is a piece whose realness has not been demonstrated, and whoever reads it
should be told that plainly.

**3 · THE LINE.** Print the `LINE` from `{position}`. Then name the beat, by
timestamp, where our script says it, its `SPINE`, or its `MECHANISM` in
`MECHANISM NAME`'s words. If no beat carries any of the three, print
`LINE HAS NO BEAT` and add it to CONFLICTS — the source's structure gave it no
room, the placement pass decides where it lands, and you do not add a line to
make room. Then print every `NEVER` word that appears in our column; each one
is a defect fixed in the copy above and reported here.

**4 · THE STORY.** Print `STORY` and `TELLER` from THIS RUN'S STORY. Map each of
the story's beats (BEFORE · REASON TO SWITCH · TURN · AFTER) to the timestamp in our
script that carries it, or print `BEAT MISSING` for it and add it to CONFLICTS. Then
one line: does the person on screen match the TELLER.


**5 · SHOWN RESULTS.** List every scene where something changes on camera
because of our product. Beside each, quote the product file line that says it
does that, and how fast. A change with no such line is rewritten to show the
action instead, and printed again, corrected.

## LANGUAGE USED — the last thing you print

LANGUAGE USED:
- <row id> | <the words as they appear in what you wrote>

One line for every language row above whose words you carried into what you
wrote. The id is the one in [brackets] beside the row. A row you read and did
not use is not listed. If you used none: `LANGUAGE USED: none`. The machine
reads this block to mark those rows as used in this asset, so the next run
reaches for words we have not said yet — list only what you actually used.


## THE VIBE HOLDS

This is social media. We are technically making an ad, but it has to live in
the feed as content, and the source earned its numbers partly on how it
feels. The doctrine read above names that feel: its `HUMOR`, `DELIVERY
STYLE`, `REGISTER`, `PACING`, `REFERENCE WORLD` and `AVOID` lines.

Print, before anything else you write:

`THE VIBE: <one line — how the source feels to watch: funny or straight, and
in which register; the world it leans on; what it never does>`

Then hold it in every line you write:

1. **A funny source stays funny.** Where the source jokes, what you write
   keeps joking in the same register — new lines included. Humor stays out of
   the proof and the offer beats only where the source itself played those
   straight.
2. **A planted joke needs a receipt** (humor rule 1, v21; culture rules v2). Straight is the default. A straight source stays straight unless the culture read or the culture map gives a receipt AND the piece is not a testimonial, a before/after, a founder story, a demonstration, or about pain, health or money. A straight source may take a planted joke only when it has a receipt: a meme, format or reference the culture read or the avatar's culture map shows this audience already laughs at. Name the receipt. A joke the writer invented is still a finding.
3. **Every new line sounds like it came from the same video**: the same
   speaker's voice, the same reference world, nothing from the `AVOID` line.
   A line that reads like an ad dropped into the video is a defect, however
   true it is.
4. If the doctrine read says `not shown in the record` for a line, hold what
   the source's own transcript does.

**Feel versus structure.** Some `AVOID` lines are about feel (a too-clean
look, stacked superlatives, ad-voice, a sell in the opening seconds) and some
are about structure (no price, no proof beat, no mechanism, no offer). Feel
always holds. Structure gives way to the sections line: on a Variation video
run, a section the branch's sections line adds is written — in the source's
feel — even where the source avoided it. A price is still bound by the target
level's must-not.

**The mood and the one-of-a-kind line hold too.** The doctrine read's `MOOD:`
(the rhythm and where it shifts) and `WHAT MAKES IT UNIQUE:` (the combination
worth banking) are part of the feel: new lines keep the mood's rhythm, and the
unique combination is never smoothed away.


## THE CULTURE HOLDS (v20, 2026-10-05)

Here is the culture read of the swipe — what its audience recognizes in it, with receipts, why it lands, what must be kept and what breaks it: {culture_read}

Here is this avatar's culture map — what they watch and listen to, who they trust, their references and slang, what they care about, what they laugh at, and what is off-limits: {culture_map}

The swipe's references are part of the swipe, so the structure lock already covers them. This says how:

1. **A person the read names is that person in our version too.** For a reference read `CONFIRMED` or `LIKELY`, write the role with the read's NAME IT AS words, never the record's generic label, and carry every item on its KEEP lines (the look, the manner, the line, the setting, the pairing, the timing of the reveal) exactly where the swipe has them.
2. **A known line the swipe says is a fixed line.** A line spoken or shown in the swipe that the read marks load-bearing stays word for word; our words go around it, never over it.
3. **Nothing on a NEVER line appears in what you write**, above all a generic stand-in where the swipe had someone the audience recognizes.
4. **The brand never explains or renames the reference.** It enters through the swipe's own slots, as everywhere else in this prompt.
5. `POSSIBLE` and `UNIDENTIFIED` references are kept as the swipe shows them and never named. `NO CULTURE READ` or `CULTURE LOAD: NONE` changes nothing above.

# Writing v2 · pass A — Reading + Hooks

This is one pass with 2 parts. Write every part, in order, in ONE answer.
Each part begins with its marker line, printed exactly as shown and alone on
its line — the machine splits your answer on these lines:

=== PART 1: READING ===
=== PART 2: HOOKS ===

A later part reads the earlier parts of THIS answer where its rules say
"given above" / "your PART n". Inputs are given once, at their first mention;
a later mention that says "(given in full above)" means the same block.

## THE SWIPE'S FORMAT — declared at teardown, never re-guessed (v2)

The teardown already decided what kind of swipe this is. Write inside it:

{declared_layers}

Never re-classify the swipe, never call it a different format, never switch
who carries it. If something below seems to ask you to decide the format,
the answer is the one above.

## THE CREATIVE CONTROLS FOR VIDEO (VIDEO-RULINGS V47, V46)

Every video is made against one set of dials, guards and a never-sterile floor, the same set copy and pictures use. Here are the video dials, each with its scale and what it does on camera, then the guards and the floor: {creative_controls}

Here are the levels this video aims for: {controls_target}

Here are the twelve emotions and how each one looks on camera: {emotion_video_lines}

How every pass uses them:
- The floor holds on every awareness level, offers included: nothing below it, and a real mark or face is never kept or blanked.
- A dial is a level of something the viewer can see or hear. Write each one into the words, the faces and the timing, never as the label.
- If any input above reads `[UNFILLED]`, write on without it and say so in one line.
- THIS PASS: write each hook version to reach the stopping-power and intensity levels in its first three seconds. For each version name the emotion it opens on (by id from the twelve) and, in a few visible words, what the opening face and hands do.

## THE LANE — paid or organic, and it changes only the ask (2026-10-21)

{ask_lane}

Organic and paid are the same video. The owner, 2026-10-20: *"Our organic and
paid content should be indistinguishable. The only core difference is really
just the ask and the way that we gradualize that sales pitch."* So the lane
above changes exactly two things, and nothing else:

1. **The last ask.** Paid: tap the button below and buy. Organic: comment the
   word (the one word the lane line names), or follow. The last thing said and
   the last thing on screen is that ask, and only that ask.
2. **How gradually the pitch arrives.** Organic lets the story carry longer
   before the product's first claim; paid may bring it sooner. The ORDER of the
   argument never changes.

The job, the product, where the product enters and every claim are the same
on both lanes; only the close differs (below — V23). Claims stay as bold as the brand files allow on both
(the owner, 2026-10-20: "don't worry about over claiming at this point it's
content"). An organic piece is never a piece without the product, and a paid
piece is never a different video with a button added.

**On the organic lane the close is the turn and the ask — no offer stack** (the owner, 2026-10-21: "i'm going for
real binge watch content man, nothing sterile at all"). After the remedy, the product's turn and the lane's ask,
word for word, and nothing else: no bundle or multi-buy, no dosing or usage schedule, no guarantee text, no
label-style statistics ("85 in 100…"). The product, the mechanism and the claims stay — as bold as the brand files
allow. The paid lane keeps the offer.

**Keep the swipe's bite in every opening** (V23, the owner 2026-10-21: "nothing sterile at all"). A scary, concrete
thing in the swipe's opening becomes an equally concrete, slightly gross true thing for our problem — never a mild,
abstract word — and the opening names the enemy and the visible result in the swipe's own shape. A variation that
is tamer than the swipe's opening is a defect.



---

=== PART 1: READING ===

## PART 1 — READING: the rules for this part

Here is a baseline injection — our brand substituted into a format that
performs: {baseline_injection}

Here is the teardown record it came from: {teardown_record}
Here is its replication spec: {replication_spec}
Here is the product: {product_file}
Here is our objection bank: {objection_bank}
Here are the five awareness levels, each with what it must and must not do:
{awareness_levels}

Read the source. Decide nothing about our version yet — that comes after the
hooks are written, because the opening is inherited from the source and should
not be shaped by a placement decision made before anyone has seen it.

Three rules govern this pass:

**The lane sets the depth.** An asset that is already an ad has structure,
offer logic and objection handling to inherit, so the work is mostly swaps.
An organic asset has none of that, so the added work is heavy — but heavy
means justified beat by beat, never doubled by default. A static asset has no
sequence, no scenes and no runtime. A sequential asset carries an awareness
ladder even without motion.

**Everything here is a reading of what the source already is.** Nothing in this
pass invents, improves or plans. If you find yourself deciding where our product
goes or how long our version runs, stop — that is the later pass.

**Cite, never derive.** Every answer points at a timestamp in the teardown or a
line in the spec.

Give me these three sections, and nothing else.
**1. THE LANE**

Answer with exactly one of these four labels, spelled this way, and nothing
else: **ALREADY AN AD** · **ORGANIC** · **STATIC** · **SEQUENTIAL**. Then one
line of why. Later passes key off this label, so an invented name for it
breaks them.

If it is STATIC, say so and stop here — only the opening pass runs.

**3. THE OPENING BEAT**

The asset's first beat, identified by its timestamp from the teardown record,
quoted. This is the beat the opening pass rewrites — not the second line, not
the first line that mentions the product. Name it explicitly so the next pass
cannot guess.

**4. AWARENESS**

The rungs have names, and they are the five supplied above. Use those names,
spelled exactly as the slice spells them — an invented label for a rung is a
rung no later pass can check a line against.

Three labelled lines:

- **Entry level** — the named level the viewer is on when this asset finds
  them. Read it off the source: what it explains, and what it takes for
  granted. Cite the timestamp that settles it.
- **Exit level** — the named level the asset has to land them on by its end,
  and therefore how many rungs it has to carry. Cite the beat where it tries.
- **Entry must-not** — that entry level's `must_not`, quoted verbatim from
  the slice above. This is the line the hook pass is checked against, so it is
  copied, never summarised.

Each level sits behind a wall from its neighbours: a line written for one
level is invisible or stale at another. That is why the entry level decides
where the argument is allowed to BEGIN — at the product, at the desire, or at
the problem — and why breaking the entry level's must-not is a defect rather
than a matter of taste.

**An upstream `[SLOT: …]` is still empty.** Where the baseline injection
left a slot, the fact behind it does not exist yet — do not describe the
end rung as if it did, and never restate a claim the injection deliberately
slotted out. This holds for every section of your output, not only this
one: a fact that was too uncertain to write into the script is too
uncertain to assert in a plan built on it.


---

Renumber them 1, 2, 3 in your output: **1. THE LANE**, **2. THE OPENING BEAT**,
**3. AWARENESS**. Write nothing else. No product placement, no runtime budget,
no device check, no risk — those come after the hooks exist.

---

## EXAMPLES ARE EXAMPLES

Every specific in this prompt — an age, a gender, a garment, a body part, a
product category, a named person — is **illustrative only**. None of it
describes the asset in front of you. Read an example for the shape of the rule,
then apply the rule to what you actually have.

The subject may be any age, any gender, anywhere, selling anything. This chain
runs the same on one person on a beach, someone talking to camera in a parked
car, two people in a kitchen, or a pair of hands on a countertop. Nothing in
this prompt assumes which.

**Who is on camera comes from the teardown record, never from this prompt.** If
the teardown says a person in their fifties in a parked car, that is the
subject — every rule below applies to them unchanged, and any pronoun in an
example above is about that example, not about the person in front of you.

**And the variables are agnostic too.** An example may be reworked freely — it
is illustrative and always was. But nothing that gets filled in at run time
may name a brand, a product or a category: brand detail arrives only through
the variables above, resolved from that brand's own folder. A rule that
cannot be written without naming the brand belongs in the brand folder, not
in this prompt. This machine is meant to run on every brand we own; anything
that only makes sense for one of them does not scale.


---

=== PART 2: HOOKS ===

## PART 2 — HOOKS: the rules for this part

Today is: {today}
Here is the doctrine read of the source as swiped — its humor, delivery and reference-world lines included: {doctrine_read}
Here is real language selected for this stage: {language}
Here is the replication spec — the construct of this script: {replication_spec}
The reading of the source is your PART 1 above, in this same answer.
Here is the baseline injection: {baseline_injection}
Here is the teardown record: {teardown_record}
Here is our language bank: {language_bank}
Here is our avatar: {avatar}
Here are the five awareness levels, each with what it must and must not do:
{awareness_levels}
Here is our brand's declared position — its LINE, SPINE, MECHANISM, the MARKET STAGE it sells
into and what that stage LEADS WITH, as labelled slots: {position}
Here is our brand's story — who tells our version and what happens to them: its SPINE, TELLERS, STORIES with their beats and who each fits, and, once stage 1b has run, the story and teller this run picked (THIS RUN'S STORY), as labelled slots; `[UNFILLED]` when the brand has no story yet: {story}

Here is where the ad starts — the line the owner chose, word for word, or `none`: {opening_at}

Here is the target awareness for this run — filled only on a Variation video run, empty everywhere else: {target_awareness}
Here are the sections this awareness level carries, from the awareness map — filled only on a Variation video run, empty everywhere else: {branch_sections}

**The language above is QUERIED, not dumped.** These rows were chosen
because of what they ARE — the kind of thing this stage needs — and ranked by
topic fit, how loud the row was, and how well sourced. Each is a real sentence
a real person said, carrying who said it and where it came from.

Use their words. Weigh the provenance: a row flagged `ATTRIBUTION ASSUMED` is
weaker evidence, and a paid panel is not a customer speaking — never present
either as something a customer said. If nothing in the rows fits, say so; an
empty result is a fact about the bank, not a licence to write the sentence
yourself.

**The bank is evidence, not a script. Interpret it; do not transplant it.**
Everything in it was true of a particular person, at a particular moment, in a
particular conversation — none of it was written for the asset you are making.
Re-judge every line before using it: does it fit what this is about, who is
speaking, and when this runs. **Keep the truth, drop the frame.**

**A month must be the real one.** Today's date is at the top and it is data,
exactly like the avatar and the banks. The banks record months because real
people name them — but they recorded when the speaker spoke, not when this
runs. Do not inherit theirs; substitute ours. A genuine historical reference — a decade,
an age, "when the kids were small" — is a fixed point and stays.


Write the opening. Six versions — a control and five variations, five
different TAKES on the same ad. **All of
them ship to test.** The human gate on this pass is approval, not
selection: nothing is picked, nothing is ranked, and the body written
after this pass must carry any hook in the set. The variations are the
test cells; the market decides.

**A hook is five things, printed as five labelled slots: its TAKE (the angle
in a few words), its LINE (what is said), its CARD (the words on screen in the
first second), its SCROLL (the scroll stopper) and its THUMB (the thumbnail —
the still that sells the video in the grid, with its words).** The line is what the speaker
hears or reads; the scroll stopper is the first-frame visual event that
arrests the thumb before a single word lands. Neither is optional and
neither is the other's caption — they are the same thought in two
channels, show and tell at once. "Selfie walk-and-talk at the entrance"
is a camera setup, not a scroll stopper. A scroll stopper is a thing
*happening*: an act mid-motion, an object where it should not be, a
texture close enough to feel, a before mid-transformation. If the frame
would read as ordinary with the sound off, it stops nobody. Take the
visual vocabulary from what the source already does where it serves;
build inside the format's world where it does not.

**The spec already did the abstraction — read it, do not redo it.** The
replication spec names the opening phase, its duration and its fixed
bounds in the structural skeleton; the element inventory carries the
opening element's mandate; the script architecture names the premise and
its job. **That is the foundational hook, and it is read out of the spec,
cited, never re-derived from the teardown.** The teardown record is for
what was in frame; the reading supplies the awareness entry. If
the spec is missing any of this, that is a stage-2 defect — flag it and
stop rather than deriving around it.

**The reading names the opening beat by timestamp.** Rewrite that
beat and no other. Do not rewrite a later line because it looks more like a
hook.

**THE OPENING POINT — when `{opening_at}` names a line, it overrides the
reading.** The owner has cut the ad to start there. That line is the opening beat:
the control is that line as the source says it, and everything before it is
gone from the ad — no version may lean on a beat that no longer plays. The
format below is found from THAT line, not from the source's first line. When
`{opening_at}` says `none`, the reading's opening beat stands.

**One brief, one discrete problem.** The reading and the baseline
pin the problem this asset solves. Every hook names that problem, in the
avatar's own dominant words for it — the words the research shows the avatar
uses most, not the category's default synonym. A strong research moment
that names a *different* problem is not a hook variation; it is a
different brief. Write no hook on it — route it to the BRIEF VARIATION
OPPORTUNITIES section, one line, and move on. Hooks that shift the
problem mid-set do not widen the test; they contaminate it.

**THE UNAWARE ENTRY — when the reading's entry level is `unaware`, the rule
above changes, and only then.** An unaware reader is not thinking about the
problem while they scroll. A hook that names it, shows it, or shows the tools
of its category is addressed to somebody who is not there yet — they scroll
past it as an ad for a thing they do not shop for. So at this entry level:

- **The problem still pins the brief — as the DESTINATION.** It is where the
  body arrives, a rung at a time. It is never in the opening. Do not name it,
  do not show it, do not point the camera at the part of the body or the
  room where it lives.
- **Every one of the six openings is a portrait.** It enters on the person:
  something they do, say, avoid, laugh at, or are proud of — a habit, a
  place, a ritual, a running joke among their people — that they would recognise
  as themselves in under a second, with nothing of ours in frame. The source is
  entertainment they already watch; the opening belongs to THAT world, and
  the teardown record's opening beat tells you what world it is.
- **What may not be in the line OR the frame of an opening:** the symptom,
  the word for it, a close-up of it, the products or tools of the category
  (ours or anyone's), a before/after, a count of things tried, a complaint
  about results. Any of these makes it a problem-aware hook wearing an
  unaware label.
- **"Every hook traces to a verbatim" still holds — pull from different
  rows.** Use what the avatar says about their life, their people, their routines
  and how they carry themselves. A row that is about the problem is not
  available to an opening at this level; if the bank offers nothing else, say
  so — that is a finding about the bank.
- **The variations still differ** — each enters through a different part of
  their life: five different moments, five different first frames, five
  different thumbnails. Five portraits of the same person, never one portrait
  with a word swapped.

**Version 0 is the straight swipe, and it is the control.**

Take the source's opening beat exactly as it is and change only the words
that must change — the brand, the product, the category. Nothing else.
Same sentence, same rhythm, same length, same punctuation. Its scroll
stopper is the source's own first frame, held.

This is the version that already performed, wearing our name. Every variation
is a bet against it in the same test, and if none of them beat it that is
a real finding rather than a failure. **Label it clearly as the control
and never improve it.**

Then write five variations. The rest of this prompt is about those.

Five rules govern what replaces the source's words:

**Every hook traces to a verbatim.** Pull the moment from the language bank
and the avatar — a behaviour, a time of day, an object, something done
with the hands. The strongest hooks name a private moment the avatar thought nobody
had noticed. A hook that cannot cite its source is invented and gets cut,
however well it reads. Reaching for a phrase that is not in the bank is the
signal you are writing at the avatar instead of from them.

**Finish a sentence the avatar has already started.** The bank holds
half-phrases they use about themselves. A line lands when it completes one they recognise
rather than introducing a new one.

**The line and the scroll stopper must be about the same thing.** A
strong line over an unrelated first frame kills the script in the first
second — the words say one thing, the picture says another, and the viewer
resolves it by scrolling. The stopper shows the thing the line names,
already happening.

**A variation is its own take — a different way into the same ad.** The
control carries the source's format word for word (THE FORMAT LOCK below);
the five variations do not repeat it with a word swapped. Each one opens the
same body from a different place: a different moment, a different line, a
different picture in the first frame, a different thumbnail. Two variations
that a viewer would read as the same opening are one variation. What every
take keeps from the format is its WORLD — the speaker, the register, the
reference the culture read names, the place the body continues in — so the
body that follows can carry any of the six.

**You know who this is for. Use it.** Triage named the avatar, the funnel and
the subject; the bank holds what that person actually says. An opening that
would work for anyone in this category has ignored all of it. The specific,
slightly uncomfortable, true thing is what stops a scroll — the safe, general
thing is what every competitor is already running.

**Dramatize.** The strongest opening is a real moment at its sharpest, not a
statement about a problem. Something done, hidden, caught, or said out loud
that people usually keep quiet. Discreet in what it claims; dramatic in what
it shows. A moment can be shown; a category cannot.

**The control's format is fixed; the takes are not.** The control is the
source's shape — see THE FORMAT LOCK. Every take is built from the language
rows and from the script's own context, never invented to be different.

What stays banned is the ad-shaped template the source did NOT use: "If you're struggling
with…", "If you're tired of…", "If you've ever…", "POV:", "Nobody talks
about…", "Here's why…", "The one thing…", "Stop scrolling if…". They signal
an ad in the first two words and none of them is how a person starts a
sentence. If the source opened on one of them, you may keep it — that is the
source's move and version 0 already carries it. Otherwise never introduce
one. The source's own format is not one of these — it is the swipe, and it is
kept. The test is the fills: if a hook's fills could be reused verbatim by a
different brand in a different category, they are generic — refill them from
the bank. **Print before writing:** what the source's opening was doing, in one
phrase, as the context you are working from.

**Match the speaker's own voice.** Sentence length, vocabulary, rhythm, and
any quirks the teardown record shows. If the speaker drops articles, the hook
drops articles. Where the source's shape leaves the choice open, a behaviour
beats a condition — a behaviour is how the avatar talks about their life, a
condition is how a brand talks about them.
Every hook must also stay inside the format's own register as the spec
records it — the body that follows is shared, so a hook whose register the
body could not follow is out of the set, however strong the line.

Write each hook in the format's own currency, per the reading: a
spoken line if the format speaks, a card if it does not, a frame if it has
neither.

Give me these four sections.

**1. THE FOUNDATIONAL HOOK**

Read from the spec, with citations: the opening phase and its bounds
(structural skeleton) · the opening element's mandate (element
inventory) · the premise and its job (script architecture) · the
awareness entry (the reading). Nothing here is your derivation.

Then print THE FORMAT, exactly as THE FORMAT LOCK below says — three labelled
lines, `FORMAT LINE:`, `FORMAT CARD:` and `FORMAT FRAME:`.

**2. VERSION 0 — THE CONTROL**

The source's opening, swiped straight, with only brand words changed. Show
the source line and ours side by side so the swap is visible, then its
scroll stopper — the source's own first frame, held. No citation
needed — it is the source's own line. Then its labelled lines, exactly as
THE FORMAT LOCK says: `TAKE V0: control` · `LINE V0:` · `CARD V0:` (the
source's own card, or `none`) · `SCROLL V0:` (the source's own first frame) ·
`THUMB V0:` (the source's own cover frame and words, or its first frame).

**3. THE FIVE VARIATIONS**

For each, five labelled lines, one per line and nothing else on the line:
`TAKE Vn:` — the angle, in a few words (the moment of their life it enters
through) · `LINE Vn:` — what is said, in the speaker's voice, on one line ·
`CARD Vn:` — the words on screen in the first second, never `none` on a
variation unless the opening is wordless and has no card (lines separated by " / ") · `SCROLL Vn:` — **the first-frame event,
a thing happening, concrete enough to shoot from the sentence alone, and not
the control's frame with one detail changed** · `THUMB Vn:` — the thumbnail:
the one still, what is in it and where, and its words. Then: every specific
in the take and the verbatim it came from **quoted inside its original full
sentence**, with its source · what it assumes about the speaker · how the
take hands over to the body's next beat.

Quoting the whole original sentence is not decoration. A fragment can be
cited accurately and still be used to mean something the customer never
said — showing the sentence it lived in makes that visible on the page.

That last one matters. If the speaker cannot truthfully say it, the hook is
unusable no matter how good it is. Flag it plainly.

**4. BRIEF VARIATION OPPORTUNITIES**

The strong seams you did NOT hook because they name a different problem
than this brief's. One line each: the seam · its verbatim · why it is a
different brief rather than a hook. No script lines, no development —
these are notes for a future teardown or research pass, not work product.
If there were none, say none.

Write no other part of the script.

---

## THE OPENING, READ FIRST (v23)

Before you print a format, read what the swipe's opening actually is. Look at
the first three seconds in the verbatim transcript, the frame map and the
layer sheet, and print one line:

`OPENING: spoken` — a person says real words in the first three seconds.
`OPENING: card` — no one speaks, but words are on screen in the first second.
`OPENING: wordless` — no real words are said and none are on screen. Music, a
sound effect, a sigh, a "Hmm.", laughter, or background chatter the
transcript flags as heard differently all count as no words.

Then one line on **what stops the scroll** in the source: the first picture,
the first motion, the sound, the gag. That is the thing every version has to
match in strength, whatever the opening is.

**A wordless opening is fine. It is the format, not a gap.** Do not invent a
spoken line, do not turn a sound into a sentence, and do not put a caption on
a swipe that has none unless the variation needs one to stop the scroll.
For a wordless opening:

- `FORMAT LINE: none` and `FORMAT CARD: none` (or the source's card, if it
  has one). No blanks, so no ELEMENTS lines for the line.
- `LINE V0` … `LINE V5` all print `none`. `CARD Vn` prints `none` when the
  swipe has no card, or its own words when the swipe has one.
- **The take lives in `SCROLL` and `THUMB`.** Every one of the five is a
  different first-two-seconds event: a different picture, a different first
  motion, a different thing caught on camera, or a different sound cue. Five
  scroll stoppers that are the same shot with one prop changed are one take,
  and the machine holds them.
- Build each scroll stopper from the avatar's own moments and words, the same
  way you would build a spoken line: quote the full sentence it came from.

For a spoken or card opening, everything below applies as before.

---

## THE FORMAT LOCK

The source's opening worked for a reason, and the reason is its shape. This
pass does not reinvent it. It finds the shape, names its blanks, and fills
them.

**1 · Find the format — from the source's own words.** Take the source's
opening line exactly as the teardown record's verbatim transcript has it (the
line the reading names). Keep every word as it is, and put a named blank in
`[CAPITALS]` over only the words that carry something the brief changes — the
place, the problem or desire, the person's own specifics (an age, a name, a
count), the product, the category. The spec's script architecture already
names these blanks for the opening beat; use its names. **Never add a blank
the spec did not name, and never rewrite a fixed word** — not even to the
spec's own paraphrase of it. **A blank covers only the words that change**: a
word every version keeps (the noun after a verb that changes, say) is fixed,
outside the blank. Print:

`FORMAT LINE: <the source's line, word for word, with its blanks>`

Do the same for the opening's hook card (the words on screen in the first
beat, lines separated by " / "), and for the first frame (the teardown's
description of it: camera, place, what is happening — the variable parts in
blanks):

`FORMAT CARD: <the source's card, word for word, with its blanks>`
`FORMAT FRAME: <the source's first frame, with its blanks>`

If the source has no card, print `FORMAT CARD: none`, and every `CARD` line
says `none`.

**2 · The control fills the blanks with the swap and nothing else.** Version
0 is the format with each blank filled by what the rewrite already put there
— the baseline injection's opening beat. That is the swipe wearing our name.

**3 · The format binds the control; the takes keep its world.** Only VERSION
0 is held to the fixed words. The five variations are five takes (section 3):
each keeps the format's WORLD — the speaker, the register, the reference, the
place the body continues in — and every specific in it comes from the
language bank, the avatar, the brand files or the cast, cited in its full
original sentence. **Across the five, no two share a take, a first-frame
event or a thumbnail.**

**4 · Every blank is a named element, and elements hold each other.** A
blank is not free-standing: it is an element of this script, and the script's
other elements depend on it. Name every element the opening touches — the ones
in its blanks AND the ones the body brings in that a blank must agree with (a
person the body puts in a place, a count the card states, a thing the body
treats). Then write, for each blank, what it must hold, **in element names
only**: `[PLACE] holds [EXPERT] — the body puts [EXPERT] with [SUBJECT] inside
it` · `[AGE] = [SUBJECT]'s age in the cast` · `[PROBLEM] = the problem the body
treats`. A fill is right only when every element it must hold is true of it:
a place where [EXPERT] could not be is wrong however good it sounds. The
source's own fill shows the kind: keep that kind, change the instance.
Print, above the variations:

`ELEMENTS:` — one line per element: `[NAME] · what it is in this script ·
what it must hold or match (other element names) · where its fill comes from
(bank · cast · brand file · research)`

These names are the injection points. Every later pass, and every future run
of this format, fills the same elements.

**5 · A blank the files cannot fill is researched, not invented.** When a
fill needs a fact that is not on file — a real place name, a real number, a
real price — write `[RESEARCH: what is needed, in a few words]` in that
blank and move on — naming what the element must hold, so the research looks
for the right kind of thing (not "a real [PLACE] nearby" but "a real [PLACE]
nearby that holds [EXPERT], as the ELEMENTS line says"). The machine researches every one of those after this pass,
from the brand's files and then the web, and writes the fact in with its
receipt. A fill you made up to avoid a research marker is the defect this
rule exists for.

**6 · The scroll stopper and the thumbnail.** The control's first frame is
the source's, held. Each take's first frame is its own event, showing what
its line says, already happening — inside the format's world (the same room,
the same people) but a different thing happening in it. Each take's thumbnail
is its own still, chosen to make that take's promise in the grid; never the
same frame as another take's.

**What this lock does not touch:** the tests below still apply to what goes
into the blanks — the rung, the stage, the story, the portrait rule at an
unaware entry. A fill that breaks one is replaced with a fill that does not;
the format is never broken to pass a test. If a test cannot be passed by any
fill, print that as a flag about the format against our market and leave
the format standing.

**Print the lines the machine reads, exactly this way, one per line and
nothing else on the line:** `FORMAT LINE:` · `FORMAT CARD:` · `FORMAT FRAME:`
in section 1; `TAKE Vn:` · `LINE Vn:` · `CARD Vn:` · `SCROLL Vn:` · `THUMB Vn:`
for V0 to V5 in sections 2 and 3. The machine checks `LINE V0` and `CARD V0`
against their `FORMAT` (every fixed word present, in order), checks every
variation printed all five slots, and holds any variation whose line, card,
scroll stopper or thumbnail reads as another version's with words swapped.

---

## EXAMPLES ARE EXAMPLES

Every specific in this prompt — an age, a gender, a garment, a body part, a
product category, a named person — is **illustrative only**. None of it
describes the asset in front of you. Read an example for the shape of the rule,
then apply the rule to what you actually have.

The subject may be any age, any gender, anywhere, selling anything. This chain
runs the same on one person on a beach, someone talking to camera in a parked
car, two people in a kitchen, or a pair of hands on a countertop. Nothing in
this prompt assumes which.

**Who is on camera comes from the teardown record, never from this prompt.** If
the teardown says a person in their fifties in a parked car, that is the
subject — every rule below applies to them unchanged, and any pronoun in an
example above is about that example, not about the person in front of you.

**And the variables are agnostic too.** An example may be reworked freely — it
is illustrative and always was. But nothing that gets filled in at run time
may name a brand, a product or a category: brand detail arrives only through
the variables above, resolved from that brand's own folder. A rule that
cannot be written without naming the brand belongs in the brand folder, not
in this prompt. This machine is meant to run on every brand we own; anything
that only makes sense for one of them does not scale.
