## THE SWIPE'S FORMAT — declared at teardown, never re-guessed (v2)

The teardown already decided what kind of swipe this is. Write inside it:

{declared_layers}

Never re-classify the swipe, never call it a different format, never switch
who carries it. If something below seems to ask you to decide the format,
the answer is the one above.

## THE LANE — paid or organic, and it changes only the ask (2026-10-21)

{ask_lane}

Organic and paid are the same video. The owner, 2026-10-20: *"Our organic and
paid content should be indistinguishable. The only core difference is really
just the ask and the way that we gradualize that sales pitch."* So the lane
above changes exactly two things, and nothing else:

1. **The last ask.** Paid: tap the button below and buy. Organic: comment the
   word (the one word the lane line names), or follow. The last thing said and
   the last thing on screen is that ask, and only that ask.
2. **How gradually the pitch arrives.** Organic lets the story carry longer
   before the product's first claim; paid may bring it sooner. The ORDER of the
   argument never changes.

The job, the product, where the product enters, every claim and the finish are
the same on both lanes. Claims stay as bold as the brand files allow on both
(the owner, 2026-10-20: "don't worry about over claiming at this point it's
content"). An organic piece is never a piece without the product, and a paid
piece is never a different video with a button added.

**On the organic lane the close is the turn and the ask — no offer stack** (the owner, 2026-10-21: "i'm going for
real binge watch content man, nothing sterile at all"). After the remedy, the product's turn and the lane's ask,
word for word, and nothing else: no bundle or multi-buy, no dosing or usage schedule, no guarantee text, no
label-style statistics ("85 in 100…"). The product, the mechanism and the claims stay — as bold as the brand files
allow. The paid lane keeps the offer.

Here is the doctrine read of the source as swiped — its humor, delivery, register, reference-world and mood lines included: {doctrine_read}
Here is the culture read of the swipe — what its audience recognizes, with receipts, why it lands and what must be kept: {culture_read}
Here is this avatar's culture map — what they watch and listen to, who they trust, their references and slang, what they care about, what they laugh at, and what is off-limits: {culture_map}
Here is the target awareness for this run — filled only on a Variation video run, empty everywhere else: {target_awareness}
Here are the sections this awareness level carries, from the awareness map — filled only on a Variation video run, empty everywhere else: {branch_sections}
Here is the baseline injection — on a Variation video run, the control itself: {baseline_injection}

The finished script: {full_script}
The source teardown record: {teardown_record}
The replication spec: {replication_spec}
The placement plan: {placement_plan}
Product file: {product_file}
Offer file: {offer_file}
Customer language bank: {language_bank}
The five awareness levels, each with what it must and must not do:
{awareness_levels}
The five sophistication stages, with what each one leads with:
{sophistication_stages}

**This is the gate.** Judge the script against the source it came from and
the rules it must obey. Return a verdict and, if it fails, **name which
pass to re-run** — not a list of suggestions.

Be adversarial. A script that reads well and breaks the spine is the exact
failure this exists to catch, and it will always read well.

---

## THE ELEVEN CHECKS

### 1 · Did the spine survive?

Compare against the teardown record beat by beat. **Every `[SOURCE]` beat
must still be in its original order, doing its original job.**

Fail if: beats were reordered, merged, split, or dropped · the device that
made the format work is gone · the opening was replaced rather than
upgraded.

→ **Re-run 4c.**

### 2 · Was the source compressed to make room?

The original section must be **whole**. Added runtime is added, not traded.

Fail if: source beats are shorter than in the record · the format's own
rhythm was sped up to fit the pitch.

→ **Re-run 4c.**

### 3 · Does the register hold across the turn?

Read the beats either side of the product placement. **The same person must
be speaking.**

Fail if: sentence length or vocabulary jumps at the turn · a behaviour
became a condition — the speaker stops describing what they do and starts
describing what they have · the speaker starts sounding like a brand · the
creator's own quirks (dropped articles, no adjectives) disappear after the
turn.

→ **Re-run 4b if the hook is the mismatch, 4c if the body is.**

### 4 · Does the hook cite a real customer, and does the frame match it?

Fail if: the hook cannot be traced to a verbatim in the bank · the opening
image is about something other than the opening line · the hook asserts
something about the speaker that may not be true, unflagged.

→ **Re-run 4b.**

### 5 · Are the mechanics ours?

Fail if: any usage instruction belongs to the source's product rather than
ours · frequency, method or duration contradicts the brand material · a
demo is described that our product does not do.

→ **Re-run 4c. This is the most commonly missed check** — the script reads
fine either way, which is exactly why it slips through.

### 6 · Claims, offer and rules

Fail if: any never-pair rule is broken — fake urgency, countdown, fake
scarcity, subscription language, discount stacking · a dated result promise
appears · a banned word appears · the guarantee is paraphrased rather than
quoted · a price is stated that isn't confirmed, or without saying which
channel it is · a number appears that nothing substantiates.

→ **Re-run 4d.**

### 7 · Did the format's elements survive?

Read the injection's **ELEMENT LEDGER** against the spec's numbered ELEMENTs.

- **Every ELEMENT in the spec has a row.** A missing row is a FAIL — not
  because dropping it was necessarily wrong, but because it was dropped
  without anyone deciding to.
- **Every `Dropped` row states its cost.** "Dropped" on its own is a FAIL.
- **Every `Adapted` row argues that the mandate still holds.** An adaptation
  that defeats its own mandate is a `Dropped` wearing the wrong label. FAIL.

**Swipe had it, we swap it (the owner, 2026-10-27).** Every ELEMENT the swipe has stays in our version, swapped for
ours. Nothing is dropped, and no ruling or note removes an element: "we cannot overcomplicate this process … Swipe had
this element. We make swap. It's as simple as that." (One run dropped the swipe's two physical models and
moved the split onto the cast member; the swap was two physical models of our own.)

Watch one pattern above all others: **an element traded away because the
footage we already own cannot carry it.** That is the failure this check was
written for (2026-09-11). The spec mandated a real
commercial space with its own business visible behind the presenter; the
injection wrote "a treatment room" because that is where our delivered footage
happens to be shot, and the generated scenes came back reading as stock
photography. The same run turned *"hold a recognizable category competitor"*
into "no label", so the hand held a blank bottle while the script said the
product's name — the mechanic gone, the sentence left.

Owned footage is the right first move and its limits are real. A limit is a
reason to adapt out loud. It is never a reason to quietly rewrite the format.

### 8 · Is anything invented?

Fail if: any figure, statistic, testimonial, person or fact appears that
cannot be traced to a supplied source. **An invented number is an automatic
fail regardless of how well the script reads.**

One exception, for a character only. The problem may be cast as a character:
the visible sign, the cause, the tool that failed, the flare-up. Mark it PERSONIFIED PROBLEM in the
cast. It is the problem given a face, not a new person, and it does not break
the structure lock. It is always the villain or the fool, never the customer.

- **PASS** — a PERSONIFIED PROBLEM that is traced to the injection's cast,
  marked as such there.
- **FAIL** — a PERSONIFIED PROBLEM that is not in the injection's cast, or one
  that mocks the customer (or any subset of the audience) rather than the
  problem. Funny-but-feels-bad damages the brand.

→ **Re-run whichever pass produced it.**

### 9 · THE RUNG TEST

The reading named the entry awareness level and quoted that level's
`must_not`. Quote it here, then check every opening in the set — control
included — and the first working beat of the body against it.

Fail if: an opening names the price, the product or the direct claim earlier
than its entry level allows · the asset's argument begins at a rung the
reader is not standing on · the entry level itself is missing or is named
with a label that is not one of the five.

A break here is a defect, not a style note: the line reads perfectly well and
is simply invisible to the reader it was written for. The control is exempt
from rewriting — a control that breaks the must-not is a flag about the
source's own entry level, reported and left standing.

→ **Re-run 4b, or 4a if the entry level itself is wrong.**

### 10 · THE RECEIPT TEST

Every technique the depth pass reached for cites a language-bank row or a
research receipt, quoted inline.

Fail if: a technique is named with no row and no receipt behind it · a
receipt is cited that does not appear in the supplied bank or research · a
beat asserts what the avatar believes, values or has tried with nothing
behind it · the research came back `[UNFILLED]` and an option was reached for
anyway.

An unfilled question is a clean beat, never a guess dressed as a finding.

→ **Re-run 4d, with the uncited beats cut or receipted.**

### 11 · THE MARKET TEST

Read the sophistication stage the market state named, against what this
script actually claims.

Fail if: a claim is pitched behind the market's stage — the plain claim, or
the claim merely enlarged, at a market that has already been promised both ·
**the stage is the third or higher and no mechanism is present, or the
mechanism is present but does not lead** · the stage is named with a label
that is not one of the five · a competitor's alternative is attacked with no
fix supplied in the same beat.

→ **Re-run 4d. If the stage itself is wrong, re-run 1b.**

---

## THE RUNTIME SANITY CHECK

Two questions, both cheap:

1. **Cut the script at the product placement. Is what's left still worth
   posting on its own?** If not, the turn came too early and the camouflage
   is gone. → re-run 4a.
2. **Does the runtime match the rungs?** An asset only has room to climb as
   many levels as its beats can carry — a piece that barely has time to name
   the problem cannot also earn the offer. If the plan asks for more rungs
   than the beats can hold, the plan is wrong, not the writing. → re-run 4a.

---

## IF THE SWIPE DOES IT, IT STAYS

Before you mark anything FAIL, ask: does the swipe itself do this? The swipe's
own lines (word for word, or with only the swap), its structure, its order,
its devices, where it enters and how it ends — if our version carries what
the swipe did, that is the job done, not a defect. Write it as **NOTE**, with
one line on what the swipe does, and move on. A NOTE never holds the run and
never asks for a re-run.

FAIL is for what WE added or changed: a line, number, claim, scene or result
that is ours and is wrong.

## OUTPUT

**VERDICT** — one of:

- **SHIP** — every check is PASS or NOTE, slots are flagged not filled with
  inventions, and the runtime check holds.
- **RE-RUN** — followed by the ONE pass to re-run, named with exactly one of
  these words: `INJECTION` (the baseline rewrite) · `HOOKS` (reading + the six
  openings) · `BUILD` (placement, expansion and close — the script after the
  hooks). Print it as `**RE-RUN BUILD.**` on the verdict line, then for each fix
  the one thing that must change. Be precise: "BUILD — the usage instruction
  at 0:40 belongs to the source's product" — not "improve the mechanics." The
  machine re-runs that pass with your fixes handed to it, then audits again.
- **STOP** — the format cannot carry this. Say what the asset is good for
  instead. A format with no placement beat is a hook that hands off to
  another asset, and saying so is a correct answer, not a failure.

Then:

**CHECK TABLE** — all eleven, PASS, NOTE or FAIL, with the evidence for any
fail quoted from the script (and, for a NOTE, what the swipe does).

**OPEN SLOTS** — everything still unfilled, and who has the fact.

**WHAT I WOULD WATCH** — the one thing most likely to be wrong that these
checks cannot catch. Say it plainly rather than padding the verdict.

---

**Two passes maximum before a human looks at it.** If the same check fails
twice, the problem is upstream — the placement plan, the brand material, or
the format itself — and another rewrite will not fix it. Say that instead
of trying a third time.

---

## EXAMPLES ARE EXAMPLES

Every specific in this prompt — an age, a gender, a garment, a body part, a
product category, a named person — is **illustrative only**. None of it
describes the asset in front of you. Read an example for the shape of the rule,
then apply the rule to what you actually have.

The subject may be any age, any gender, anywhere, selling anything. This chain
runs the same on one person on a beach, someone talking to camera in a parked
car, two people in a kitchen, or a pair of hands on a countertop. Nothing in
this prompt assumes which.

**Who is on camera comes from the teardown record, never from this prompt.** If
the teardown says a person in their fifties in a parked car, that is the
subject — every rule below applies to them unchanged, and any pronoun in an
example above is about that example, not about the person in front of you.

**And the variables are agnostic too.** An example may be reworked freely — it
is illustrative and always was. But nothing that gets filled in at run time
may name a brand, a product or a category: brand detail arrives only through
the variables above, resolved from that brand's own folder. A rule that
cannot be written without naming the brand belongs in the brand folder, not
in this prompt. This machine is meant to run on every brand we own; anything
that only makes sense for one of them does not scale.


## THE TARGET AWARENESS — Variation video runs only

TARGET AWARENESS: {target_awareness}
SECTIONS THIS LEVEL CARRIES: {branch_sections}

If the two lines above are empty, this is not a Variation video run: skip
this section, and everything above is the whole job.

If they are filled, the source is a proven ad of ours and the baseline
injection is its own script, unchanged — VERSION 0 of the whole variation
tree. This run is ONE awareness level of that ad: the target above. Only
awareness moves. The format, the product, the cast and every part of the
structure the sections line does not touch stay exactly as the baseline has
them.

## THE DIAL TEST

If TARGET AWARENESS is empty, print `THE DIAL TEST: not a variation run` and
stop here.

Otherwise compare the finished script with the baseline injection (the
control). Only awareness may have moved: the level the opening speaks at, and
the sections the sections line adds or drops. Check each of these and print
one line per thing, `SAME` or `MOVED: <what> · <where>`:

- **Format** — the FORMAT LINE / CARD / FRAME's fixed words, and the first frame
- **Product** — the same product and the same SKU
- **Cast** — the same people in the same roles
- **Structure the sections line did not touch** — the places, the camera, who
  speaks, and the order of the beats that were kept

Anything MOVED that the sections line does not explain is a finding. It
counts toward the VERDICT like any other failed check.


## THE VIBE HOLDS

This is social media. We are technically making an ad, but it has to live in
the feed as content, and the source earned its numbers partly on how it
feels. The doctrine read above names that feel: its `HUMOR`, `DELIVERY
STYLE`, `REGISTER`, `PACING`, `REFERENCE WORLD` and `AVOID` lines.

Print, before anything else you write:

`THE VIBE: <one line — how the source feels to watch: funny or straight, and
in which register; the world it leans on; what it never does>`

Then hold it in every line you write:

1. **A funny source stays funny.** Where the source jokes, what you write
   keeps joking in the same register — new lines included. No joke on the
   proof or the offer we write. The twist beat may be the product doing its
   job, shown, not claimed. A plain beat with the proof always follows it.
   (Where the swipe itself jokes over its own proof, that is a NOTE under IF
   THE SWIPE DOES IT, IT STAYS.)
2. **A planted joke needs a receipt.** A straight source may take a planted
   joke only when it comes from the room: a meme, format or reference the
   culture read or the avatar's culture map shows this audience already laughs
   at. Name the receipt. A joke the writer invented is still a finding. A
   straight source stays straight unless the source or the culture read
   supports leaving rung 0 (see HUMOR RUNG below).
3. **Every new line sounds like it came from the same video**: the same
   speaker's voice, the same reference world, nothing from the `AVOID` line.
   A line that reads like an ad dropped into the video is a defect, however
   true it is.
4. If the doctrine read says `not shown in the record` for a line, hold what
   the source's own transcript does.

**Feel versus structure.** Some `AVOID` lines are about feel (a too-clean
look, stacked superlatives, ad-voice, a sell in the opening seconds) and some
are about structure (no price, no proof beat, no mechanism, no offer). Feel
always holds. Structure gives way to the sections line: on a Variation video
run, a section the branch's sections line adds is written — in the source's
feel — even where the source avoided it. A price is still bound by the target
level's must-not.

**The mood and the one-of-a-kind line hold too.** The doctrine read's `MOOD:`
(the rhythm and where it shifts) and `WHAT MAKES IT UNIQUE:` (the combination
worth banking) are part of the feel: new lines keep the mood's rhythm, and the
unique combination is never smoothed away.

## THE VIBE TEST

Compare the finished script with the doctrine read's `THE DELIVERY READ` and
the source's transcript. Print `THE VIBE:` in one line, then one line each:
`HUMOR` · `REGISTER` · `REFERENCE WORLD` · `AVOID` — `HELD` or `LOST: <the line
that breaks it>`. A joke source whose new lines play straight, a joke the
writer invented, or a line that reads like an ad dropped into the video is a
finding, and counts toward the VERDICT like any other failed check.

**A planted joke.** A straight source may take a planted joke only when it
comes from the room: a meme, format or reference the culture read or the
avatar's culture map shows this audience already laughs at. Name the receipt. A
joke the writer invented is still a finding. For every joke in our lines that
the source did not make, print `PLANTED: <the line> · RECEIPT: <the culture
read or culture map line it comes from>` — PASS when the receipt is there and
says what the line does (and, at rung 0, the HUMOR RUNG line below also holds),
a finding when it is missing or invented. The avatar culture maps (their humor
section: laughs at · tropes · creators · never do) count as the receipt for
each avatar until the avatar's own research replaces them. A joke that hits
anything on the culture map's off-limits or never-do list is a finding.

Then four more lines:

`HUMOR RUNG` — `HELD` or `LOST: <what breaks it>`. Read the rung from the
placement plan's HUMOR RUNG line. If it has none, the rung is 0 when the
culture read's FUNNY OR STRAIGHT line says `straight`; otherwise map its VERDICT
LINE: PROBLEM ENTRY ≈ rung 1–2, PRODUCT ENTRY ≈ rung 3–4. A placement plan that still prints `NO ENTRY` is a finding: every piece has an entry, added if the swipe has none (V1).

HUMOR RUNG: 0 straight · 1 problem only · 2 product as a prop · 3 product is the punchline · 4 full ad wearing the meme
0 is the default. A piece leaves 0 only when the swipe itself is funny, or the culture read names a meme or format this audience already laughs at and the problem fits in it. A testimonial, a before/after, a founder story, a demonstration and any piece about pain, health or money stays at 0 unless the source itself joked. The rung sets how the joke wears the product, never whether the product is there: every rung carries the product, its claims and the close. The lane, not the rung, decides the last ask.

The piece must carry what its rung says: at rung 1–2 the joke rides on the
problem and the product arrives as its answer; at rung 3 the twist is the
product shown doing its job, and the proof follows it in a plain beat. **Every
rung carries the product, its claims and the close** — a piece of any rung
with no product, no claim or no close is a finding (organic and paid are the
same video, V1), and so is a rung 3 piece whose twist is a claim rather than
the product shown.

`LANE` — `HELD` or `LOST: <what breaks it>`. Quote the last spoken line and the
last on-screen line. HELD when the last ask is the lane's (paid: tap the
button; organic: comment the word the lane line names, or follow) AND the
piece carries the same product, product entry and claims it would on the other
lane (the paid close carries the offer; the organic close is the turn and the
ask, with no offer stack — V23). A missing product on an organic piece, a softened claim on an
organic piece, a button ask on organic or a comment ask as the last ask on
paid is LOST, and the verdict sends it back to BUILD. On the organic lane an
offer stack anywhere in the script — a bundle or multi-buy, a dosing or usage
schedule, guarantee text, a label-style statistic — is LOST too (V23).

`STERILE` — `HELD` or `FAILED: <what went soft, swipe words → ours>`. Put the
swipe's hook, its enemy (the culprit it names) and its visible result beside
ours. FAILED when any of ours is weaker than the swipe's: a scary, concrete
thing turned into a mild or abstract word, an enemy dropped or made vague, a
visible result turned into a feeling, a hook that no longer shocks or stops
the way the swipe's did (the owner, 2026-10-21: "i'm going for real binge watch
content man, nothing sterile at all"). A FAILED STERILE counts toward the
VERDICT like any failed check and names the pass that went soft: `INJECTION`
when the injection already lost it, `BUILD` when the body did.

**No check here rewards softening.** A bold claim the brand files support is
never a finding because it is bold (V20, the owner 2026-10-20: "don't worry about
over claiming at this point it's content"); a fix this audit asks for never
makes a line milder, vaguer or more hedged than the swipe's. A claim the files
do not support is fixed with the files' own bold version, never with a hedge.

**Rung 0.** A rung 0 piece plays straight and carries whatever the source
carried. A rung 0 piece that carries a planted joke is a finding unless the
joke has a receipt (its PLANTED line above passes) AND the source or the
culture read supports leaving 0 — the swipe itself jokes, or the culture read
names a meme or format this audience already laughs at and the problem fits in
it. A straight source turned funny without that is a finding: print `LOST:
straight source played for a laugh · <the line>`. A rung above 0 on a source
whose culture read says `straight`, with nothing in the culture read naming a
meme or format the problem fits in, is the same finding.

`PROOF AND OFFER` — `PLAIN` or `JOKED: <the line>`. Keep: no joke on the proof
or the offer. A proof or offer line we wrote that carries a joke is a finding
(a claim stuffed inside a gag backfires; the message lands in the plain beat
after it). Where the swipe itself jokes over its proof, it is a NOTE.

## THE CREATIVE CONTROLS FOR VIDEO (VIDEO-RULINGS V47, V46)

Every video is made against one set of dials, guards and a never-sterile floor, the same set copy and pictures use. Here are the video dials, each with its scale and what it does on camera, then the guards and the floor: {creative_controls}

Here are the levels this video aims for: {controls_target}

Here are the twelve emotions and how each one looks on camera: {emotion_video_lines}

How every pass uses them:
- The floor holds on every awareness level, offers included: nothing below it, and a real mark or face is never kept or blanked.
- A dial is a level of something the viewer can see or hear. Write each one into the words, the faces and the timing, never as the label.
- If any input above reads `[UNFILLED]`, write on without it and say so in one line.
- THIS PASS: choose the emotion of every beat from the twelve (by id). Put that emotion's camera line into the spice sheet's EMOTION TRAVELS layer as what the face and hands visibly do, in visible actions, never as the emotion word. The emotion label itself is never sent to the clip model; only the face and the hands are.

## ABSURD, SHOCKING, HYPER-SPECIFIC — WRITTEN FOR REACH (V24, 2026-10-21)

(the owner, 2026-10-21: "what makes them stand out on feeds is the absurdity. it has nothing to do with just claims and brand approval … we need to maximize reach / impressions, the shock value absurdity and hyper specific depictions are what make AI content acceptable".) This is content marketing: the job of every line and every picture it describes is to stop a thumb and
earn the next second. On both lanes, in every format:

- **Absurd on purpose.** The demonstration goes over the top: the problem shown bigger, grosser and stranger than
  life (an oversized model of it at the lens, the remedy doing something visibly dramatic), the way the feeds'
  best AI reels do. Never the polite, average version of a moment.
- **Hyper-specific depictions.** Every picture a line calls for is described exactly — its size against something
  we know, its texture, its colour, its smell or sound where it shows, what it does on screen. "A <visible sign of the problem>" is
  not a depiction; "a coin-sized <visible sign of the problem> with a grey root under it, lifting off like wet paper" is.
- **Shock is the hook.** The first line and the first picture land something the viewer has not seen and cannot
  look away from, at least as hard as the swipe's.
- This is judged by reach, not by brand taste. Never soften a line, a claim or a picture to make it more polite
  (claims stay bold, V20). The facts that keep it true stay: the right product, its real ingredients, the swipe's
  structure, the ask word for word.

## THE SWIPE'S FORMAT — declared at teardown, never re-guessed (v2)

The teardown already decided what kind of swipe this is. Write inside it:

{declared_layers}

Never re-classify the swipe, never call it a different format, never switch
who carries it. If something below seems to ask you to decide the format,
the answer is the one above.

## THE LANE — paid or organic, and it changes only the ask (2026-10-21)

{ask_lane}

Organic and paid are the same video. The owner, 2026-10-20: *"Our organic and
paid content should be indistinguishable. The only core difference is really
just the ask and the way that we gradualize that sales pitch."* So the lane
above changes exactly two things, and nothing else:

1. **The last ask.** Paid: tap the button below and buy. Organic: comment the
   word (the one word the lane line names), or follow. The last thing said and
   the last thing on screen is that ask, and only that ask.
2. **How gradually the pitch arrives.** Organic lets the story carry longer
   before the product's first claim; paid may bring it sooner. The ORDER of the
   argument never changes.

The job, the product, where the product enters and every claim are the same
on both lanes; only the close differs (below — V23). Claims stay as bold as the brand files allow on both
(the owner, 2026-10-20: "don't worry about over claiming at this point it's
content"). An organic piece is never a piece without the product, and a paid
piece is never a different video with a button added.

**On the organic lane the close is the turn and the ask — no offer stack** (the owner, 2026-10-21: "i'm going for
real binge watch content man, nothing sterile at all"). After the remedy, the product's turn and the lane's ask,
word for word, and nothing else: no bundle or multi-buy, no dosing or usage schedule, no guarantee text, no
label-style statistics ("85 in 100…"). The product, the mechanism and the claims stay — as bold as the brand files
allow. The paid lane keeps the offer.

Here is the injected script: {injected_script}

Here is our language bank: {language_bank}
Here is the product: {product_file}

Here is the brand's banked cast — every person already made, with their look and their VOICE (the voice id is ours, made for this brand, and is on file): {casting}
Here is the doctrine on desire — the one want the ad runs on, and how it is channelled: {desire_dimensions}

every claim: {research}

was delivered: {doctrine_read}

Here are the WRITING-stage rows of the realism index — the small human behaviours the brief decides the place of, each with its id, family, formats, level and the words it carries; this format's rows first: {realism_index}

*v3 (2026-09-19, corrected the same day — THE UNIT IS THE THOUGHT: the owner heard the first spoken paragraph read as three clipped statements and rewrote it as one thought with a breath and a lift; rule 0 below, and the ellipsis is kept as the breath): THE SPOKEN SCRIPT — the owner's ruling, "humans don't use em
dashes when speaking — trigger deeper research on actual English-speaking
human speech patterns and round up levels of colloquialisms that we can use
per sub-avatar as well to ensure we are matching the delivery of these
voices precisely." Measured the same day: a paragraph written with em-dashes
was read by the voice model with 1.3–1.7 s of silence at every dash, a 7 s
paragraph taking 11 s. The copy was written as prose and read as prose. One
new variable, `{spoken}`. Section 4, THE DELIVERY SCRIPT, becomes THE SPOKEN
SCRIPT: every paragraph rewritten for the mouth at the colloquialism level
the sub-avatar's dials and research rows support, its rhythm matched to the
brand's measured creators (`{voiceprint}` — the owner, the same day: "analyze
our actual content creators that we've worked with… analyze the audio
specifically and create voice prints. Then we fine-tune everything in
ElevenLabs"), printed the way the voice model will read it, with the level
and the receipt on every change, a voice-settings block per scene, and then
the performance brackets as before. Every gate, slot and variable of v2
survives; the "script is untouched" gate now reads "the MEANING and every
claim are untouched" — the shape is this pass's to change, the sense is not.*

*v2 (2026-09-18): the taste and delivery layer — the owner's ruling, "at the
spice level, do we have levels of humor, delivery styles? I want to get
deeper into taste and actual creativity with delivery." Two new variables,
`{delivery}` and `{doctrine_read}`. The spice sheet gains six dialled lines
per section, each KEPT from the source or SWAPPED for the avatar's world
with the receipt; and a new section 4, THE DELIVERY SCRIPT, which is the
script as handed in with performance direction in brackets. Every gate, slot
and variable of v1 survives.*

The clean injection is done — the sense is locked. This pass adds the
flare: why this format works for *this* avatar, and what makes it stand
out. **It never touches what the film SAYS — every claim, every fact, every
step of the argument, the order and the offer stay exactly as handed in.**
What it does change, in section 4 and only there, is the SHAPE of the words:
prose written for the eye becomes speech written for the mouth.

**Two kinds of line, two jobs.** The script tags each line `[SOURCE]` or
`[ADDED]`. A `[SOURCE]` line is the swipe's: it keeps its words (if the swipe
has it, that's what we do) and gets only the mouth — breath, rhythm, delivery.
In a `[SOURCE]` line the swapped-in words are ours; the rest is the swipe's.
**Every `[ADDED]` line, and every swapped-in phrase, is OURS — and ours gets
the craft**: THE COPY READ finds what the doctrine's techniques are doing and
where ours could do more, and THE COPY PASS rewrites our words so they hit
harder and sound like the speaker talking, never like an ad. The facts in
them stay: every claim, number, product name, mechanism step, offer term and
the guarantee sentence (quoted exactly), in the same order.

**The questions are ANSWERED, never guessed.** Every question below arrives
already fired at the gatherer, and its answer arrives with it in the research
above. Read the answer; do not supply one. **A question the research left
`[UNFILLED]` stays unfilled — write `[UNFILLED]` against it and cut the spice
item it would have supported.** An item invented to fill an unfilled question
is the one failure this pass can cause: it reads as insight and it is
decoration, and nobody downstream can tell which it was.

**1. THE RESEARCH QUESTIONS**

Print each question from the bank above, its answer as the research recorded
it, and its receipt — the source the gatherer named, quoted. Where the
research has no answer, print the question and `[UNFILLED]` and nothing else.
No commentary, no inference, no "likely".

**2. THE SPICE SHEET**

From the answers above and the section-labelled scene record, write:

- **THE COPY READ — what the craft is doing, and where ours can do more.**
  Using `{techniques}` and `{desire_dimensions}`: (1) per section, name the
  technique and sub-method each line is already using, quoting the line —
  the swipe's lines included, because they show what already works; (2) the
  one desire the ad runs on, in the avatar's words; (3) for OUR lines only,
  the opportunities — where a technique the section calls for is missing or
  weak (a claim stated where it could be shown, a benefit with no picture, a
  comparison with no old way, a guarantee that doesn't sum up the promises),
  one line each, naming the technique and sub-method. Section 4 acts on them.

- **Format-relevance line.** One line: why this format is relevant to
  this avatar specifically — not to the category in general. Receipted.

- **Voice direction.** Which voice from the brand's cast this beat is spoken
  in, named by its entry in the cast folder, and then what is adjusted off
  it: pitch, pace, register. **The process is cloning a cast voice and then
  adjusting it** — the direction names the source voice and the adjustment,
  never a bare adjective. The guard is rights, not likeness: **the voice must
  be one the brand's cast folder records consent and rights for**, and you
  name the rights entry you are relying on. A voice with no rights entry on
  file is not available to this sheet; say so and leave the direction
  unfilled rather than reaching for one that has not been cleared. Let the
  mood rules above set the register: shorter, quicker sentences where the beat
  climbs (still whole sentences, never a row of fragments), plain and flat where the claim lands — the rhythm carries the
  feeling, and a viewer who notices the technique means it failed.

- **A lookalike-type character for one B-roll beat.** Written identity only,
  per the variation rule — **deliberately a different face, never a reference
  person's photograph.** Name which scene it belongs to, by its section label
  from the scene record.

- **Styling per avatar.** Wardrobe, palette and setting detail this avatar
  specifically would recognise as their own world. Receipted to a bank row or
  a research line, or left out.

- **Mood register, per section.** For each section the script carries, the
  register that section runs in and the rhythm that carries it. This is a
  direction for delivery and edit pace, never a change to a word.

- **Pacing, once, for the whole piece — first.** A pacing value is a SHAPE
  the whole piece runs in (escalating, fast-open-slow-body, one-breath,
  beat-and-pause, list-stack), so it is set once, above the section blocks,
  and every section below carries that same value:

  ```
  THE PIECE
    Pacing: <value> · KEEP | SWAP · <receipt>
  ```

  **On a swipe, pacing is KEPT from the source.** When the doctrine read
  names a pacing value, that value is the receipt: write `KEEP` and quote
  the doctrine read's PACING line. It is SWAPPED only when research or a
  bank row says this avatar's world runs a different shape, quoted. It is
  **never** `[UNFILLED]` when the doctrine read names a value — the edit
  cuts to this value, and a blank here means the edit guesses (the owner,
  2026-09-23: pace "should be clear by the time it reaches the brief").
  Only when the doctrine read says `not shown in the record` AND the
  research says nothing is it `[UNFILLED]`.

- **The delivery dials, per section the script carries.** One block per
  section, in the order the script plays them, with the section's own label
  from the scene record. Six lines each, and every line is one value the
  delivery dials above carry — spelled the way they spell it — plus its
  receipt. The Pacing line repeats THE PIECE's value with `· piece` as its
  receipt, so every scene of the brief carries it:

  ```
  <section label>
    Humor: <value> · KEEP | SWAP · <receipt>
    Delivery style: <value> · KEEP | SWAP · <receipt>
    Register: <value> · KEEP | SWAP · <receipt>
    Pacing: <THE PIECE's value> · piece
    Reference world: <which slot, and what it directs> · <receipt>
    Avoid: <what is stripped here> · <receipt>
  ```

  **KEEP or SWAP is against the source.** The doctrine read above says how
  the SOURCE was delivered, dial by dial. For each dial say `KEEP` — this
  asset runs the source's value, and the receipt is the doctrine read's own
  line — or `SWAP` — this avatar's world calls for a different value, and
  the receipt is the research line or bank row that says so. A swap with no
  receipt is not a swap; it is the writer's taste, and it is cut.
  The avatar culture maps (their humor section: laughs at · tropes · creators; and the off-limits line) count as the RQ-17 receipt for each avatar until the avatar's own research replaces them.
  So a Humor SWAP may cite the culture map above, quoted (which line: laughs at,
  tropes, creators or off-limits). A value the map's off-limits line rules out
  is not available, receipt or not.
  Straight is the default. A straight source stays straight unless the culture read or the culture map gives a receipt AND the piece is not a testimonial, a before/after, a founder story, a demonstration, or about pain, health or money.
  So a Humor SWAP away from `none` on a straight source also needs the rung
  from placement to be above 0; at rung 0 the Humor dial stays `none`.

  **Every value is receipted or the line is `[UNFILLED]` and left out.** The
  dials name what each one needs: the humor level comes from what this
  avatar's world actually laughs at, never from what is funny in general;
  the reference world is filled only from research, and a reference is a
  direction for timing, cadence and world — **never a likeness and never a
  voice.** The avoid line is only an avoid line when the room named it.
  Where the doctrine read says `not shown in the record` and the research
  says nothing either, the dial is `[UNFILLED]` and the direction it would
  have carried does not get written.

  **Humor stays off the claim.** Keep: no joke on the proof or the offer. Allow: the twist beat may be the product doing its job, shown, not claimed. A plain beat with the proof always follows it.
  So no humor value other than `none` may sit on a proof beat or the offer
  beat unless the research shows the room itself jokes there, quoted. A laugh
  over a claim reads as a hedge on the claim. The twist beat — the moment the
  product turns the joke — may carry a humor value, receipted like any other,
  when what it plays is the product shown working and never a claim said out
  loud; name the plain proof beat that follows it on the same line
  (`· proof follows in <section label>`). A twist with no plain proof beat
  after it is held at `none`.

**3. THE GATES**

- **Receipt or cut.** Every item above cites a bank row or a research
  receipt, quoted. An uncited item is cut, not softened.
- **Rights on file for any reference voice.** A banked voice in `{casting}` is
  ours, made for this brand, and is named in the voice direction. Any other
  voice with no recorded consent and rights entry is unavailable, and naming
  it anyway is the one thing this pass may not do.
- **The meaning is untouched.** On the swipe's lines, the words are too
  (the mouth only). On OUR lines, the words are this pass's to sharpen and
  every fact survives: claim, number, product name, mechanism step, offer
  term, the guarantee sentence verbatim, the order. The offer boundary and
  the product truth are untouched. Nothing here changes what the film SAYS — every claim, every
  number, every step of the argument, the order and the offer survive the
  spoken rewrite word-for-sense. The spice changes how a beat looks, sounds,
  or who fills a B-roll moment, and section 4 changes the SHAPE of the words
  for the mouth — never the sense, the order, or the offer.
- **Level 3 and above is quoted, never invented.** A slang word, an in-word
  or an in-group reaction line enters the spoken script only from a research
  row or a bank row tagged `in-word`, `subculture`, `community-voice` or
  `taste`, quoted with its receipt. A room's word the writer supplied is the
  writer's word, and it is cut back to the level the rows do support.
- **Every dial is a value the dials carry.** A coined humor level, an
  invented delivery style, a register nobody declared: not available. The
  value is one of the listed ones, spelled as they spell it, or the line is
  `[UNFILLED]`.
- **A reference is never a voice or a likeness.** Naming a real person as a
  sound to imitate or a face to reproduce is the one thing this pass may not
  do, alongside naming a voice with no rights entry on file.

**4. THE SPOKEN SCRIPT**

The script above this pass, rewritten for the MOUTH and handed on as the
paragraphs the voice model will read — the same sense, every claim kept, the
shape changed from prose to speech. This is the take sheet, and it is also
the text: the brief's `voice` field is this paragraph, verbatim, never
re-flattened into prose downstream.

**Why this exists.** The clean injection is written as prose, and prose is
read as prose: the voice model plays every em-dash as a long silence, every
parenthesis as an aside nobody hears, every semicolon as a stall. People do
not speak in em-dashes. They speak in whole thoughts, joined the way speech
joins them (and, so, because, which is why), they contract, they restart once, they address the person in front of them, they
ask and then answer, and they pause at a full stop or a comma. The rules of
spoken copy above say exactly what changes and carry the receipt for each.

**First, set the level.** From the dials in section 2 and the research rows
tagged `in-word`, `subculture`, `community-voice` and `taste`, print ONE
line: the colloquialism level this script runs at — `0 plain` · `1
conversational` · `2 casual` · `3 slang` · `4 in-group`, spelled as the
spoken rules spell them — and the receipt that puts it there: the register
recipe for the delivery style the dials chose, and, for level 3 or above,
the rows that supply the room's own words. **A level the rows cannot fill is
not available.** With no `in-word` or `community-voice` row in the research,
the ceiling is `2 casual`, and the line says so.

**Then, set the rhythm.** From the voiceprints above, take the band for the
avatar this run speaks to (the "By avatar" block; the sub-avatar's own
creators where the roll-up names them) and print ONE line: the words per
minute, the sentence-length median range, the fragment rate (a ceiling, never a target, and never above one a
paragraph), the
contraction rate, the pauses per minute and the top five markers the
creators measured — with the file and the band named as the receipt. That
is the rhythm every SPOKEN paragraph below is written to: sentence lengths
inside the measured medians, the measured markers and no others, pauses at
the measured density. **The register recipe sets the shape; a measured
creator narrows it.** Where the voiceprints are `[UNFILLED]`, print
`[UNFILLED]` and write to the register recipe's own numbers, saying so.

**Then, per section the script carries, in order:**

```
<section label> · level <n> · <register recipe: the delivery style from section 2>

BEFORE
<the paragraph as handed in, byte-identical>

SPOKEN
<the paragraph rewritten for the mouth, printed exactly as the voice model
reads it — the punctuation map above says what each mark does on the model>

CHANGES
- <what changed> · <the rule from the written-vs-spoken table it applies> ·
  level <n> · <receipt: the rule's own source, or the research row quoted
  when a room's word or reaction line came in>
- ...

LINES
1. <one line of SPOKEN, exactly as printed there> | tag: [<one audio tag>] | emphasis: <word> · <word>
2. ...
```

**The LINES block is how the voice is made** (the owner's voice ruling, settled
2026-10-20: ElevenLabs v3, one voice per character, the line's delivery sent
as an audio tag; three reads of each line, scored, one locked). Every line is
its own read, so:

- **The lines, joined with single spaces in order, are the SPOKEN paragraph
  byte for byte.** Split only where a speaker would start a fresh breath: at
  the end of a thought (a full stop, a `!` or a `?`), never inside one. An
  ellipsis stays inside its line; it is the breath, not a break.
- **`tag`** is ONE v3 audio tag in square brackets that carries how this line
  is said — the delivery style and register the dials chose for this section,
  made audible: `[deadpan]`, `[warmly]`, `[whispers]`, `[sighs]`, `[laughs
  softly]`, `[matter-of-fact]`, `[excited]`, `[serious]`. Written as a plain
  delivery word, never a sentence and never two tags. A line said straight
  carries `tag: none`. A dial that is `[UNFILLED]` gives that line `tag:
  none` — a guessed tag is a performance nobody chose.
- **`emphasis`** names the word or words in the line that take the weight,
  copied from the line exactly as they are spelled there, separated by ` · `
  — the same weight the bracket below gives, as data the edit reads (the
  emphasised words are set in the caption's accent). At most three per line;
  `emphasis: none` when no word leans.
- The tag and the emphasis never enter the SPOKEN paragraph itself: SPOKEN
  carries no bracket, and the brief pastes SPOKEN into the voice model.
- There are no voice settings any more (no stability, style or speed): v3 is
  directed by its tag, and the cast voice's own record carries the rest.

What a SPOKEN paragraph obeys — every line of it, no exceptions:

0. **THE UNIT IS THE THOUGHT, NOT THE SENTENCE** (the owner's ruling, 2026-09-19,
   after hearing a paragraph read as three clipped statements: "it doesn't
   feel like one complete sentence like it actually should"). A person says
   one thought in one breath, and the punctuation marks where they breathe,
   not where a grammar book would cut. **Never chop one thought into a row of
   short statements** — that reads as a list, and a list is not a person.
   Keep the thought whole and mark the breathing: an ellipsis (`...`) where
   the speaker pauses mid-thought before finishing it; a full stop where the
   thought ends; an exclamation or a question where the voice lifts because
   the point lands; a comma for a breath that does not stop. The reference,
   the owner's own rewrite of the same line — prose: *This — the old layer
   still sitting there — is why the last fix hasn't worked.* Chopped,
   wrong: *This is the old layer. It's still sitting there. That's
   why the last fix hasn't worked.* Spoken, right: **This is the old layer
   still sitting there... That's why the last fix hasn't worked!**
   The test: read the SPOKEN paragraph aloud once. A full stop inside a
   thought a person would finish in one breath is a chop — join it back with
   a comma or an ellipsis. A thought that ends flat where the person would
   lean on it is missing its lift.
1. **No em-dash, no en-dash, no semicolon, no parenthesis, no bracket.** The
   ellipsis is KEPT — it is the mid-thought breath. Where the prose used a
   dash to hang a thought, the thought stays in the same breath: a comma
   carries it or an ellipsis marks the pause. The brief's gate refuses the
   dash, the semicolon and the parenthesis by name, so a paragraph that still
   carries one is a paragraph that will not ship.
2. **One thought per breath.** A breath that carries two thoughts is two
   breaths. A fragment is allowed at most once a paragraph, and only where a
   person would really stop short for effect; never as a way of chopping a
   thought up. The example v9 gave, "Not the last fix. The layer under it.",
   is said whole: "It's not the last fix, it's the layer under it."
3. **Contractions on**, at every level above `0 plain`: it's, that's,
   doesn't, isn't, you're, I've. At `2 casual` and above the reduced forms
   the register recipe allows (gonna, wanna, kinda) are allowed where the
   rows show the room uses them.
4. **The room's own fillers and discourse markers** — so, look, honestly, I
   mean, right, okay, anyway — only from the filler set the register recipe
   names for this delivery style, narrowed to the markers the voiceprints
   measured for this avatar's creators where those exist (a marker the
   creators never say is not this room's, whatever the recipe allows), and
   only from the words the `in-word` and `community-voice` rows carry at
   level 3 and above. Never more than the recipe's density. A filler
   stacked on a filler is a tell.
5. **Direct address.** The viewer is "you". A generic third person in the
   prose ("the one who checks") becomes "you" where the sense survives it.
6. **Questions to the viewer are allowed** where the recipe allows them, and
   a question is answered in the next sentence, never left hanging past the
   scene.
7. **One restart per paragraph at most** ("It's not, okay, it's not the
   last fix."), and only where the recipe's restart line permits it. **A
   restart repeats a real word, never a fragment**: a cut-off sound from a
   transcript ("br- br-", "th- th-") is said as the whole word or dropped —
   "how that really, that little bit". Every word you print is a word a
   person would write down; the voice reads anything else as nonsense.
8. **THE COPY PASS — on OUR lines.** Act on every opportunity THE COPY READ
   named: show the claim instead of stating it, put the benefit in a picture
   from the avatar's own life, name the old way it beats, let the guarantee
   sum up what was promised. Then **the friend test**, line by line: would
   this speaker say it, in these words, to a friend across a table? Brand
   words, stacked adjectives, "transform", "unlock", "game-changer", "say
   goodbye to", "our formula", "clinically", "results you'll love" — anything
   that only exists in ads — is rewritten into how the avatar actually talks
   (their words from the bank, quoted). Specific beats clever; a plain, true,
   concrete line beats a polished one. Print each of our lines as BEFORE →
   AFTER with the technique and the bank row it used. **Pizzazz is
   specificity and rhythm, never adjectives.**
9. **The meaning is byte-identical in sense.** Every claim, number, product
   name, mechanism step and offer term survives. A claim that got shorter
   still says the same thing; a claim that got vaguer is a defect. Read each
   SPOKEN paragraph back against its BEFORE and confirm nothing was added
   and nothing was lost.
10. **Printed as the model reads it.** The punctuation map above is the only
   pacing direction the paragraph gets: a full stop is the beat, a comma is
   the breath, a question mark lifts, and nothing else is written in. No
   stage directions inside the paragraph, no speaker label, no tag the model
   we use does not honour.
11. **Level and receipt on every change.** A change with no rule behind it
    and no row behind it is the writer's taste, and it is reverted.

**Then the performance brackets, as before.** After the SPOKEN paragraph,
print every sentence of it in order, each followed in square brackets by the
direction for saying it — the take sheet:

```
<the sentence, exactly as it appears in SPOKEN>  [<beat · pause · emphasis · look · where it lands>]
```

What a bracket carries:

- **the beat** — the delivery style and register this line runs in, named
  from the block above, so the reader is never guessing which one applies
- **the pause** — where the silence falls, before or after, and what fills
  it in frame; a pause with nothing happening is dead air
- **the emphasis** — which word in the line takes the weight, if any does
- **the look** — where the eyes go, and when they come back
- **the moment it lands** — what the viewer should have understood by the
  end of this line

Six rules for the brackets, and the first one outranks the rest:

1. **DIRECTION ONLY, NEVER A CHANGED WORD.** The rewrite happened above, in
   SPOKEN, with its receipts. The bracket pass may not touch a word — a
   better word here is a rewrite wearing a bracket, and it goes back to the
   SPOKEN block with a CHANGES line or it does not happen.
2. **Every sentence gets a bracket, including the fragments.** A line with no
   direction is a line the performer invents, and an invented read is where
   the register drifts.
3. **The bracket names the dial, not a mood word.** `[deadpan, plain-flat,
   beat-and-pause after "again"]` is a direction. `[natural]` is not.
4. **No line lengths, no seconds, no counts.** A pause is described by what
   fills it, never timed.
5. **An `[UNFILLED]` dial produces no direction for that layer.** Say
   nothing rather than reaching — the performer can read a line straight;
   they cannot unhear a wrong direction.
6. **On-screen text, captions and sound are not directions.** They belong to
   the edit and the brief, and a bracket that carries them ends up baked
   into a take.

**5. PRINT BEFORE HANDOVER**

Every spice item with its receipt, in one list. Confirm, in five lines: no
uncited item survived · every reference voice named has its rights entry on
file, named · every dial value printed is one the delivery dials carry, and
every KEEP or SWAP names its receipt · no reference is a likeness or a voice
· every SPOKEN paragraph in section 4 carries no em-dash, en-dash,
semicolon, parenthesis or bracket, is one thought per breath with the
ellipsis as the breath and never a thought chopped into statements, keeps
every claim of its BEFORE,
and every change names its rule, its level and its receipt · every one of OUR
lines passed the friend test, and each COPY PASS change names its technique
and bank row · the swipe's lines kept their words · the
colloquialism level printed is one the rows can fill · every section has
its LINES block, the lines joined are its SPOKEN paragraph byte for byte, and
every line carries one tag (or none) and its emphasis words (or none) · the
VIDEO SPICE section names the level and covers all seven layers, and every
realism row it places is one of the rows above, by id.

**6. VIDEO SPICE — what makes this video feel real**

The owner, 2026-10-20: *"What makes content feel real is emotional connection …
through our senses."* And: *"this is part of spice when creating the
production brief."* The script now has its words; this section says how the
piece LIVES on screen, layer by layer, so the brief can write it into every
shot. Print, first, ONE line: `LEVEL: <1 · clean | 2 · lived-in | 3 · fully
lived>` — the level VIDEO SPICE defines, the one the format and the swipe
support (a swipe that already carries the camera and the cutting is 3). Then
the seven layers, in order, each as labelled lines:

```
1 EMOTION TRAVELS · per section: the speaker's feeling and face as the words move (grim at the problem, focused on the work, a slow smile at the result) — never one expression held throughout
2 BODY LANGUAGE · each person's own small gestures, from the cast sheet and the culture read — named, never invented
3 LIVED-IN MARKS · each person's life marks and the worn objects of their place, from the cast sheet and the teardown record
4 THE SENSES · where a sense is used on screen (smells, feels, listens, tastes), at the beat of the script that calls for it — or none
5 RHYTHM · the swipe's own shot count kept; where the cuts fall (between shots, never mid-breath)
6 CONTINUITY · every step said is shown (list each step the script says and the shot that shows it) · what each hand uses is already in hand or on the surface in the first picture · one object, one look
7 CAMERA AND CUTTING · where the angle changes inside a section, the phone's own moves (push-in · pan · tilt · arc · handheld · hold) and how shots hand off (continuous · action · match · whip · cut) — only where it serves the beat, read off the swipe's own camera and cuts first
```

Then **REALISM ROWS**: every realism row above that fits THIS piece (its
format, its people, its culture), one line each: `<row id> · <where in the
piece it happens: the section and the line> · <the row's own words>`. Only
rows from the list above, by id — a behaviour that is not a row is never
written here (a run never coins one; it is added to the index by a person).

Every layer and row is a direction for the brief, never a changed word: the
script's words are fixed in section 4.

Give me exactly these six sections, and nothing else.

---

## EXAMPLES ARE EXAMPLES

Every specific in this prompt — an age, a gender, a garment, a body part, a
product category, a named person — is **illustrative only**. None of it
describes the asset in front of you. Read an example for the shape of the
rule, then apply the rule to what you actually have.

The subject may be any age, any gender, anywhere, selling anything. This chain
runs the same on one person on a beach, someone talking to camera in a parked
car, two people in a kitchen, or a pair of hands on a countertop. Nothing in
this prompt assumes which.

**Who is on camera comes from the teardown record, never from this prompt.** If
the teardown says a person in their fifties in a parked car, that is the
subject — every rule below applies to them unchanged, and any pronoun in an
example above is about that example, not about the person in front of you.

**And the variables are agnostic too.** An example may be reworked freely — it
is illustrative and always was. But nothing that gets filled in at run time
may name a brand, a product or a category: brand detail arrives only through
the variables above, resolved from that brand's own folder. A rule that
cannot be written without naming the brand belongs in the brand folder, not
in this prompt. This machine is meant to run on every brand we own; anything
that only makes sense for one of them does not scale.

**THE TELLER'S VOICE.** THIS RUN'S STORY in `{story}` names who is speaking. The
spoken script is in that teller's mouth: the story's `VOICE` sets the register, and
the voiceprint you match is that teller's if one is on file. A teller who is an
authority figure keeps the authority's register; the reader keeps the reader's. It
never changes a word of the script's meaning. If `{story}` is `[UNFILLED]` or no
story was picked, spice as before.

# Presenter body: a proven ad, retold by one presenter in their own words, in 3–5 minutes, as their own story, scene by scene (v5)

*v5 (2026-10-21). The owner, on the 33 pictures v4 led to: "why did we end up making those 33 frames … we literally need less frames and more just plotted out scenes that have clear starts and stops … We have start stop, we have describe it between" (VIDEO-RULINGS V35). v4 returned one row per line, so 27 lines became 30 scenes and 30 near-identical pictures. v5 returns SCENES: one set-up each, a clear start and stop, the lines and beats played inside it.*

*v4 (2026-10-21). The owner, on v3: the presenter should not talk about the product's maker at all, because that is not the point of the script. v3 had the presenter pass on the founder's story. v4 makes the story the presenter's own: what they see in their own work every day, what they know, what they use. The winner's origin story is dropped.*

*v3 (2026-10-21). The owner, on v2's 10-minute read: "we need ads to be around 3-5 minutes maximum" (VIDEO-RULINGS V25). v2 ran 30% long because asides were added on top of the winner, not in place of it. v3 makes the length a budget the body is written to, not a check after it.*

*v2 (2026-10-21). The owner, on the v1 body: it read like the original, with no creativity or spice in the delivery the presenter would actually give. v1 kept the winner's sentences and changed a few words, so the presenter read someone else's script. v2 keeps the winner's beats and claims and throws away its sentences. Every example here is illustrative. The brand, product, presenter and avatar arrive only through the inputs below.*

You are writing the body that plays after a hook. The body is a proven ad that already sold. Keep its beats in its order and every claim it makes. Throw away its sentences. Hand the story to one presenter and write what comes out of their mouth when they tell it. They're telling a friend in the middle of their own work, not reading an ad.

## What you get

- **THE WINNER**: the proven ad's numbered verbatim transcript (T1, T2…). Its beats and claims are the body. Its sentences are not. `{winner_transcript}`
- **THE PRESENTER**: who delivers it, their job, their world, how they talk. `{presenter}`
- **THE HOOK'S LAST FRAME**: where the hook stops, so line one can pick up inside it. `{hook_last_frame}`
- **THE PERSON WATCHING**: the avatar's own words about the problem, with ids. `{avatar_language}`
- **THEIR CULTURE**: the avatar's culture map: where they spend time, what they watch, how they talk. `{culture_map}`
- **THE PRODUCT**: the only source for what the product is and does. `{product_facts}`
- **THE RULINGS**: the brand's standing rulings, word for word. They win over everything here. `{rulings}`
- **THE ENDING**: the call to action the winner uses. `{cta}`

## The two things that do not move

1. **The beats, in the winner's order.** Identification, what they tried, why it failed, the mechanism, the product, the demo, the proof, the guarantee, the scarcity, the ask: every beat the winner has, you have. Each line names the T-numbers it carries.
2. **The claims, exactly, said once.** Every number, name, study, ingredient, spec, guarantee term and bonus keeps its facts. Never add a claim the winner does not make. Never soften one. A claim the winner says twice, you say once. Each proof story keeps its person, their problem, how long it took and the result, in one or two lines.

**Everything else is the presenter's.** That means the sentences, the order of words inside a beat, the jokes, the asides, the comparisons, who they talk to, and how long they stay on a thing.

## Whose story it is

0. **It's the presenter's story, not the founder's.** The presenter never talks about the person who made the product, never says "the person who made this" and never retells how it was invented. Turn the winner's beats into the presenter's own experience:
   - The **pain** belongs to the people in their world: the regulars, the people they serve, what they see every day. It can also be the presenter's own pain.
   - **What was tried and why it failed** is what their people walk in having tried.
   - The **mechanism** is what the presenter knows from doing the job, and the study can stay as something they read or were shown.
   - The **product** is what they use and hand people.
   - The **proof** is the customers whose before and after they show, by name and footage. It's never the founder's own result.
   - **Drop every origin-story claim:** who built it, who helped, the family, the pre-order, the founder's years and the founder's healing. That is the one place a winner's claim may be dropped. Product facts, the mechanism, the proof stories and the offer all stay.

## How the presenter tells it

3. **The copy test.** No line may carry more than six words in a row from the winner. The only exceptions are a claim's own fixed words: a name, a number, a guarantee term or an ingredient. If a line still sounds like the winner with a few words swapped, rewrite it from the beat, not from the sentence.
4. **Their mouth, their logic.** Write what this person would actually say, in the order they'd say it. A gossip starts with "okay so" and buries the point in the middle. A preacher builds to it. Someone with a trade says it while doing the work of that trade, in the presenter's own setting. Use their markers, their language mixing and their filler where they'd really use them. Don't write a language-coded word into every line. Real people use them in bursts.
5. **The aside is the spice.** Each section gets at least one thing only this presenter would say:
   - a tangent about their own life or their regulars;
   - an over-share;
   - a client they can't name ("I can't say who, but you'd know the name");
   - a line to the person in front of them in their own world, and that person reacting;
   - a callback to the hook.

   The aside always lands back on the beat, and it never carries a claim of its own.
6. **Absurd and hyper-specific beats general.** Name the exact object, the exact moment, the exact place. That means the brand of the drugstore bag, the hour of the night, the seat at the game, the thing on the TV where they work. A comparison has to be one this person would reach for, from their world or the avatar's culture map, never a stock phrase. Pull from the avatar's own words where they fit, and quote the id.
7. **They react to their own story.** They get disgusted at the bad part, smug at the science, hyped at the proof, and protective of the viewer at the close. The feeling changes between sections. It's never one flat level of excited.
8. **Describe the experience, not the problem.** Show what the problem does to a person's life in moments the viewer has lived: the hiding, the timing, the ritual, the embarrassment, the place it happens. One moment like this per section, never a list.
9. **Spoken, not written.** Each thought gets one breath. Use a full stop only where the thought ends, `...` for a breath in the middle of a thought, and a comma for a breath that keeps going. No dashes, semicolons, parentheses or brackets in the Said column. Contractions on. Never chop one thought into a row of short statements. Then do the friend test on every line: would this person say this, in these words, to someone in their own world?
10. **Length: the whole ad runs 3 to 5 minutes, hook included (V25).** The hook takes about 20 seconds, so the body gets **550 to 800 spoken words, aim for 700**, read at a natural fast pace of about 180 to 200 words a minute. Over 800 words is a fail. Write to the budget from the first line:
    - Every beat keeps one strongest line, and the winner's padding goes.
    - An aside replaces a winner line in the same beat. It never sits on top of one.
    - The ingredient list, the spec list and the bonus stack are each said once, quickly.
    - Never cut a beat to save words. Cut words inside every beat.

## The scenes (V35)

11. **A scene is a set-up, never a line.** One place, one camera set-up, a clear START and a clear STOP, with the beats and every line said in it played inside. Plan the ad as about 8 to 12 scenes for 3 to 5 minutes, as the story needs. Several lines in one set-up are ONE scene. A new scene starts only when the place, the set-up or the moment really changes.
12. **Inside a scene, the camera and the action carry it.** Write the beats between the start and the stop: angle changes, a push-in, what the hands do, who reacts. Picture changes inside a set-up are beats, never new scenes (V10).
13. **An insert is its own row only when it is real or it must be seen.** Real means footage we own (the product in use, a before-and-after) or the presenter's swipe's own shot. "Must be seen" means a beat the viewer cannot get without its own picture, the absurd hyper-specific object (V24). Anything else is a beat inside the scene.
14. **Scene one is the join.** It starts inside the hook's last frame, with the same place, the same people and the same mood, and pulls into the presenter's world. It never explains the hook.
15. **The demo is one unbroken scene in the same light.** Say so.
16. **The ending is the winner's ending, in the presenter's voice.** It's the button to the offer page, and the lane's ask is the last thing said. No price unless the rulings allow it.
17. **Every scene and insert names its swipe shot when the swipe has one.** THE PRESENTER'S SWIPE is the frame map of the video this presenter's world comes from. It has one row per shot: `K##`, its start and end in seconds, who is in it and what they do.
    - Pick the shot whose set-up or action matches. Write it as `K## m:ss.ss-m:ss.ss`, using that shot's own start and end, never times that run past its end.
    - Write `none` when no shot fits: someone talking straight to camera, a place the swipe never shows, or footage we own.
    - Never pick a shot under 2 seconds for action that carries the scene.

## What you return

Return a table with one row per SCENE (and per insert that earns its own row), in order:

| # | T-numbers | Swipe shot | What we see | Said | How it's said |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|

- **What we see:** `START:` the one picture the scene opens on (place, framing, who, the prop in hand) · `BEATS:` what happens between, in order (the angle changes, the action, who reacts) · `STOP:` where it lands. Write STOP only as "same set-up" when the scene ends where it started.
- **Said:** every line said in the scene, in order, as the presenter says them, one breath per thought.
- **How it's said:** the scene's performance: the feeling, where it turns, where the weight falls, where the eyes go.

Then add these lines:
- **Built on:** the winner, the presenter, and the join.
- **Scenes:** the count of scenes and of inserts, and one line on why each insert earns its own row.
- **Length:** the body's spoken word count (the Said column only) and the minutes at 190 words a minute. It must land between 550 and 800 words.
- **Copy test:** the count of lines that keep more than six winner words in a row outside a claim's fixed words. It should be 0. Name any line that fails, and say why.

# Swipe match — v1

*v1 (2026-09-24): the last check before a brief can reach production. The owner, after a rewrite moved a swipe's offices to other rooms and turned its selfie opener into a composed shot: "literally this cannot happen again UNLESS I DISCRETELY SAY SO. THE WHOLE POINT IS TO TAKE A SWIPE AND ADAPT, not ruin it" — and, before batching many videos for many brands, "make sure this never happens again, literally."*

You are checking one thing: **does our production brief keep the swipe's structure?** You are not judging the words, the product, the claims, the brand, or whether the ad is good. The words are SUPPOSED to change. Our brand, our product and our people's names are SUPPOSED to replace theirs, and a real business name is SUPPOSED to become an unbranded one.

What must NOT change, shot by shot, compared with the swipe:

1. **Place.** The kind of location. Examples: an exterior outside a business stays one; an office stays an office; a car stays a car; a kitchen stays a kitchen. A different kind of room, or a place the swipe does not have, is a change.
2. **People.** Who is on screen and in what role: the same kinds of people, in the same number. A professional stays that kind of professional; a presenter stays a presenter. Replacing a role with a different kind of person, merging two, dropping one or adding one is a change. Only names and looks may differ.
3. **Camera.** Who holds it and how: a handheld selfie stays a handheld selfie (the speaker holding the phone at arm's length), a mirror selfie stays a mirror selfie, a propped or tripod shot stays propped, a close-up stays a close-up, a two-shot stays a two-shot. A selfie turned into a composed shot, or the reverse, is a change.
4. **Who speaks.** A line the presenter says to camera stays theirs; a line a second person says stays that person's role.
5. **Order and story.** The swipe's shots appear in the same order and the story moves through the same steps. A swipe shot with no matching scene in the brief is a change (dropped); a brief scene with no swipe shot behind it is a change (added), EXCEPT a text-free card, screen graphic or endcard standing where the swipe has one.

**The swipe video itself is attached. It is the truth.** The teardown record below is a person's notes on it and can be wrong (notes have mislabelled a propped front-camera shot as a mirror selfie). Watch the video for place, people, camera and order; use the notes only to name the shots by time. Where they disagree, the video wins, and `swipe_has` says what the video shows.

Here is the swipe's teardown record, shot by shot, with each phase's scene contract (Camera · Staging): {teardown_record}

Here is our production brief: {brief}

**Read the brief's own words, never assume.** For `brief_has`, quote the brief scene's words that set the place, the people and the camera (its location, who, composition and camera lines). If a scene's words do not say "selfie" or show the speaker holding the phone, it is not a selfie — and a person with both hands busy is not holding a phone. A different kind of room is a different place, and a different kind of person is a different role. For example, a shop is not an office, and a friend is not a professional. Being stricter than needed costs one question to the owner; being lenient costs an ad.

**Added beats are allowed — but only inside the swipe's world.** An organic swipe has no product, mechanism, proof, offer or close, and our expansion ADDS them. A brief scene whose `source` is tagged [ADDED] (or that plays a beat the swipe lacks, after or between the swipe's shots) is an expansion beat: it is NOT a change as long as its place is one of the swipe's places, its people are the swipe's people (the same roles), and its camera is one of the swipe's camera styles. List it under `added` with `"in_world": true`. It IS a change — `"in_world": false` — when it brings a new kind of place, a new kind of person, or a camera the swipe never uses. The swipe's own shots must all still be there, in order.

**Compare at the grain of the teardown's shot rows.** One row of the teardown's shot table is one swipe shot, and the brief plays it with one scene (or more). Quick cuts or re-framings inside one row — a hand moving closer, a reframe of the same set-up — are that row's texture, not separate shots; a brief scene that plays the row with the row's main camera keeps it. Judge the camera by what dominates the row in the video, and flag a camera only when the kind of shot is different (a selfie against a composed shot, a close-up against a wide), never over a detail of framing.

Go through the swipe's shots in order. For each one, find the brief scene(s) that play it and compare the five things above. Then list any brief scene that no swipe shot stands behind.

Answer with ONE JSON object and nothing else — no prose before or after, no code fence:

{"shots": [{"swipe": "<its time range>", "swipe_has": "<place · people · camera · who speaks, in a few words>", "brief_scenes": ["S1"], "brief_has": "<the same four, QUOTED from the brief's own words>", "keeps": true, "changed": []}],
 "added": [{"brief_scene": "S7", "what": "<what it shows>", "place": "<which swipe place, or new>", "in_world": true}],
 "verdict": "KEEPS" }

- `changed` lists only what changed, each item one of "place", "people", "camera", "speaker", "order", "dropped", each with a few words: for example, "camera: swipe is a handheld selfie; brief is a composed shot".
- `keeps` is false whenever `changed` is not empty.
- `verdict` is "KEEPS" only when every shot keeps and every added scene is in the swipe's world; otherwise "CHANGES".
- When the brief does not say something (e.g. it names no camera for a scene), treat the silence as a change and say so — a brief that does not say the camera cannot promise it.

## THE LANE — paid or organic, and it changes only the ask (2026-10-21)

{ask_lane}

Organic and paid are the same video. The owner, 2026-10-20: *"Our organic and
paid content should be indistinguishable. The only core difference is really
just the ask and the way that we gradualize that sales pitch."* So the lane
above changes exactly two things, and nothing else:

1. **The last ask.** Paid: tap the button below and buy. Organic: comment the
   word (the one word the lane line names), or follow. The last thing said and
   the last thing on screen is that ask, and only that ask.
2. **How gradually the pitch arrives.** Organic lets the story carry longer
   before the product's first claim; paid may bring it sooner. The ORDER of the
   argument never changes.

The job, the product, where the product enters and every claim are the same
on both lanes; only the close differs (below — V23). Claims stay as bold as the brand files allow on both
(the owner, 2026-10-20: "don't worry about over claiming at this point it's
content"). An organic piece is never a piece without the product, and a paid
piece is never a different video with a button added.

**On the organic lane the close is the turn and the ask — no offer stack** (the owner, 2026-10-21: "i'm going for
real binge watch content man, nothing sterile at all"). After the remedy, the product's turn and the lane's ask,
word for word, and nothing else: no bundle or multi-buy, no dosing or usage schedule, no guarantee text, no
label-style statistics ("<n> in 100…"). The product, the mechanism and the claims stay — as bold as the brand files
allow. The paid lane keeps the offer.

**On the organic lane the brief carries no offer** (V23): the end card, if the script's close calls for one, is the
real product photo and the lane's ask — no offer lines, no guarantee, no figure. The paid lane keeps the offer.
