# THE SHAPE

Each concept:

```
# ⟨concept name — three or four words, the way you'd say it in conversation, coined from THIS concept's own idea; never the words of this example⟩

Core message: ⟨the teardown record's `Core message:` line, word for word — the one
belief this ad leaves behind (the owner, 5 Oct 2026). Every scene below carries it or
sets up one of the beliefs under it; a scene that does neither is named in the
decisions for the owner, never silently kept.⟩

⟨One to three sentences: the core idea this concept runs on, from its own
material.⟩

## WHAT TO FILM

### The openings — shoot all six

**Opening 1 · Control**
**Film:** the product held completely still for three seconds while the card
sits over it. Nothing moves.
**Say:** "One product. Four places I actually need it."

![Opening 1](—)

**Opening 2 · "The left one"**
**Film:** two forearms side by side in the same frame, one visibly different
from the other.
**Say:** "I only did the left arm for a month."

![Opening 2](—)

…all six, control first.

### The frames

**Frame 1 · 0:00–0:04**

**Source:** "Creator in black V-neck, green-screened over footage…"

**Film:** A keyed frame. You are filmed against a plain wall and cut out,
sitting in the lower left of the picture and taking about a third of it.
Behind you, filling the rest of the frame, plays the footage of the two of
them. You are looking straight down the lens, mid-sentence.

**Hear:** your voice close on the phone mic. Nothing from the plate behind.

**Wearing:** cream knit sweater, sleeves pushed to the elbow, hair down, small
gold hoops.

**On screen:** "the day I finally"

> **Say:** "One product. Four places I actually need it."

![Frame 1](—)

**Frame 2 · 0:05–0:07**

**Source:** "Transition to Creator lying on grass…"

**Film:** Cut. A plain shot now, no keying — the counter, one window to your
left, the product held up at chest height with the label to camera. You do not
move; the stillness is the shot.

**Hear:** nothing but the room.

**Wearing:** cream knit sweater, sleeves pushed to the elbow, hair down, small
gold hoops.

**On screen:** nothing

> **Say:** nothing — picture only

![Frame 2](—)
```

## EVERY OPENING GETS A PICTURE

Openings are written as numbered units — `**Opening 1 · Control**` — in the
same shape the frames use, each followed by its own placeholder line. This is
not cosmetic: the checks and the editor find the openings by that shape, so an
opening written any other way silently drops out. (No picture fills the
placeholder any more; the assemble step removes it.)

The opening is the shot being tested. Its Film line has to let the creator see the
moment from words alone — they are all the same moment shot differently, so
say exactly what differs.

## THE SHOT LIST IS AUDITED AGAINST THE SOURCE BEFORE YOU FINISH

Last thing before you return anything. Walk the teardown record's rows in
order against your frames, and satisfy yourself of three things:

1. **Every shot in the record has a frame.** None dropped.
2. **Every frame is accounted for** — either by a shot in the record, or by
   a line in the script that runs past where the source ends. A frame with
   neither behind it is invented; delete it. On an organic source most
   frames will be script-backed, and that is correct: the post was the hook
   and the script is the ad.
3. **The script is covered to its last line.** If your final frame lands
   before the script does, the brief is unfinished.
4. **Each frame is doing what its source shot did** — same place, same
   action, same construction. Ours substitutes the brand, never the
   behaviour. If the source is in a shop, ours is in a shop. If the source
   uses its product the way it is meant to be used, ours uses ours the way the brief says. If the source is a
   keyed talking head, ours is keyed.

Where the source's own product had to be swapped for ours, the *action*
still holds: a demo is still a demo, a reveal is still a reveal, a reaction
shot is still that reaction. **Substituting the product never licenses
changing what the person is doing.**

If a source shot genuinely cannot be reproduced with our product — the
mechanic does not exist for us — say so in one line under that frame and
keep the frame. That is a finding for the owner, not something to quietly
paper over with a different shot.

## THIS DOCUMENT IS SENDABLE AS IT STANDS

**Nothing unresolved reaches this document.** Not a `[SLOT: …]`, not a
bracket, not a note to whoever fills something in, not a checklist at the
top. It is a creative brief: it goes to a creator, to a freelancer, to
whoever picks it up, and it has to read as finished to all of them.

Upstream stages write `[SLOT: …]` wherever a fact was needed and the brand
files did not hold it. That is the anti-invention rule working, and you do
not undo it. **You never fill a slot in.** What you do instead depends on
what kind of slot it is, and there are only two kinds.

**A slot asking who someone is — cut the slot, keep the shot.** Casting is
not a fact anyone looks up; it is a decision made when the thing is filmed.
The person filming is **you** throughout, never named and never described as
a casting requirement. Anyone else on screen is written by their role in the
story — "a friend", "someone their age", "the person who sent it" — and that
is all the brief ever needed. A frame is never held up waiting for a name.

**A slot asking for a brand fact — cut the line.** A price, a promotion, a
date, a guarantee wording: if it is not in one of the files above, the
sentence carrying it does not appear. Say nothing rather than something
unapproved.

**A cut leaves no hole.** When the slot sat inside a card, a caption or a
spoken line, the whole line is rewritten without that fact — or dropped —
so what remains reads as a finished sentence. "How that [SLOT: age] year
old influencer is telling you to fix <the problem>" does not become "How that
year old influencer is telling you to fix <the problem>"; it becomes "How that
influencer is telling you to fix <the problem>", or the card goes. A sentence
with a gap in it went out as on-screen text four times on 2026-09-02.

There is no third option. **Generalising a slot is filling it.** Turning
`[SLOT: current promotion]` into "the bundle deal" or "today's sale" has
announced a promotion nobody approved, in the one document that goes to a
person who will say it out loud.

**Where the idea came from is credit, not a gap.** If naming the source is
useful, it goes at the end as one line of inspiration — "built from a format
that ran on [platform]" — never as a task, never as a blank to fill.

## Naming and describing the concept

**The `# ` heading names the concept.** Short, the way you'd refer to it in
conversation — not a slogan, not a headline. **Coined from this concept's own
idea, never borrowed from an example in this prompt:** on 2026-09-03 four
briefs in one creator's folder came back titled after the prompt's own sample
heading, and the reviewer saw four copies of one brief.

**Under the heading, one to three sentences describing what this concept is** —
the core idea it runs on, in plain language. Pull this from the concept's own
material (the placement plan, the injection, the stage-4 passes); never invent
a rationale that isn't already there. This replaces any explanation the maker
would otherwise have to guess at from the scenes alone — it's the only place in
the document where the "why" appears, and it stays to one paragraph.

## The opening section — the part that gets mangled

Every hook in the hook set goes in, control first, then each variation under
its own name. For each one, two labeled lines — never blended into one
paragraph, never left for the maker to infer:

- **`Film:`** the scroll stopper — the frame itself, exactly as the hook set
  describes it.
- **`Say:`** the spoken line, word for word. If the format has no speech,
  label it **`Card:`** instead and give the on-screen text.

A maker reading this must never have to guess whether a line is something they
say out loud or just a description of the scene. `Say:` means it comes out
of their mouth on camera. `Film:` never contains a spoken line.

**The scroll stoppers are already written. You are copying them, not writing
them.** The hook set hands you each one as a described frame. Reproduce that
frame — the angle, the distance, where the subject is, what is in shot, the light.

This is the single thing that goes wrong most. On one run the source was a
person lying on a towel on a beach in midday sun, the hook set said exactly
that, and the brief opened on a bathroom counter in the morning instead. A
frame was then generated of the bathroom, and the entire reason the source
stopped anyone scrolling was gone. **The scroll stopper is the swipe.** It is
the most valuable thing in the teardown and the least replaceable.

So:

- **Never relocate the opening.** If the source's first frame is outdoors, the
  opening is outdoors. Do not move it somewhere easier to shoot.
- **Never substitute a product shot for a scroll stopper.** A product held up to
  camera is not an attention device; it is what the source spent its opening
  earning the right to show.
- **Never paraphrase it into a summary.** "Relaxing outside" is not the
  frame. The angle, the distance and what fills the frame are the frame.
- **Frame 1 of the shot list IS the control's scroll stopper**, written out
  as a frame with its wardrobe line. The two must describe the same picture.
  If Frame 1 and the control disagree, Frame 1 is wrong.

Check the teardown record if a scroll stopper is unclear — it holds the source
shot by shot, and the opening frame is in its first row. All hooks get filmed;
nobody picks.

## THE UNIT IS THE FRAME — AND THE SCRIPT DECIDES HOW MANY

**Every frame exists because a line or a beat in the script calls for it.
One shot, one frame. No frame without something to carry.**

Two different sources come through here and they are not the same job.

**A source that is already a finished ad** is a format to replicate. Its
shots are the plan. Transcribe them: the teardown's Visuals column, split
where a row holds several shots — "Creator in kitchen. **Cut to** family member
on sofa. **Close-up** of the button" is three shots — and that count is your
count. Writing more means inventing footage nobody filmed.

**A source that is an organic post** is a hook, not an ad. It is a few
seconds long and the ad you are briefing is not. The close pass hands you
the full script; **your shot list covers all of it, to the last line.** The
source's shots cover the opening and then run out, and where they run out
you keep going — the script is still talking, so there are still frames.

### Count against the script, not the clock

Before writing, read the script the close pass produced and count what it
needs: every spoken line, every card, every held beat. **That is your frame
count.** Then check the teardown: wherever the source has a shot for that
moment, the frame is the source's shot. Wherever it does not, the frame
comes from what the line needs — plainly, one shot, nothing invented around
it.

Finishing at the source's runtime when the script runs longer is the failure
this rule exists to stop. A run on 2026-08-26 briefed a nine-second post as
a nine-second ad: seven frames, no body, nothing after the hook. The script
was there and the shot list stopped short of it.

### Why the opposite rule exists too

A run on 2026-08-25 turned a source describing eighteen shots into a brief
of forty-one frames, on a source that was already a finished ad. The extra
frames had nothing to describe, so each was filled with plausible invention:
a wardrobe change nobody called for, a mortar and pestle nobody filmed.
**Every fault in that brief lived in a frame that should not have existed.**

Both failures are the same mistake — the frame count coming from somewhere
other than the script. Invented frames are not extra value; a shot list that
stops before the script does is not restraint.

### What "every shot" includes

If the source cuts to a shop, you have a shop. If it cuts to B-roll of
somewhere abroad, you have that B-roll. If it goes back to the keyed talking
head for one line, that is a frame too. **A shot you find awkward to shoot
is still a shot** — describe it and let the maker or the owner decide. Never
silently drop one because it is inconvenient; a dropped shot is as wrong as
an invented one, and it is the reason a swiped format stops working.

### Do not write a transcript at the end

The finished brief ends with every spoken line laid out in order, so a
creator can read the script without hunting through a shot list for it.
**That section is assembled from your `Say:` lines automatically — you do
not write it.** Writing your own would produce a second version of the
script that drifts from the frames. Put each line where it belongs, on its
frame, and the transcript takes care of itself.

## HOW THE FRAME IS PUT TOGETHER

A frame is not always one plain camera shot, and when it is not, **say so
first** — this is the part that makes a format reproducible. Read the
teardown record's own description and carry the construction across:

- **A keyed or composited frame** — the creator is filmed against a background that
  is not the room they are in, cut out and laid over other footage. Say what
  they occupy, and what is playing behind them.
- **Picture-in-picture** — their talking head in a corner over a full-frame
  plate. Say which corner and roughly how much of the frame they take.
- **Split screen** — say how it divides and what is on each side.
- **A screen recording** — a phone or a browser being captured. Say what is
  on the screen.
- **A plain shot** — say nothing about construction; just describe it.

Formats live or die on this. Reproducing the words of a keyed talking-head
opener as a plain selfie throws away the thing that made it work.

## Every frame

Eight labeled lines, always in this order, never merged into one paragraph:

1. **`**Frame N · start–end**`** — bold, the word Frame, number, middle dot, span.
2. **`Source:`** the source shot this frame replaces, quoted from the
   teardown record's Visuals column, shortened to its first few words.
   **Frame N replaces source shot N — the order never changes.**

   This line is what makes the brief checkable. Anyone can read down the
   two columns and see instantly whether the shot list still tracks the
   video it came from. Without it, one dropped shot silently shifts
   everything after it by one and the second half of the brief quietly
   stops being the format at all — which is exactly what happened on
   2026-08-25, where frame 16 was a pair of before-and-after photographs
   standing where the source had a person walking into a restaurant.

   If you find yourself unable to write this line for a frame, that frame
   has no source shot and should not exist.
3. **`Film:`** how the frame is built (above) and then what the maker does —
   where they are, the light, what is in their hands, what they do with it.
   This is direction for the camera. **It never contains a line they say out
   loud** — a spoken line always goes under `Say:`, never folded into the
   filming direction.
4. **`Hear:`** what the moment sounds like — the room tone, the thing that
   makes a noise, the silence when it drops out. One short line.

   Only what the maker can actually capture in the room they are in. No music
   direction, no sound design, no effect they would have to go and source. If
   the honest answer is that the moment is nearly silent, write that —
   "nothing but the room" is a real instruction, and often the strongest one
   on the page.

   **Take it from what is physically happening in the frame**, never from
   atmosphere you imagine. A box being opened makes a noise; a tap running
   makes a noise; a room full of people makes a noise. The teardown record
   only describes sound where nobody is speaking, so for every other scene
   you are reasoning from the objects and actions already in the shot — not
   inventing a mood. If nothing in the frame would make a sound worth
   naming, say so and stop.
5. **`Wearing:`** on every single frame, spelled out in full — garment,
   colour, sleeves, hair, jewellery. Never "as before", never "same as frame 2",
   never left out. A frame with no wardrobe line is a broken frame: the picture
   for it gets generated from nothing and comes back as a stranger in clothes
   the subject does not own.

   **The outfits are the source's, and there are only as many as it has.**
   The teardown record lists the wardrobe — usually one main outfit and
   occasionally a second for a specific scene. Use those and no others.
   Write the same outfit, word for word, on every consecutive frame until
   the source itself changes clothes.

   **A change of clothes is a change of time or place, and it must be
   visible in the story.** The creator is in their main outfit narrating; they are in
   the second outfit at the event. If you cannot point at the moment in the
   source where they changed, they did not change — repeat the previous
   wardrobe line exactly. Outfits drifting frame to frame for no reason is
   the single clearest sign a brief was invented rather than observed, and
   it reads as slop to anyone who watches it back.
6. **`On screen:`** the words burned into the picture at this moment, copied
   **verbatim** from the teardown record's own On-Screen Text column, in
   quotes. This is the caption the viewer reads, and it is very often not
   the same as what they are saying — it lags, it shortens, it stops
   mid-sentence. Copy what the source actually put on screen; never tidy it
   into a full sentence and never re-derive it from the spoken line.

   If the record shows nothing on screen for this frame, write
   `On screen: nothing`. That is a real instruction — it tells the editor to
   leave the picture clean.

   **A frame can have both a caption and speech at the same time, and most
   of these formats do.** An earlier version of this prompt said a scene had
   one or the other; that was wrong and it lost every caption in the brief.
7. **On its own line, as a markdown quote (`> `):** **`> **Say:**`** followed
   by the exact words they speak on camera, in quotes — the voiceover,
   nothing else. If nobody speaks in this frame, write
   `> **Say:** nothing — picture only`. Keep the leading `> ` always; it is
   what keeps the spoken line out of the picture the frame generates.
8. **`![Frame N](—)`** on its own line.

## EVERY FRAME STANDS ALONE — IT CANNOT SEE THE ONE BEFORE IT

**Each frame is read on its own, by someone who has not read any other
frame.** So every Film line names the place, and what the body is doing, in
full — every time, even when nothing has changed since the last frame.

Continuation shorthand is the failure. "You stay exactly where you are",
"same as before", "back out to the seated wide", "same frame" — these are
references, and there is nothing there to refer to. A frame reading "you
stay exactly where you are, hands behind your head" put a person standing on
a wooden deck in the middle of a shoot that happens entirely on a beach
(2026-08-26), because the only thing the line actually said was "hands
behind your head".

Write it as if it were the first frame. **"Lying on the white towel on the
sand, hands behind your head, nothing moving."** Repeating the place on
every frame is not clutter — it is the only thing holding the set together.

**Where the camera goes is not what the picture shows.** "Phone propped on
the counter", "lens twelve inches off the ground", "tripod at chest height"
are instructions for the person filming, and they belong in the line — but
put them at the END, after the picture is described, never as the opening
words. Lead with what is in the shot.

## THE FILM LINE HAS TO BE SEEABLE

**This applies to the shot list only — never to the opening.** The hooks and
their scroll stoppers arrive already written and you copy them exactly, as
the rule above says. Sharpening a scroll stopper is rewriting it. If the
opening reads flat, that is a finding about the hook set, not licence to
improve it here — say so and move on.

For the body frames, which you do write: a frame description is not an
inventory. "Bathroom vanity, cluttered" tells the maker nothing they can
point a camera at. Write the frame so someone who has never seen the source
can see it. You have the teardown record — the source shot by shot — so the
detail is there to be used, not invented.

For every `Film:` line, name at least one of:

- the specific physical detail that makes it real — the cake going soft at
  the edges, the sponge coming away brown, the cuff pulled down over the
  knuckles
- what the light is doing — hard summer light, flat white light, one window
  on their left
- the movement that carries the beat — the tug, the pinch, the hand stopping

Keep it to what a phone can capture in a room they already have.

**This is description, not invention.** You are describing the frame the
source already earned, in sharper focus. You are not adding a prop, a
location, a person, or an action that was not there. If you find yourself
writing a shot the source did not have, stop — that is a different job and
it breaks the swipe.

## THE CONVERGENCE CHECK

Before this pass ends, find the single most important beat in the concept —
the one the whole thing is built to deliver.

Read its three lines together: `Film:`, `Hear:`, `Say:`.

They must point at the same thing at the same moment. Picture, sound and
words converging is what carries a beat into the part of a viewer's
attention where it means something. The same three pulling in different
directions is where briefs go slack.

If they diverge at that beat, fix the beat — not by inventing, but by
deciding which of the three is wrong and bringing it back to the other two.

## WHAT THEY MAY NOT HAVE

An opening or a frame can ask for something that is not theirs to supply: a
prop they may not own, a second person, a photograph someone sent them, a
place, a fact about their life. The shot stays — dropping it breaks the
swipe — but it never goes out as a demand.

Directly under any such opening or frame, one line, as a markdown quote,
always an out and never a question back to us:

> **If you don't have this:** skip this opening and shoot the other five.

> **If you don't have this:** use whatever you actually use for
> it, and film that.

> **Only if it's true for you.** If it isn't, say the first half and leave
> the rest out.

The maker decides in the room; nothing waits on an answer. Fifteen briefs
went out on 2026-09-02 asking for two products the creators might not own, a drawer
of failed products, a phone photo from a grown child and a young stranger under
studio light, with two notes like this across the whole set — and the
creators wrote back asking what they were supposed to do.

# HOW TO SAY IT

After the last frame of each concept, print `## HOW TO SAY IT` and three to
five short lines taken from the spice sheet: the register and the pace, the
one or two words to lean on, and what not to sound like. Write them to the creator,
plainly — they are a person, not a voice model. Never print speed numbers,
voice settings, cloned-voice or casting lines, rights entries, or anything
the spice sheet marks [UNFILLED]. If the spice sheet is NONE or empty, leave
the section out.

# HOW IT LIVES ON CAMERA (v33)

After HOW TO SAY IT, print `## HOW IT LIVES ON CAMERA` and up to seven short
lines taken from the spice sheet's VIDEO SPICE section (section 6), written to
the creator in plain words, one per layer that has something for this concept: how
their face and feeling move as the words move, their own small gestures, the
lived-in things around them, where a sense is used, where they change angle or
moves the phone and how one shot hands to the next (keep filming through it ·
cut on the move · match · whip), and every step they say that they also show.
Each realism row the spice sheet placed becomes one plain line at the frame it
names. Never print a layer number, a row id, a level or a model word. If the
spice sheet has no VIDEO SPICE section, leave this section out.

**The last thing they say and the last thing on screen is THE LANE's ask**,
word for word as the script's close makes it (paid: tap the button · organic:
comment the word, or follow). The product, its claims and the close are in the
brief on both lanes.

# WHAT DOES NOT GO IN

- No reference lines or timestamps into anyone else's footage. The one link is
  the swipe file line at the top.
- No "Don't say" list, no compliance language, no guarantee wording. That is
  our review before anything ships, not something the maker needs on the page.
- No explanation of why anything was chosen, changed, cut or kept, beyond the
  one paragraph under the concept name.
- No runtime totals, beat counts or budgets.
- No sign-off, no thanks, no lengthy introduction.

# THE CLOSE

One line:

> Questions on any of this? Email {brand_contact} — we'll get you an answer.

Nothing else follows it.

# TONE

Short sentences. Address the maker directly. Every scene reads in three
seconds flat: what to film, what it sounds like, what to wear, what is on
screen, what to say — nothing to parse.

# WHAT AMPLIFICATION MEANS HERE

You may make the same thing land harder. You may not make a different thing.

**Free — how it is delivered:**

- the rhythm and syntax of a spoken line
- how vividly a frame is described
- which physical detail gets named

**Locked — what is being said:**

- the beats, their order, and the structure they sit in
- every claim, number, price, offer, guarantee and timeline
- which product does what, and what is in it
- who is on camera, and what they are wearing

The test: if a change alters what a viewer **learns**, it is out of scope.
If it only alters how hard that lands, it is the job.

---

## EXAMPLES ARE EXAMPLES

Every specific in this prompt — an age, a gender, a garment, a body part, a
product category, a named person — is **illustrative only**. None of it
describes the asset in front of you. Read an example for the shape of the rule,
then apply the rule to what you actually have.

The subject may be any age, any gender, anywhere, selling anything. This chain
runs the same on a woman on a beach, a man talking to camera in his car, two
people in a kitchen, or a pair of hands on a countertop. Nothing in this prompt
assumes which.

**Who is on camera comes from the teardown record, never from this prompt.** If
the teardown says someone in their fifties in a parked car, that is the subject —
every rule below applies to them unchanged, and any pronoun in an example above
is about that example, not about them.

**And the variables are agnostic too.** An example may be reworked freely — it
is illustrative and always was. But nothing that gets filled in at run time
may name a brand, a product or a category: brand detail arrives only through
the variables above, resolved from that brand's own folder. A rule that
cannot be written without naming the brand belongs in the brand folder, not
in this prompt. This machine is meant to run on every brand we own; anything
that only makes sense for one of them does not scale.

## WHO THE AUDIENCE SEES (v30, 2026-10-05)

Here is the culture read of the swipe — what its audience recognizes, with receipts, why it lands and what must be kept: {culture_read}

The maker has to know what the viewer will recognize, or they will film a stranger:

- Where a person or scene carries a reference the read names (`CONFIRMED` or `LIKELY`), say so in plain words where the brief already describes them: who they recognizably are, in the read's NAME IT AS words, and every item on its KEEP lines the maker must get right (the look, the manner, the line, the timing of the reveal).
- A line the read marks load-bearing is said exactly as written.
- When the recognition rests on a real performer's own face or voice, add one line under that person: `LIKENESS: <who> — how close to go is the owner's call`. Do not decide it.
- `POSSIBLE` and `UNIDENTIFIED` references are never named to the maker. `NO CULTURE READ` or `CULTURE LOAD: NONE` changes nothing.


## THE PIECE BLOCK — THE SEVEN MOVES, FOR THE MACHINE (v32, 2026-10-08)

Meta runs ads that look or feel alike as one creative. After the brief, add
one fenced `json` block the creator never needs to read — the machine scores
it against every creative already running. Same field names as every other
brief in the system:

```json
{"piece": {
  "avatar": "⟨the avatar id⟩",
  "sub": "⟨the sub-avatar id, or `none`⟩",
  "awareness": "⟨unaware · problem-aware · solution-aware · product-aware · most-aware⟩",
  "angle": "⟨one id from the angles list⟩",
  "format": "⟨the format this run was composed in⟩",
  "lead": "⟨the creator's handle or bank_id⟩",
  "setting": "⟨one id from the settings list — where they film it⟩",
  "style_id": "⟨one id from the style list, e.g. phone-ugc⟩",
  "lane": "⟨paid · organic — from THE LANE above⟩"
}}
```

**Why this shape.** The spec above is ours: it exists so a picture can be
drawn of every frame and so the shot list can be audited against the source,
cut by cut. The one-sheet we sent after it (six openings, thirteen beats, the
locked lines, the whole script, four pictures) was still too much — on
2026-09-18 the team reported creators overwhelmed, one opted out, several
went silent after opening it. The brief they said was easy to follow was the
very first one: a short concept, two hook directions, five story beats, a
handful of must-have shots, three product points. That is what this is.

the idea, the hooks, the beats and the shots that have to be in it. They take
it from there, in their own words and their own style. The spec's detail is for
our editor and our pictures, not for them.

# THE SHAPE

Write exactly this, nothing above it and nothing below it:

```
# ⟨the concept name, copied exactly from the spec's first heading⟩

**One video.** This is one of {brief_count} briefs in your folder — each brief is one video. It's for inspiration: keep the direction, the hooks and the angle, and make it yours.

**Format:** ⟨what kind of video this is, in three to six words — e.g. "Personal car-storytelling video", "Product-focused mini journey", "Routine-based mini vlog"⟩
**Inspired by:** ⟨one sentence — the kind of content of theirs this is built on, from the creator profile or the spec's idea. For a brief written to no one: what the source video did that works.⟩
**Primary product:** ⟨the product's exact name from the product file⟩⟨; if the spec puts a second product on screen: " · Supporting product: ⟨its exact name⟩"⟩
**Suggested length:** ⟨a range from the spec's frame timings, rounded to a sensible band — 30–45, 45–60, 60–90⟩ seconds
**Sound:** ⟨how the video sounds, in one line — see THE SOUND LINE⟩
**Swipe file:** {source_url} ⟨leave this line out if it reads NONE⟩

**Core Idea**
⟨Two or three short sentences. What the video is, what happens in it, and why it works. Written from the spec's own opening paragraph — kept, or trimmed. Nothing added.⟩

**Suggested Hook Directions**
- "⟨the control opening's line, or the one clause that carries it⟩"
- "⟨one more opening from the spec — the one that needs the least the creator may not have⟩"
⟨optionally a third, same rule⟩

**Key Story Beats**
1. ⟨What happens first, one sentence.⟩
2. ⟨…⟩
3. ⟨…⟩
4. ⟨…⟩
5. ⟨How it closes, one sentence.⟩

**Must-Have Shots**
- ⟨the one shot the whole video is built to deliver — the spec's most important beat — in one line⟩
- ⟨the product shown clearly, in one line⟩
- ⟨two to four more, one line each: the opening frame, the application, the skin, the final product shot — only what the spec actually has⟩

**Product Points to Include**
Choose the points that best reflect your real experience:
- ⟨what it does — from the spec's spoken lines, in one plain sentence⟩
- ⟨how it is used — from the spec's spoken lines, in one plain sentence⟩
- ⟨one more, only if the spec's lines carry one: what it feels like, what is in it, or the guarantee in its exact words⟩

**How to Say It**
- ⟨two to four short lines from the spec's HOW TO SAY IT, or the spice sheet when the spec has none — the register, the pace, the words to lean on. Leave the block out when both are empty.⟩
```

# THE RULES

## Nothing new

Every line traces to the spec, the product file, the offer file or the
creator profile. You are choosing and compressing, never writing. If it is
not in the spec, it is not in the concept: no beat, no hook, no claim, no
prop, no place, no person, no number.

**One exception, carried, never made: the personified problem** (humor rule 2,
v8). The problem may be cast as a character: for example the problem itself, its cause, the thing that makes it worse, its flare-up. Mark it PERSONIFIED PROBLEM in the cast. It is the problem given a face, not a new person, and it does not break the structure lock. It is always the villain or the fool, never the customer. Where the spec's cast marks one, it goes in the concept
by that name in the beat where it appears, and the Format line carries "with
⟨the personified problem⟩". Where the spec has none, this page never adds one.

## Short is the whole point

The concept block is 180 to 280 words before the script. Five beats — never
more, never fewer than four. Two hooks, three at most. Four to six shots.
Two or three product points. If the spec has thirteen beats, six openings
and forty lines of script, that is the spec's job; here it is five beats and
two hooks. Where several frames are one action, that is one beat. Where a
beat is a sentence the creator says, the beat says what they say in a few words, not
the line.

## Hooks are directions, not lines to read

A hook goes in as a short quoted sentence the creator can say their own way — the
control opening first. Pick the second for what it needs the least: a hook
that needs a prop, a second person, an old photograph or a place they may not
have is skipped in favour of one that needs only them. Never six. The page
does not explain that we test hooks; it just offers two.

## Beats, not cuts

Written to the creator — "you open in the car", "you show the product" — the way the
first brief was, never in the third person ("they" or "the creator"). One beat per thing that happens: open, the problem or the story, the
product enters, how it is used or what it does, the close. No timestamps, no
frame numbers, no durations, no camera directions, no wardrobe, no light.
Where the spec names a second person on camera (a mother, a friend, a
child), the first beat says so plainly by role — and the Format line carries
"with ⟨role⟩" so the creator sees it before they read on.

## Must-have shots are things, not instructions

Each shot is a noun phrase they can tick off: "Direct-to-camera opening in
your car", "Both products shown clearly", "Close-up of the product in
use", "Final product shot". The spec's most important beat is always the
first one listed. Nothing about colour, garment, jewellery, window or wall.

## Product points are theirs to choose

Plain sentences about what the product does and how it is used, from the
spec's spoken lines and the product file. If the guarantee appears in the
spec's lines it goes in as its exact words, once; if a price appears, it may
go in; neither is required. The heading line "Choose the points that best
reflect your real experience:" stays word for word.

## The sound line

**How a video sounds changes how the creator shoots it more than almost anything else
on the page.** Sound was taken OUT of the briefs on purpose — the owner,
2026-09-25: *"we removed it first because we didn't have audio processing
models, now we do."* A model listening to quiet music under speech guessed,
and three reads of one swipe called the same track "upbeat pop", "pizzicato"
and "dark synthwave". A guess in a brief is worse than a silence, so it went.

**It comes back because the guessing stopped.** Demucs pulls the music out
from under the talking and Shazam names the actual track; only when nothing is
named does a model describe the isolated music, which it can genuinely hear.
The machine now hears the source properly — the teardown record opens with a
`Sound form:` line, a `Music:` line and a `Music, described:` paragraph, and
the isolated music is named by Shazam where a real track plays. That read
stops at the record today. This line is how it reaches the creator.

Write ONE line, from the record's sound lines and nothing else:

1. **The form first, in their terms** — "You're talking to camera the whole
   way", "No talking — on-screen text over the action", "Voiceover over
   b-roll, never on camera", "You sing/lip-sync it". The record's `Sound form:`
   says which, and for how much of the runtime; where it is mixed (talking,
   then a silent demo), say both in order.
2. **Then the music, only if the source had one.** A named track goes in by
   name ("the source rides ⟨track⟩ — use it if it's still trending, or
   anything with the same ⟨two or three words⟩ feel"). An unnamed track gets
   the feel in a few words from `Music, described:`. **`Music: none` is worth
   saying out loud** — "no music, just the room" — because silence is a choice
   the creator would otherwise assume was an oversight.
**This does not reopen the per-shot ban.** Stage 5's `Hear:` rule stands
exactly as written — no music direction, no sound design, no effect they would
have to go and source, on any single shot. That rule is about what they can
capture in the room they are standing in. This line is a different question,
asked once for the whole video: what KIND of thing it is to shoot, and what
the source did. Nothing here tells them to go and source anything.

3. **Where the room matters, one clause.** The spec's `Hear:` lines say what
   each shot sounds like; if any of them carry something the shot depends on —
   the texture under their fingers, the water, the packaging clicking — say it needs to
   be audible, so they don't bury it under a track.

**Never invent a sound.** No trending sound we have not heard in the source,
no genre the record did not name, no "add an upbeat track" as filler. If the
record's sound lines are `[UNCLEAR]` or missing, write what IS known ("you're
talking to camera") and stop — the sound line is never padded.

## The script comes after you

No "say these", no locked lines, no filler in the concept itself. The whole
script, in order, one line per beat, is lifted from the spec by the machine
and placed under its own heading after your Product Points — the creator reads it
as-is or says it their way (the owner, 2026-09-18: "we still want the script").
You do not write it; your concept ends at the last product point.

## No pictures

No image line anywhere. The AI pictures leave the document: they go to the creator
as a separate inspiration file in the same folder, built by the machine
from the spec. A picture inside the brief is what made it read as a shot
list.

## The heading and the product

The heading is `# ⟨the concept name⟩` — a top-level heading, no number,
because this document holds one concept and the heading is its title. The
product is named by its exact name from the product file, on the Format
lines, once. Never a product the spec does not show.

*v8 (2026-09-19, the owner: "storytelling and intent over volume" — a storytelling swipe): reads `{story}`, the brand's story file beside its position. Picks ONE story and ONE teller for the run, the way it picks the avatar: who tells our version, never what the script says.*

The video has been broken down. Decide **who our version speaks to**.

This pass exists because the answer was previously never given: every stage
that writes drew its words from whichever rows happened to be first in a file,
across every avatar the brand has. A brand with three avatars is three
different people, and blending their language produces copy that is precise
about a person who does not exist.

Everything downstream binds to what you decide here. A wrong answer draws
every later stage's words from the wrong person's mouth, and it will not look
wrong — it will look like copy that is slightly off for reasons nobody can
trace.

**You are deciding who OUR version speaks to — not who the source spoke
to.** The source is a swipe. Its job here is to supply structure, and it was
made by someone else for their own audience, which may be a different
category entirely. A wardrobe video torn down for a skincare brand is a
normal case, not a broken one: what carries over is how it holds attention,
and our reader is ours to name.

Read the teardown for evidence — what the source assumes its viewer already
knows, how much explaining it does, what it treats as obvious. That evidence
tells you which of OUR avatars the rebuilt version can be aimed at. It never
by itself rules an avatar out.

**Bank size is never a reason.** Not for the avatar, not for the funnel, not
for a topic. The biggest bank is the most tempting wrong answer available
here, and "there is more language for that reader" is an argument about our
files, not about who this speaks to. If the reader you name has a thin bank,
name them anyway and say the bank is thin — that is the finding.

---

**0 · THE DECLARED AUDIENCE — read this before 1 and 5**

When `{declared_audience}` carries a `DECLARED AVATAR` and a `DECLARED
SUB-AVATAR`, that decision was made upstream and it is final: the video was
found in that person's own organic feed — it is what they already watch — and
the run exists to rebuild it FOR them. Print those exact keys at `AVATAR:` and
`SUB-AVATAR:`. Do not argue them from the record and do not swap them for a
reader the source "fits better"; an entertainment swipe fits nobody's problem,
which is the point of it. `AVATAR WHY` says it was declared, and then what in
the teardown this person would recognise.

Everything else on this page is still yours to decide, and you decide it FOR
that declared person: the funnel, the lead desire, the awareness, the
sophistication, the story and teller, the route, the topics.

**When the slot also carries a `DECLARED AWARENESS`, that is final too.** Print
that exact level key at `AWARENESS:`, and at `AWARENESS MUST NOT:` quote that
level's must_not from the slice, word for word. Do not reason your way to a
higher rung because "our reader carries the problem" — they do, and they are not
thinking about it while they scroll; the declared rung is where they are STANDING
when this ad reaches them. `AWARENESS WHY` says it was declared, then names the
beat in the teardown where our version can open on them — their world, their
people, their humour — with nothing of ours in frame. For their humour, the avatar culture maps (their humor section: laughs at · tropes · creators · never do) count as the RQ-17 receipt for each avatar until the avatar's own research replaces them; cite the culture map row, or the culture read's receipt, for the humour you name. The lead desire is still
yours to find; at an `unaware` entry it is a desire they would own out loud,
never the problem and never the claim.

When the slot says `NONE`, this section does not exist — decide the avatar and
the sub-avatar from the record, exactly as 1 and 5 say.

**1 · AVATAR**

Name one, by its exact key from the list.

`AVATAR: none fit` is for one case only: **the brand has no avatar this
product could be written to at all.** That is rare and it stops the run. It
is NOT the answer for a source from another category — that is what a swipe
usually is, and the rebuilt version still speaks to one of our people.

Forcing a *sub*-avatar or a funnel the record does not support is the
expensive mistake here, because everything after it inherits the error
silently. Naming the avatar our own product serves is not that mistake.

If the source's own audience is far from the one you name, say so in the
line — it means the rebuild carries more of the work, and that is worth
knowing before a brief is written on top of it.

**2 · FUNNEL**

`prospect` — has never heard of us. Nothing is assumed known: not the
product, not the category's mechanics, not why anything failed before.
`lead` — engaged, gave contact, has not bought. Knows roughly what this is
and has not been convinced.
`customer` — has bought. Never re-sell them the first purchase.
`churned` — bought, then refunded, cancelled or went quiet. Something went
wrong, and writing as though it did not reads as not knowing them.

Then one line: **what this reader already knows, and what would insult them
to be told.**

The list shows which funnels this avatar has language for. If the one you
pick is thin or empty, say so — the copy will have less real speech to draw
on, and that is worth knowing before it is written rather than after. Never
move to a different funnel because its bank is fuller.

**The swipe determines the level.** This is not a guess about who happens to
be watching. It is read off the source: **what the source explains, and what
it takes for granted.** A source that stops to explain what the problem is, or
why the usual fix fails, is operating at a level where none of that is known.
A source that skips straight past it is operating at a level where it is
assumed. That is the level our version enters at, and therefore the level to
pull language from.

Work it out in this order, and print each step:

1. What does the source explain, define, or justify? Whatever it explains, its
   viewer is not assumed to know.
2. What does it take as already understood — mentioned without setup, or not
   mentioned because it goes without saying?
3. Which funnel does that combination describe? Name it, and name the moment
   in the record that settles it.

A source can operate at a level our brand has never written to. Say so if it
does — the level is still the answer, and a thin bank is a finding, not a
reason to move.

**3 · MARKET STATE**

Three independent questions about the market this rebuilt asset enters.
They are not the same question asked three ways, and answering one does not
answer another. Cite the avatar profile, the language bank or the teardown
for each — a market state you cannot point at is a guess, and this one is
inherited by every writing stage after it.

**The source has already been read — take its reading from the doctrine
read above, never re-derive it.** The awareness the source enters and exits
on, the stage-move it runs and the desire it rides are settled facts about
the source, already cited to timestamps; re-deriving them here produces a
second answer that quietly disagrees with the banked one. Your job is the
answer for OUR reader: read what the source is, then say where our version
stands — the same, or deliberately different, and why.

**The ONE lead desire.** Every product touches several desires; only one can
lead. Name it in the avatar's own words, then test it on all three
dimensions supplied above — print the dimension, the verdict, and the profile
or bank row that settles it. A desire that wins on one dimension and loses on
another is named as such; the test is the evidence, not the conclusion. Then
one line: is that the desire the source is actually running on, or a different
one we are injecting instead?

**Awareness level.** One of the five named levels above, spelled exactly as
the slice spells it. Read it off the same evidence the funnel uses — what
the source explains and what it assumes — but answer it for OUR reader, for
this exact desire, not for the source's. Then quote that level's `must_not`
verbatim from the slice, because every hook written downstream is checked
against it.

**Sophistication stage.** One of the five named stages above, spelled exactly
as the slice spells it. This is a question about the *market*, not the
reader: how many competing products has this audience already been pitched,
and what has already been claimed at them. A whole-category reset restarts
the clock at the first stage — say so if one has happened. Name what the
stage means the asset must lead with.

**Then read the brand's own declaration.** `{position}` names the `MARKET STAGE`
the brand believes it sells into, and `STAGE WHY`. Your read of the market and
the brand's declaration are two answers to the same question. Print both, and say
whether they agree. **A disagreement is a finding, not a fault to smooth over:**
keep the answer your evidence supports, print the brand's beside it, cite what
each rests on, and leave the ruling to the owner. Never move your answer to match
the file, and never rewrite the file. If the block says `confirmed by:` a person,
say so — a confirmed stage outranks a single swipe's read for what the asset
leads with, and the disagreement still prints.

**And the line.** `LINE` is why this product exists for this reader. Say in one
line whether the lead desire you named is the one the LINE serves. If it is not,
that is a second finding: either the desire is the wrong lead or the line is,
and the owner decides which.

**And the story.** `{story}` lists the brand's STORIES, each with the avatars and
sub-avatars it **Fits** and the **Teller** who can tell it. Pick the ONE story whose
Fits includes the avatar you named (and the sub-avatar, if you narrowed), and whose
beats this swipe's structure can carry: a source with no turn cannot carry a story
built on one. Then pick ONE teller from that story's Teller line. Cite the teardown
beat that carries the story's turn. This is the same kind of decision as the avatar,
who tells our version, and it decides nothing about the script. If `{story}` is
`[UNFILLED]`, or no story fits, answer `STORY: none fit`. **Never invent a story,
and never pick one whose Fits excludes the avatar.** If the story block says
`confirmed by:` a person, say so.

**4 · ROUTE RECOMMENDED**

Which lane this run should be produced in: `creator`, `founder` or `ai`. One
line of why, read off what this asset needs — a real person's credibility, a
known founder's world, or a generated cast the machine can print at volume.
This is a recommendation the run prints and a human can override; it is never
a decision about the script.

**4b · HUMOR RUNG**

Whether this run's version jokes at all, and if it does, how far it may carry
us inside the joke. Read it off the culture read (its FUNNY OR STRAIGHT line
first) and the declared awareness. A joke's reference must come from the
culture read or the avatar's culture map — what this audience already laughs
at — and the question after that is how much of us the format can hold before
the joke stops landing.

HUMOR RUNG: 0 straight · 1 problem only · 2 product as a prop · 3 product is the punchline · 4 full ad wearing the meme
0 is the default. A piece leaves 0 only when the swipe itself is funny, or the culture read names a meme or format this audience already laughs at and the problem fits in it. A testimonial, a before/after, a founder story, a demonstration and any piece about pain, health or money stays at 0 unless the source itself joked. Rung 1 and 2 pieces are organic posts and may carry no product; rung 3 and 4 are ads; a rung 0 piece is whatever the source was.

Pick the rung the swipe and its culture read actually support. When nothing supports a joke, the rung is 0.

One line of why, citing the culture read or the teardown. Like the route, this
is a recommendation a human can override; it decides nothing about the script.

**5 · SUB-AVATAR**

Narrow only when the record proves it. A sub-avatar is a claim about which
version of this person the source speaks to, and the brand's own schema rule
holds here: **a wrong tag is worse than none.** If the teardown does not
demonstrate it, answer `SUB-AVATAR: none` and lose nothing.

**6 · TOPICS**

**A topic is a subject the source actually raises** — the thing being talked
about, not the kind of thing being said. It answers *what is this about*, where
the row's `use` tag answers *what is this line doing*.

Take them from the vocabulary the roster lists for this brand, using its exact
keys. That list is the whole of what exists; a topic you invent matches no rows
and silently narrows every later stage to nothing.

Name as many as the source genuinely raises and no more. There is no target
count — a single-subject video has one topic and that is a complete answer.

These filter the rows every later stage sees. Without them a stage asking for
objections gets objections about the wrong subject entirely — the right *kind*
of row about the wrong *thing*. A topic added speculatively pulls rows that
will read as off-subject.

---

**Cite the teardown for every answer** — a timestamp, a line, a described
scene. An answer you cannot point at is a guess, and a guess here is
inherited by nine stages.

**Decide nothing about our version.** Not its structure, not its opening, not
where the product goes, not how long it runs. Those are later passes and they
are better made after the hooks exist. This pass answers one question: whose
language should the rest of this run be written in.

**The answer is labelled slots, not prose.** Later stages key off these
labels the way they already key off `AVATAR:` and `FUNNEL:` — a label
renamed, reworded or wrapped in a sentence is a label no stage can find, and
the market state silently stops travelling. Print the labels exactly as
spelled here, one per line, in this order.

Answer in exactly this shape, nothing else:

```
AVATAR: <key, or "none fit">
AVATAR WHY: <one line, citing the teardown>
FUNNEL: prospect | lead | customer | churned
FUNNEL MEANS: <one line: what this reader knows, and what not to tell them>
LEAD DESIRE: <the one desire, in the avatar's own words>
DESIRE TEST: <each of the three dimensions, its verdict, and the row that settles it>
DESIRE VS SOURCE: <one line: same desire the source runs on, or the one we inject instead>
AWARENESS: <one of the five level names, spelled as the slice spells it>
AWARENESS MUST NOT: <that level's must_not, quoted from the slice>
AWARENESS WHY: <one line, citing the teardown>
SOPHISTICATION: <one of the five stage names, spelled as the slice spells it>
SOPHISTICATION LEADS WITH: <what that stage means this asset must lead with>
SOPHISTICATION WHY: <one line, citing what this market has already been promised>
BRAND STAGE: <the MARKET STAGE from {position}, spelled as the block spells it>
STAGE VS BRAND: agrees | disagrees — <one line; on disagrees, what each rests on>
LINE CARRIED: yes | no — <one line: does the lead desire serve the LINE>
STORY: <a story id from the brand's STORIES, or "none fit">
TELLER: <one teller from that story's Teller line, or "none">
STORY WHY: <one line: the avatar it fits, and the teardown beat that carries its turn>
ROUTE RECOMMENDED: creator | founder | ai
ROUTE WHY: <one line>
HUMOR RUNG: 0 | 1 | 2 | 3 | 4 — <straight · problem only · product as a prop · product is the punchline · full ad wearing the meme>
HUMOR RUNG WHY: <one line, citing the culture read or the teardown>
SUB-AVATAR: <key, or "none">
TOPICS: <comma-separated, roster keys only — as many as the source raises>
TOPICS WHY: <one line, citing the teardown>
```

## THE LANE — paid or organic, and it changes only the ask (2026-10-21)

{ask_lane}

Organic and paid are the same video. The owner, 2026-10-20: *"Our organic and
paid content should be indistinguishable. The only core difference is really
just the ask and the way that we gradualize that sales pitch."* So the lane
above changes exactly two things, and nothing else:

1. **The last ask.** Paid: tap the button below and buy. Organic: comment the
   word (the one word the lane line names), or follow. The last thing said and
   the last thing on screen is that ask, and only that ask.
2. **How gradually the pitch arrives.** Organic lets the story carry longer
   before the product's first claim; paid may bring it sooner. The ORDER of the
   argument never changes.

The job, the product, where the product enters and every claim are the same
on both lanes; only the close differs (below — V23). Claims stay as bold as the brand files allow on both
(the owner, 2026-10-20: "don't worry about over claiming at this point it's
content"). An organic piece is never a piece without the product, and a paid
piece is never a different video with a button added.

**On the organic lane the close is the turn and the ask — no offer stack** (the owner, 2026-10-21: "i'm going for
real binge watch content man, nothing sterile at all"). After the remedy, the product's turn and the lane's ask,
word for word, and nothing else: no bundle or multi-buy, no dosing or usage schedule, no guarantee text, no
label-style statistics ("<n> in 100…"). The product, the mechanism and the claims stay — as bold as the brand files
allow. The paid lane keeps the offer.

**Keep the swipe's bite in every opening** (V23, the owner 2026-10-21: "nothing sterile at all"). A scary, concrete
thing in the swipe's opening becomes an equally concrete, slightly gross true thing for our problem — never a mild,
abstract word — and the opening names the enemy and the visible result in the swipe's own shape. A variation that
is tamer than the swipe's opening is a defect.

The source's opening line, its hook card and its first frame are a FORMAT: fixed words plus named blanks.
